## Page 1


LATTICE HOMOLOGY OF INTEGRALLY CLOSED SUBMODULES AND ARTIN
ALGEBRAS
ANDRÁS NÉMETHI AND GERG ˝O SCHEFLER
ABSTRACT. The general construction of lattice (co)homology assigns to a lattice Zr and a weight func-
tion w : Zr →Z a bigraded Z[U]-module H∗. The weight function w is often obtained from some geomet-
ric data as the difference of two ‘height functions’. In this paper we consider the case when these height
functions are Hilbert functions of valuative multifiltrations on a Noetherian k-algebra O and a finitely
generated O-module M. We introduce the notion of ‘realizable submodules’ in M, the prime example
of which are finite codimensional integrally closed submodules in the sense of Rees (or integrally closed
ideals when M = O). We prove, that whenever two sets of ‘extended’ discrete valuations ‘realize’ the
same submodule N ≤M, then, although the corresponding lattices and weight functions might be differ-
ent, the resulting lattice homology modules are isomorphic and have Euler characteristic dimk(M/N). In
this way, we associate a well-defined lattice homology to any quotient of type M/N, where N is a realiz-
able submodule of M. We also present some structural and computational results: e.g., we geometrically
characterize the (lattice) homological dimension of integrally closed monomial ideals of k[x,y].
The main upshot of the paper, however, is the possibility of categorifying numerical invariants defined
as codimensions of realizable submodules or integrally closed ideals. The geometric applications include:
the delta invariant δ(C,o) of a reduced curve singularity; the geometric genus pg(X,o), the irregularity
q(X,o) and the various plurigenera of higher dimensional isolated normal singularities. The corres-
ponding categorifications generalize the analytic lattice homologies of Ágoston and the first author.
We also discuss relations with singularity deformation theory: we initiate the study of ‘conductor
ideal constant’ Gorenstein deformations, which automatically preserve the analytic lattice homology.
The manuscript presents computations for several key families of algebras and singularities, including,
e.g., Newton nondegenerate singularities and deformations which does not preserve the lattice homology.

1. INTRODUCTION
1.1. Lattice homology constructions.
The theory of lattice (co)homology is a connecting bridge between several mathematical fields.
In its current form, it encompasses a family of closely related invariants of isolated singularities of
complex analytic varieties, making connections to analytic invariants, commutative algebra, and, most
prominently, to low-dimensional topology, where it also gained independent development. In fact, one
can think of (the analytic) lattice homology as a complex analytic analogue of Heegaard Floer theory.
The original topological version of lattice homology [85, 87] is defined combinatorially from the
dual resolution graph of a normal surface singularity, hence, it is strongly related to the low-dimensional
topology of the link. It is, in fact, equivalent to its Heegaard Floer homology (see [85, 98] for some
special cases, and [132] for the most general statement), which is, a priori, much more difficult to
compute. Moreover, from the analytic viewpoint, this topological lattice homology has the ability
to bound (or, in certain cases, compute) analytic invariants as well, making it a useful tool for the
continuation and generalization of the Artin–Laufer program.
Parallel to the topological version, the analytic lattice homology [1, 2, 4, 87] is also defined, which
reveals even deeper information about the analytic type: it can help to distinguish different analytic
structures supported on a fixed topological type and categorifies the geometric genus. It comes equipped
with a natural morphism from the topological version.
The analytic lattice homology of reduced curve singularities was also recently introduced by Ágos-
ton and the first author in [5]. This is a categorification of the delta invariant, moreover, it conjecturally
has the following ‘functoriality’ property: flat singularity deformations induce nontrivial graded mor-
phisms between the analytic lattice homology modules. Its low-dimensional topology counterparts
were defined by Ozsváth, Stipsicz and Szabó for knots [99], and Borodzik, Liu and Zemke for links
[14]. They are all strongly connected with the Heegaard Floer Knot / Link homology (see, e.g., [89],
where a connecting spectral sequence was presented).
The goal of this manuscript is to show that the domain and scope of lattice homology can be broadly
extended, it can be used in several different situations, providing new, computable and interesting in-
variants. We present a simple algebraic language which unifies all the existing analytic lattice homology
theories and generalizes them to a much more abstract setting. This viewpoint enables us to rely on
the very same, so-called Independence Theorem, when proving well-definedness and structural results,
using combinatorial techniques instead of the original case-by-case analytic arguments.
Moreover, our methods extend to the purely commutative algebraic setting of finite codimensional
integrally closed ideals of k-algebras (or, more generally, integrally closed submodules in a finitely gen-
erated module), obtaining a well-defined categorification of their codimension. This level of generality
holds much promise both in singularity theory and in the more general realm of commutative algebra
as well. Our results open the gates to multiple research directions and raise interesting questions, we
present a large list of these at the end of the manuscript.
Below we present a map of existing lattice homology theories and their connections to low dimen-
sional topology in order to provide better context for our new construction introduced in this article.

FIGURE 1. A map of lattice homology (LH) theories and the present construction
1.2. The lattice homology module associated with a lattice and a weight function.
The lattice homology construction starts with a lattice Zr and a weight function w : Zr →Z, which
has some finiteness properties and is bounded from below. In fact, this weight function is the main
geometric ingredient, the one which differentiates the distinct theories, and ideally carries important
information about the studied object. For example, in the topological lattice homology of normal
surface singularities the lattice is freely generated by the irreducible components of the exceptional
divisor of a good resolution, whereas the weight function is given by the Riemann–Roch expression
on the lattice points considered as homological cycles [85]. In the analytic case, the weight function
(defined in the first quadrant of the same lattice) is given by the difference of the Hilbert function
corresponding to the divisorial valuations and the rank of the first sheaf cohomology of the structure
sheaves of these divisors [1, 2]. The level sets and critical points of these weight functions determine
a fundamentally new type of geometry of these singularities, which lies at the heart of the definition of
lattice homology.
The construction itself is based on the following filtration of Zr ⊗R = Rr (equipped with the standard
cubical decomposition), the ‘tower of spaces’ /0 = Smw−1 ⊂Smw ⊂Smw+1 ⊂··· ⊂Sn ⊂··· ⊂Rr, where
Sn is the union of those cubes, which have all vertices with w-weight at most n ∈Z and mw is the
minimal value of the weight function. Then the bigraded lattice homology Z[U]-module is
H∗(Rr,w) = ⊕q≥0Hq(Rr,w) = ⊕q≥0 ⊕n≥mw Hq,−2n(Rr,w), with Hq,−2n = Hq(Sn,Z).
The q-grading is called the homological grading, whereas the n-grading is the weight grading. This lat-
ter is — by definition — even, in order to emphasize the compatibility with the Heegaard Floer theories.
The bigraded U-action is given by the natural inclusion maps Hq(Sn−1,Z) →Hq(Sn,Z). The reduced
lattice homology also appears naturally: Hred,∗(Rr,w) = ⊕qHred,q, where Hred,q = ⊕n eHq(Sn,Z).
In some cases one only considers the cubes contained in a rectangle R(0,d) := {x ∈Rr : 0 ≤x ≤0},
which provides H∗(R(0,d),w). An example with a rank 2 lattice is presented in Figure 2.
The requirements on w ensure that the {Sn}n spaces are finite CW complexes, which are usually
empty for n ≪0 and contractible for n ≫0. Therefore, in such a case Hred,∗has finite Z-rank and
the Euler characteristic is defined as eu(H∗) := −mw +∑q (−1)q rankZ Hred,q. Notice that the smallest
weight mw (the analogue of the d-invariant used in Heegaard Floer homology [100]) and Hred,∗together
determine the whole H∗.

An important observation is that the general construction / definition of lattice homology is very
flexible: by providing different weight functions one obtains different lattice homologies. On the other
hand, the following question arises naturally: when do two different weight functions yield the same
lattice homology module? The main result of this article, the Independence Theorem, gives a sufficient
condition for this in a rather large class of naturally occurring weight functions.
1.3. Categorification with lattice homology.
In mathematical classification procedures one uses invariants to distinguish different objects. If a
certain invariant is not sufficiently ‘strong’, then one tries to endow it with some additional structure.
A categorification of an invariant is a (co)homology theory whose Euler characteristic is the invariant
considered. It is clearly a finer invariant: the Betti numbers and the torsion part provide more subtle
information than the Euler characteristic itself.
In the last decades several famous categorifications were introduced. E.g., in knot theory, the Kho-
vanov invariant was introduced as the categorification of the Jones polynomial [60], the Link Heegaard
Floer homology as the categorification of the Alexander polynomial [103]. Or, in the 3-manifold the-
ory, the Heegaard Floer homology of Ozsváth and Szabó is the categorification of the Seiberg–Witten
invariant [101, 102].
The existing lattice homology theories fit naturally into this trend. The topological lattice homo-
logy Htop,∗associated with links of normal surface singularities is a categorification of the normalized
Seiberg–Witten invariant [86], whereas the analytic version gives a categorification of the geometric
genus [2]. In the reduced curve singularity case, the Euler characteristic of the analytic lattice homology
is the delta invariant [5].
In fact, Ágoston and the first author described a simple recipe to obtain lattice homological categori-
fications. They studied the properties of lattice homology modules associated with special, so called
‘split’ weight functions of the form w(ℓ) = h(ℓ) + h◦(ℓ) −h◦(0), where h is some increasing and h◦
some decreasing function along the lattice. They realized, that if the pair (h,h◦) satisfies certain combi-
natorial properties, then the Euler characteristic of the lattice homology can be computed from h◦(cf.
[2, Theorem 5.2.1] or the Categorification Theorem 3.1.12 here). This theorem provides a powerful
tool for categorifying numerical invariants via lattice homology. In fact, Ágoston and the first author
built their analytic lattice homology theories on this cornerstone observation.


## Page 5


5
In this article we will elaborate on this construction, considering the case when the height functions
h and h◦come as Hilbert functions of some valuative multifiltrations on a k-algebra O and a mod-
ule M over it. In this setting the required combinatorial properties can be forced to hold by a slight
adjustment in the definition. In fact, this is the multiplication by 2 in (1.4.1), which marks a shift in
philosophy: moving from searching height functions with right combinatorial properties to deform-
ing interesting / relevant ones to meet the requirements. The Independence Theorem then claims that
the resulting lattice homology is a well-defined categorification of the k-codimension of a submodule
N ≤M ‘realized’ by the multifiltration. This construction enables us to give new categorifications of
other important analytic invariants of normal surface singularities such as the irregularity or several
plurigenera, as well as to extend the definition of the analytic lattice homology to singularities with
not necessarily QHS3 link (a necessary requirement in the old versions). Even more, we certainly hope
that the generality of the construction will help us produce new invariants in many geometric / algebraic
situations where similar valuative multifiltrations occur naturally. We firmly believe that, through our
results, purely commutative algebraic problems will become accessible, too.
1.4. The Independence Theorem and the lattice homology of realizable submodules.
Consider the following setup: let k be a field, O a Noetherian k-algebra and M a finitely generated O-
module. Given a discrete (semi-)valuation v : O →N∪{∞} on O, we call a map vM : M →Z = Z∪{∞}
its ‘extension’, if it satisfies the following (see Definition 4.1.3):
(a) vM(fm) = v(f)+vM(m)
for all f ∈O, m ∈M, and
(b) vM(m+m′) ≥min{vM(m),vM(m′)}
for all m,m′ ∈M.
If M is not torsion, such extensions always exist, in fact, there are infinitely many (cf. Remark 4.1.5).

get to the ‘join’ realization D ∪D′, and prove that along any single step the homotopy types of the Sn-
spaces are preserved. This last part is achieved by relying on Quillen’s Fiber Theorem [104, Theorem
A], or, alternatively, the technique of quasifibrations by Dold and Thom [35].
Definition 1.4.3 (= Definition 4.4.1). A finite k-codimensional submodule N ≤M is called ‘realizable’
if some finite collection of extended discrete valuations D satisfies N = F M
D (0). In this case D is called
a ‘realization’ of N.
Therefore, the main consequence of the Independence Theorem 1.4.2 is that to any realizable sub-
module we can assign a well-defined lattice homology computable through any arbitrary realization.
Its main properties are as follows:
Theorem 1.4.4 (= Theorem 4.4.9).
(b) The Euler characteristic is well-defined and satisfies eu(H∗(N ,→M)) = dimk(M/N), i.e., the lattice
homology H∗(N ,→M) categorifies the codimension codimk(N ,→M).
(c) The homotopy type of the Sn spaces (hence H∗(N ,→M) itself) only depend on the quotient module
M/N over the algebra O/AnnO(M/N), where AnnO(M/N) denotes the annihilator in O of M/N.
If we choose M = O, then a finite codimensional ideal I ◁O is realizable if and only if I = FD(d)
for some collection D of discrete (semi-)valuations and lattice point d ∈Z|D| (see Corollary 5.1.2). One
can also see, that in this case the weight function is automatically symmetric with respect to the lattice
point 2d (i.e., wD(ℓ) = wD(2d −ℓ) for any ℓ∈Z|D|, cf. Proposition 5.1.7), implying an inherent Z2-
symmetry of all the Sn-spaces. We claim that the homotopy equivalences of the Independence Theorem
respect this Z2-symmetry up to homotopy, hence it is inherited by the lattice homology module as well
(cf. Theorem 5.2.3). Therefore, we denote the ‘symmetric’ lattice homology of realizable ideals by
SH∗(I ◁O) , which is a categorification of the codimension codimk(I ,→O). By the above Theorem
1.4.4, it only depends on the Artin algebra O/I .
These main results are rather surprising at first. Indeed, a realizable submodule N ≤M can have
multiple very different realizations (with different cardinality and different types of valuations — see,
e.g., Examples 5.4.10, 9.6.1 and 9.6.3), yet according to the Independence Theorem 1.4.2, each of these
yields the very same lattice homology. We also emphasize that H∗(N ,→M) is often highly nontrivial
(see, e.g., non-trivial H1 in Example 7.5.10), it recovers deep information about the input algebras and
modules. On a more philosophical note, we could say that this new invariant sensitively captures the
geometry in some broad and vague sense of some ‘universal multiplicative ordering’ of the quotient
O-module M/N, which is respected by every extended discrete valuation.
1.5. Realizable and integrally closed submodules and Artin algebras.
The Independence Theorem 1.4.2 naturally raises the question of which submodules and ideals are
realizable in the sense of Definition 1.4.3. The valuative nature of the concept suggests connections
with Rees’ integrally closed submodules (cf. [107]) and ideals. Indeed, we have the following result:
Theorem 1.5.1 (= Theorem 4.5.4). Let k be a field, O a Noetherian k-algebra, N ≤M an inclusion
of finitely generated O-modules with codimk(N ,→M) < ∞. Then N is integrally closed in M (in the
sense of Rees) if and only if there exists a realization D of N (in the sense of Definition 1.4.3) such that
for every (semi-)valuation vv ∈D the prime ideal pv = {f ∈O : vv(f) = ∞} is minimal. Hence, finite
codimension and integral closedness implies realizability.


## Page 7


7
(See also Corollary 15.1.8 for the equivalent reformulation of the fact that an element m ∈M is integral
over N in the sense of Rees using extensions of (semi-)valuations.)
In the case of finite codimensional ideals the notions of realizability and integral closedness agree
due to the equational criterion of integral closedness (cf. Corollary 5.1.6, see also the valuative criterion
of integral closedness or the existence of Rees valuations in [118]).
On the other hand, part (c) of Theorem 1.4.4 implies that our new lattice homology construction is
an invariant of finite k-dimensional quotients M/N over Artin k-algebras O/AnnO(M/N), where, by
Proposition 4.8.2 (2), this ideal AnnO(M/N) is realizable. Therefore, the lattice homology theory of
realizable submodules concerns the representation theory of ‘integrally reduced’ Artin algebras:
Definition 1.5.2 (= Definition 6.1.1). We call a Noetherian k-algebra A ‘integrally reduced’ if there
exists a presentation A ∼= O/I with O a Noetherian k-algebra and I = I an integrally closed ideal.
We prove that the property of a Noetherian k-algebra A that it is form k[x1,...,xm]/I with I = I is
well-defined, i.e. in any presentation of an integrally reduced algebra with generators and relations, the
ideal of relations is inherently integrally closed (cf. Corollary 6.1.10). In the case when A is additionally
Artinian, then, by Theorem 1.4.4 (c), SH∗(A) := SH∗(I ◁k[x1,...,xm]) is a well-defined and rather
strong invariant categorifying the k-dimension of A (nevertheless, not complete: see Example 6.3.3).
We also prove that an Artin k-algebra is integrally reduced if and only if all the local components of its
direct product decomposition are so (cf. Proposition 6.2.1), and provide Künneth-type relations for the
corresponding symmetric lattice homology modules (cf. Proposition 6.5.1 and Corollary 6.5.5)
1.5.3. Realizability in the local complex analytic case. For O = OX,o, the local algebra of a complex
analytic spacegerm (X,o), M = O p a free module and N ≤M a submodule, Gaffney defined integral
closedness via holomorphic map germs of form ψ : (C,0) →(X,o) (cf. [41, 42]). In fact, his definition
is equivalent to Rees’ and we also show how it induces realizability (cf. Theorem 4.5.7).
Moreover, in this complex analytic setting Lejeune-Jalabert and Teissier characterized integral re-
ducedness of A by the following internal property (cf. [73, 2.1 Théorème]):
if a ∈A and γ(a) = 0 for any homomorphism γ : A →C{t}/(tm), then a = 0.
A similar internal characterization in the general case would be highly desirable.
In conclusion, to any finite codimensional submodule (or ideal), which is integrally closed in Rees’
or Gaffney’s sense, we can associate a well-defined lattice homology with our Independence Theorem.
1.6. Structural results and conceptual meaning.
In order to understand what our new lattice homology construction measures, it is fruitful to un-
derstand its structure better.
First, looking at the homological grading, we see that, by virtue of
the construction (a compact subcomplex of Rr has trivial H≥r), the ‘(lattice) homological degree’
homdim(N ,→M) := max{q : Hq(N ,→M) ̸= 0} is strictly smaller than the cardinality |D| of any
realization D of N ⪇M (cf. Proposition 4.9.4). [If N = M we define homdim(N ,→M) to be −1.] We
observe that in the case of integrally closed ideals the minimum of all such cardinalities might not be
obtained by the set of Rees valuations (cf. Example 9.6.2), i.e., it is a different invariant of the ideal.
Moreover, we conjecture the following:
Conjecture 1.6.1 (= Conjecture 4.9.5). Suppose that I ◁k[x1,x2] is a finite codimensional, integrally
closed ideal. If SH≥1(I ◁k[x1,x2]) = 0, then I can be realized by a single (semi-)valuation.


## Page 8


8
In fact, in the case of monomial ideals M ◁k[x1,x2] the conjecture holds true, even more, we have
the following characterization:
Theorem 1.6.2 (= Theorem 10.3.1). Let M be a finite codimensional integrally closed monomial ideal
in k[x1,x2]. Then we have the following:
homdim(M ◁k[x1,x2]) = min{|D| : D a realization of M }−1
= min{|D| : D a realization with monomial valuations of M }−1.
(For a more geometric picture see Proposition 10.3.6.) The proof relies on finding a special com-
bination of ring elements (using the Newton polytope), the existence of which helps to bound both
the homological dimension and the cardinality of any realization (cf. Proposition 10.1.1). We expect
Theorem 1.6.2 to also be true in higher dimensional polynomial rings, cf. Remark 10.3.21.
Secondly, we also study the weight grading. It turns out that the set {n : (Hred,∗(N ,→M))−2n ̸= 0}
(with O, M and N general as above) is bounded both from above and below (this follows, e.g., from
Theorem 4.4.9 (a)). The lower bound mw = −max{n ∈Z : H0,2n(N ,→M) ̸= 0} (cf. Corollary 4.9.8)
is a challenging new invariant, which, in the special case of the analytic lattice homology of reduced
curve singularities, is strongly related with their Cohen–Macauley type (see [53]). On the other hand,
by a slight generalization of [67, Theorem 6.1.1], the upper bound (under some conditions) is universal:
Theorem 1.6.3 (= Nonpositivity Theorem 4.9.9). If k is algebraically closed and O is local, then the
Sn-spaces are contractible for all n > 0, while, if N ̸= M, then S0 is not even connected. Thus,
(Hred,q(N ,→M))−2n = 0 for all n > 0, q ≥0.
(For the strongest formulation see Theorem 13.1.1. We also give an adapted version for integrally
reduced Artin k-algebras based on their product decomposition into local algebras in Theorem 6.5.9.)
The proof relies on the careful study of ‘generalized local minimum points’ of the weight function. For
their definition and main properties see subsection 13.2 (compare also with [67, section 3]).
1.7. Categorification of numerical invariants in complex analytic singularity theory.
A main consequence of the Independence Theorem is the possibility of categorifying important
numerical invariants. In algebraic geometry and, in particular, in complex analytic singularity the-
ory numerous important invariants are defined as codimensions of realizable submodules or integrally
closed ideals. In most of these cases, already the corresponding quotient modules or algebras are invari-
ants of the singularities (often they can also be defined intrinsically, without the need of a resolution),
hence so are their lattice homology modules. In this article we collect numerous such categorifications,
moreover, we give new (more general) descriptions of all existing analytic lattice homologies.
We hope that the new theory will have similar power and applicability as the topological version (or,
as the HF-theory), with the difference that in this case its applications will be mostly in the analytic
theory of singularities, or, more generally, in commutative algebra.
1.7.1. Reformulation of the analytic lattice homology of curve singularities.
The analytic lattice homology of reduced curve singularities was introduced by Ágoston and the
first author in [5]. It connects the analytic and topological properties of these singularities in a natural
way. In a certain sense it can be viewed as an algebraic analogue of the Heegaard Floer Link theory
applicable to non-planar singularities as well (see the connections in [44, 89]).

associated with the valuations corresponding to the normalizations of the components. The resulting
analytic lattice homology module Han,∗(C,o) has Euler characteristic the delta invariant δ(C,o) and
is conjectured to be functorial with respect to flat deformations [5]. By previous work of Kubasch and
the authors, the Z[U]-module structure of Han,0(C,o) detects the Gorenstein property [67]; for plane
curve singularities it detects the multiplicity [66, 67], moreover, in the irreducible case it is a complete
embedded topological invariant [68].
Our first result is a reformulation of the definition of this analytic lattice homology theory using
the framework of realizable submodules. Indeed, if we denote by nC : C →C the normalization map

1.7.3. Generalizations of the analytic lattice homology of normal surface singularities.
The analytic lattice homology of normal surface singularities of Ágoston and the first author [2]
arose as the analogue of the topological version [85], with the aim of capturing analytic instead of
topological data from the local algebra and various associated sheaf cohomologies. In [2] it is defined
for normal singularities with rational homology sphere link and categorifies the geometric genus.
The original construction of Han,∗(X,o) considers a good resolution φ : eX →X of the normal
surface singularity (X,o), and assigns to it a lattice formally generated by the irreducible components
of the exceptional divisor E (as prime divisors ). The weight function (defined on the positive orthant)
is obtained from the Hilbert function associated with the divisorial valuations and the dimensions of the
first sheaf cohomologies of the corresponding structure sheaves. Well-definedness is proved by careful
analysis of the behaviour of these numerical invariants under blow-ups. Our main observation is that,
due to Laufer’s duality and geometric genus formula [70]

For both p = 1 and 2, we can reformulate the defining modules (by using results from [128, 129])
so as to be able to apply the Nonpositivity Theorem 1.6.3 to the setting of H∗((X,o);Ωp): Corollary
7.1.5 claims that the corresponding Sn-spaces are contractible for every n > 0. We recall that a similar
statement holds in the setting of the topological lattice homology (cf. [87, Corollary 7.3.13]), which
asserts that the Sn-spaces are connected for every n > 0.
An interesting consequence of the Independence Theorem 1.4.2 is that in this analytic setting we im-
mediately get a lattice reduction–type theorem (in the spirit of [69, Reduction Theorem 3.7]), which is
especially useful for accelerating computation by reducing the rank of the lattice. Indeed, an equivalent
reformulation of the Independence Theorem claims that if D′ ⊂D are both realizations of the same
submodule N ≤M, then we can use D′ instead of D to compute the lattice homology H∗(N ,→M),
which, translated to the language of analytic lattice homology of p-forms, gives the following result
(which is slightly stronger than the original Analytic Reduction Theorem from [2]):
Theorem 1.7.5 (= Reduction Theorem 8.4.1 and Remark 8.4.8 (b) – (d)). Let (X,o) be a normal
surface singularity with good resolution φ : eX →X and reduced exceptional divisor E = ∪v∈V Ev.
In order to compute H∗((X,o);Ωp) (for p = 1,2) it is enough to consider the divisorial valuations
corresponding to a subset V ⊂V of irreducible components such that all the connected components
of the full subgraph of the resolution graph Γφ supported by V \V are rational graphs (in the sense of
Artin [10, 11]). Such a subset V can be, for example, the set of nodes, but usually it is even smaller.
Another important remark is that all of these results remain true for any isolated normal singularity
(X,o) of dimension n ≥2. Indeed, we can consider the module of meromorphic p-forms (with p = n−1


## Page 11


11
or p = n) to obtain lattice homological categorifications H∗((X,o);Ωp) of the irregularity and geometric
genus. In fact, a large part of section 8 is already written in this more general setting.
1.7.6. Lattice homological categorification of the plurigenera of normal surface singularities.
Once we have such a construction for the categorification of the irregularity and the geometric genus
of a normal surface singularity (X,o), it is natural to ask: is there an analogous theory for the several
plurigenera? Recall that in the literature there are different versions: the m-th L2-plurigenus of Watan-
abe [123], the m-th plurigenus of Knöller [61] and the m-th log-plurigenus of Morales [80]. In this
manuscript we consider their common generalizations (cf. [87, Definition 6.8.57])
∆m,n(X,o) = dimC H0(eX \E,OeX(mKeX))/H0(eX,OeX(m(KeX +E)+nE))
(m ∈Z>0, n ∈Z∩[−m,0]).
In fact, the defining submodule is realizable and the corresponding lattice homology, denoted by
H∗((X,o),∆m,n), is a well-defined categorification of the plurigenus (cf. Corollary 8.7.2). Moreover,
similarly to the previous cases, we can apply the Nonpositivity Theorem 1.6.3 (cf. Corollary 8.7.3)
and have symmetric weight function in the Gorenstein case (cf. Remark 8.7.4). On the other hand, the
Reduction Theorem does not have such a geometric reinterpretation as in 1.7.5 (see Remark 8.7.5).
We recall, that the authors already had an attempt of categorifying the plurigenera of Gorenstein
normal surface singularities in [90]. The new construction is significantly different from that, seems
more natural and has more desirable properties (see Remark 8.7.7 and part (c) of Example 12.5.15).
We also remark that the results for the m-th L2-plurigenus of Watanabe (i.e., for ∆m,−1) extend to
higher dimensional isolated normal singularities as well (cf. Corollaries 8.6.2 and 8.6.5)
1.8. Applications to singularity deformations and Newton nondegenerate singularities.
In our new formulation, it is apparent that the analytic lattice homology (or the analytic lattice
homology of n-forms for isolated normal singularities of dimension n ≥2) has deep connections to
deformation theory: if the defining quotient module remains the same along a singularity deformation,
then the lattice homology will stay the same as well. (By our convention, the studied curve singu-
larities are always reduced, whereas the higher dimensional singularities are isolated and normal.) In
the embedded Gorenstein case we can formulate this condition more explicitly using the (previously
introduced) conductor ideal:
Definition 1.8.1 (= Definition 11.3.2 (1)). Let F = {(Xt,0) ⊂(Cm,0)}t∈(C,0) be a deformation of em-
bedded Gorenstein singularities and let us denote by qt : OCm,0 →OXt,0 the natural quotient morphism.
F is a ‘conductor ideal constant deformation’ if the pullback q−1
t (CXt,0) ⊂OCm,0 is independent of the
parameter t, where CXt,0 is the conductor ideal of (Xt,0).
Applying part (c) of Theorem 1.4.4 we automatically obtain the following:
Theorem 1.8.2 (= Theorem 11.4.1). Let F = {(Xt,0) ⊂(Cm,0)}t∈(C,0) be a conductor ideal constant
deformation of the n-dimensional Gorenstein germs (Xt,0) as above. Then
• if n = 1, then the analytic lattice homology Han,∗(Xt,0) stays constant;
• if n ≥2, then the analytic lattice homology of n-forms H∗((Xt,0);Ωn) stays constant.
Some prime examples of this class are the ‘naive’ and ‘non-naive’ deformations of superisolated
surface singularities (see subsections 11.1 – 11.2 and Proposition 11.4.6).
In comparison with other types of deformations, conductor ideal constant deformations are auto-
matically δ– or pg-constant. The converse, for curve singularities is false (see, e.g., Example 11.4.7

analytic lattice homology Han,∗is stable along pg-constant deformations whenever the link is a QHS3.
If we, however, omit the rational homology sphere link condition and work with the more general in-

In fact, most of these examples rely on the theory of hypersurface singularities f : (Cm,0) →(C,0)
with Newton nondegenerate principal part. These were introduced by Kouchnirenko and Varchenko
[65, 122] and, by a theorem of Oka (cf. [94, Theorem 2.1]), their embedded topological type depends
only on their Newton boundary. Moreover, by a result of Merle and Teissier (cf. [79, 2.1.1 Théorème],
see also our formulation in Theorem 9.3.2), the pullback of the conductor ideal q−1(C )◁OCm,0 of the
germ f can be expressed as the adjoint of the monomial ideal M ◁OCm,0 generated by monomials with
exponent vector in the Newton polytope of f.
Corollary 1.8.3 (= Theorem 9.4.1 and Corollary 9.2.11). Let f : (Cm,0) →(C,0) be an isolated hy-
persurface singularity with Newton nondegenerate principal part. Then, its analytic lattice homology
(in the m > 2 case we can think of the analytic lattice homology of (m−1)-forms as well) agrees with
the symmetric lattice homology SH∗(adj(M ) ◁OCm,0) of the monomial ideal adj(M ). In particular,
Han,∗({ f = 0},0) can be combinatorially computed in terms of the Newton boundary of f.
This observation can be used to construct topologically distinct singularities with identical lattice
homology modules (see, e.g., Examples 9.6.1 and 11.4.5) and, also, to answer positively a question
proposed by the first author in [87, Problem 11.9.52]:
Corollary 1.8.4 (= Corollary 9.4.2). The analytic lattice homology (of (m−1)-forms) is constant along
an equisingular family of hypersurface singularities (in (Cm,0)) with Newton nondegenerate principal
part associated with a fixed Newton boundary.

1.9. Generalizations of the construction.
Remarkably, the conditions of the Independence Theorem 1.4.2 can be weakened. Theorem 1.4.4 (c)
already implies that we do not necessarily need M to be finitely generated, it is enough to have M/N fi-
nite dimensional over k. Similarly, the Noetherian condition on O is also required more for convenience
(we stress, however, that Rees defined integrally closed submodules in this exact setting). Moreover,
instead of counting k-dimensions, we could also use the length (of Artinian quotient modules) over (a


## Page 13


13
Noetherian ring) O to define the height functions and, hence, obtain a well-defined lattice homology
theory (though, this might give a different outcome for k-algebras than the given construction).
On the other hand, we can also leave the realm of rings and modules and consider only a k-bilinear
‘multiplication’ map • : M1 × M−1 →M0 on three (possibly) different k-vector spaces. (Our standard
setting corresponds to the substitutions M1 = O, M−1 = M0 = M and • being the ring action.) The
corresponding versions of the Independence Theorem are given in Theorems 12.5.7 and 12.5.14. They
are used to produce a reformulation of the definition of the equivariant analytic lattice homology after
[3] of normal surface singularities with rational homology sphere link (cf. Corollary 12.6.15) and the
corresponding Reduction Theorem (cf. Theorem 12.6.21).
Yet another approach to generalization arises from the following property of our new construction:
Theorem 1.9.1 (= Theorem 4.4.9 (a)). Given a Noetherian k-algebra O, a finitely generated O-module
M and N ≤M realizable, H∗(N ,→O M) can be computed on a finite rectangle: for any realization D
of N and large enough lattice point 0 ≪d ≤∞(depending on D) all the inclusions Sn ∩R(0,d) ,→Sn
are homotopy equivalences. (In fact, this result holds in each of the previously presented settings.)
Thus, from the purely computational point of view, it is enough to consider the multifiltrations FD
and F M

14.1.2) with values in the ordered commutative monoid (Ndv,∗dv) =

Such quasi-valuations can be obtained from discrete valuations by simple post-composition with the

Analogously to the case described in subsection 1.4, we can define extended quasi-valuations (cf.
Definition 14.2.1), quasi-realizability (cf. Definition 14.3.7) and the adjusted Independence Theorem
holds true in this context as well (cf. Theorem 14.3.6). There are two main advantages of working with
this quasi-valuative setup (cf. Remark 14.3.14):
• quasi-realizability is a more robust property with respect to module operations than realizabil-
ity: for example, if N is quasi-realizable in M, then so is N/N in M/N (which is false for
realizability or integral closedness, see, e.g., the example in Remark 5.1.5);
• quasi-valuations are, in a sense, finite objects, they can be determined by finite bases and the
quasi-values of their elements (cf. Remarks 14.1.6 and 14.2.8), thus, they are much easier to
construct than (non-monomial) discrete valuations (cf. Propositions 14.1.8 and 14.2.9).
Consequently, they provide a more flexible setting for further exploration, and, especially, to test Con-
jecture 1.6.1 (see, e.g., Example 14.3.15).
1.10. Structure of the article.
The structure of the article is the following. Section 2 contains the general definition of lattice ho-
mology and graded root associated with a lattice and weight function, here we follow [83, 84, 85]. In
section 3 we present the combinatorial backbone of the theory: several useful reduction arguments,
which depend only on combinatorial properties, are separated here. We especially highlight the re-
quirements of the Categorification Theorem of Ágoston and the first author.


## Page 14


14
The main results are discussed in section 4, where we introduce the concept of extended discrete
valuations, realizable submodules and state the Independence Theorem. We present the main structural
properties of the lattice homology of realizable submodules, but the more technical proofs are post-
poned in order to help easier understanding of the concept. In section 5 we discuss the case of ideals,
where realizability agrees with integral closedness and the weight function is automatically symmetric.
In the last part of this section we present the first concrete examples and computations. We introduce
‘integrally reduced’ algebras in section 6, discuss their well-definedness and study their symmetric
lattice homology modules.
In sections 7 and 8 we present concrete applications of our construction to provide categorifications
of numerical invariants of complex analytic singularities. First, in the curve case, we show a rein-
terpretation of the analytic lattice homology as corresponding to a realizable submodule. Secondly,
we provide a natural categorification of the geometric genus and irregularity of isolated singularities
with dimension ≥2. This extends the original definition of the analytic lattice homology of Ágoston
and the first author and comes with strong Reduction– and Nonpositivity Theorems. Finally, we treat
the various plurigenera of normal surface singularities: since they can be defined as codimensions of
realizable submodules, we can associate with them natural lattice homological categorifications. We
compare these with the lattice cohomological invariants introduced previously by the authors in [90].
The following section 9 discusses the case of monomial ideals and Newton nondegenerate singulari-
ties. It turns out that for this latter class, the analytic lattice homology can be computed combinatorially
from the Newton boundary. More precisely, for such analytic germs there are two naturally ocurring
weight functions (one based on the analytic data, the other one on combinatorial valuations of the New-
ton diagram), which, in general, do not agree, but give the same lattice homology. In fact, the question
of their equivalence was the main starting point of our research.
Section 10 discusses the newly introduced (lattice) homological dimension of a realizable submodule
N ≤M and its relation to the number of (extended) valuations required to obtain a realization of N. We
present a general statement providing bounds for both values, and, in the case of integrally closed
monomial ideals in the two variable polynomial ring, we prove that these determine each other.
In section 11 we discuss the relation of our analytic invariants with singularity deformations. The
case of superisolated surface germs is particularly highlighted. We introduce the conductor ideal con-
stant Gorenstein singularity deformations and present multiple examples and non-examples.
The proof of the Independence Theorem is located in section 12. Its generalized forms are also
presented here, which are able to describe the equivariant analytic lattice homology for surfaces of
Ágoston and the first author [3] as well. Section 13 contains the proof of the Nonpositivity Theorem.
In section 14 we describe a more pragmatic approach towards our lattice homology contruction via
the so-called quasi-valuations. These are in some sense finite quotients of discrete valuations, which
provide a more flexible setting and, a priori, a larger domain for our definitions.
In the Appendix we compare relevant algebraic properties of submodules: realizability and integral
closedness in the sense of Rees (respectively, in the sense of Gaffney). Throughout the manuscript
we also present several examples and problems / conjectures regarding the new theory. In the final
subsection 15.2 we list and collect further related questions and open problems.

2. BASIC PROPERTIES OF LATTICE HOMOLOGY AND THE GRADED ROOT
2.1. The lattice homology module associated with a weight function [83, 85].
The lattice homology construction associates a bigraded Z[U]-module H∗to a triple (Zr,{ev}v,w)
consisting of a lattice, a fixed basis {ev}v of it and a weight function w, see the original sources [83,
84, 85, 87] or paragraphs 2.1.2–2.1.6 below. [Here U is a free variable; hence, in fact, H∗is a bigraded
Z-module with a specified bihomogeneous endomorphism, denoted by U.] For the construction in a
more general setting (which will not be used in the present manuscript) see [6].
H∗has a direct sum decomposition ⊕q≥0Hq of graded Z[U]-modules, each Hq is 2Z-graded, with
the U-action being homogeneous of degree (−2); we use this convention to sustain compatibility with
the Heegaard Floer (Link) theory (for more on these connections see [83, 89, 88, 132]). For the sake of
notational simplicity we introduce the following distinguished graded Z[U]-modules.
2.1.1. Z[U]-modules. We modify the usual grading of the polynomial ring Z[U] in such a way that the
degree of Um is −2m (m ≥0). Besides T −
0 := Z[U], considered as a Z[U]-module with this grading,
we will consider for n ≥1 the modules T0(n) := Z[U]/(Un), too, with the induced grading. Moreover,
we can consider the Z[U]-module Z[U,U−1] with the above grading and denote by T +
0 its quotient by
the submodule U ·Z[U].
More generally, for any graded Z[U]-module P with d-homogeneous part Pd, and for any k ∈Z,
we denote by P[k] the same module graded in such a way that P[k]d+k = Pd. Then set T −

and Tk(n) := T0(n)[k]. Hence, for m ∈Z, we have T −

The lattice homology module can be defined in two different but equivalent ways, one highlighting
its geometric, the other its algebraic nature. The first construction, via the filtration of subspaces {Sn}n
of Rr, will be reviewed in the next paragraphs. For the other one, via chain complexes, see subsection
6.4.
2.1.2. The lattice and the cubical decomposition. First we consider a finitely generated free Z-
module, with a fixed basis {ev}v∈V , denoted by Zr (hence r = |V |). There is a natural partial ordering ≤
of Zr (and of Rr), defined coordinatewise, induced by the fixed basis: for lattice points ℓ1,ℓ2 ∈Zr with
ℓj = ∑v ℓjvev ( j ∈{1,2}) one says that ℓ1 ≤ℓ2 if ℓ1v ≤ℓ2v for all v ∈V . We define the minima and the
maxima of two lattice points ℓ1 = ∑v ℓ1,vev and ℓ2 = ∑v ℓ2,vev as min{ℓ1,ℓ2} = ∑v min{ℓ1,v,ℓ2,v}ev and
max{ℓ1,ℓ2} = ∑v max{ℓ1,v,ℓ2,v}ev. The support of a cycle ℓ= ∑ℓvev is defined as supp(ℓ) = ∪ℓv̸=0ev.
The space Zr ⊗R = Rr has a natural cellular decomposition into cubes. The set of zero dimensional
cubes consists of the lattice points of Zr. Any ℓ∈Zr and subset I ⊂V of cardinality q define a q-
dimensional cube (ℓ,I) ⊂Rr, which has its vertices in the lattice points {ℓ+eI′}I′, where I′ runs over
all subsets of I and eI′ denotes ∑v∈I′ ev. We regard these cubes as closed cubes of Rr, which contain
their boundaries as well. We will often use the notation □q for cubes of dimension q, whereas □◦
q will
denote their relative interior.

2.1.6. The Sn spaces and the lattice homology. For each n ∈Z we define Sn ⊂Rr as the union of
all those closed cubes □(of any dimension), which have w(□) ≤n. These spaces give an increasing
closed cubical complex filtration: ... ⊂Sn−1 ⊂Sn ⊂Sn+1 ⊂... ⊂Rr. Clearly, Sn = /0, whenever

Then for any q ≥0, set
Hq(Rr,w) := ⊕n≥mw Hq(Sn,Z) and Hred,q(Rr,w) := ⊕n≥mw eHq(Sn,Z).
We write H∗for ⊕q≥0Hq, we call q the ‘homological grading’. On the other hand, each Hq is 2Z-
graded, the summand of (−2n)-homogeneous elements Hq,−2n := (Hq)−2n is Hq(Sn,Z). This is called
the ‘weight grading’.
Also, Hq is a Z[U]-module: the U-action is given by the homological maps Hq(Sn,Z) →Hq(Sn+1,Z)
induced by the inclusions Sn ,→Sn+1. The same is true for Hred,∗. Moreover, for q = 0, any fixed ‘base-
point’ ℓw ∈Smw ⊂Sn provides a simultaneous augmentation, i.e., for each n ≥mw a splitting of form
H0(Sn,Z) = Z⊕eH0(Sn,Z); hence an augmentation of the Z[U]-modules
H0 ≃T −
−2mw ⊕Hred,0 = (⊕n≥mwZ)⊕(⊕n≥mw eH0(Sn,Z)) and H∗≃T −
−2mw ⊕Hred,∗.
Although Hred,∗(Rr,w) has finite Z-rank in any fixed weight degree, in general — without certain
additional properties of w0 — it is not finitely generated over Z, in fact, not even over Z[U].
2.1.7. Restrictions. Assume that T ⊂Rr is a subspace of Rr consisting of some union of (closed)
cubes. For any q ≥0 define Hq(T,w) as ⊕n≥min{w0|T }Hq(Sn ∩T,Z). It naturally has a graded Z[U]-
module structure. Moreover, the inclusion maps Sn ∩T ,→Sn induce a natural bigraded Z[U]-module
homomorphism
H∗(T,w) →H∗(Rr,w) (of bidegree (0,0)).
In some cases it can happen that the weights are only defined for cubes belonging to T. In our applica-
tions, T (besides the trivial T = Rr case) will be one of the following:
(i) the first quadrant (R≥0)r;
(ii) a rectangle R(0,d) = {x ∈Rr : 0 ≤x ≤d} for some lattice point d ∈(Z≥0)r, or
(iii) a path of composed edges in the lattice starting from 0: a sequence γ = {xi}t
i=0 of lattice points is
called a path from x0 to xt if xi ̸= xj for i ̸= j and xi+1 = xi + εiev(i), with εi ∈{±1} and v(i) ∈V for
every 0 ≤i < t (γ is called increasing if εi = 1 for all 0 ≤i < t); in this case T is the union of 0-cubes
marked by the points {xi}i and of the segments of type [xi,xi+1] (i.e., 1-cubes of form (xi,{ev(i)})). We
often also assume that x0 = 0.

Z-rank for every q ∈Z≥0. (This happens, e.g., when T is a finite complex.) Whenever the following
formula makes sense, we define the Euler characteristic of H∗(T,w) as
eu(H∗(T,w)) := −min{w0|T}+∑

By [86, Theorem 2.3.7], for T = R(0,d) (for some d ∈(Z≥0)r): eu(H∗(T,w)) = ∑□q⊂T(−1)q+1w(□q).
2.1.9. Cohomological formulation. Given a lattice Zr and a weight function w, we could consider the
singular cohomologies of the cubical complexes Sn (instead of their singular homologies), which would
yield the lattice cohomology module H∗(Rr,w) = ⊕q≥0 ⊕n≥mw Hq(Sn,Z). Obviously, this reverses the
direction of the U-action, which we treat by changing the weight grading to its negative, to ensure that
the action is homogeneous of degree (−2) similarly to the case of Heegaard Floer theory. On the other
hand, most of the results remain the same in the cohomological and homological formulation (e.g. the
Euler characteristic formulae, well-definedness and lattice reduction results), specifically those, which
rely only on the homotopy types of the Sn-spaces. Most of the original literature (e.g. [85, 87]) is
written in the cohomological convention with the hope of using its extra ring structure, however, some
of the recent results have nicer formulation in the homological setting (e.g. [89, 88]). We opted to use
this latter to be able to use a non-vanishing tensor product operation in subsection 6.5.
2.2. Example: the topological lattice homology of a complex normal surface singularity (X,o)
[83, 85, 87]. [We remark, that the original sources use the cohomological convention.]
We consider a good resolution φ : eX →X of (X,o) and we assume that its link LX is a rational
homology sphere. Let V be the indexing set of irreducible exceptional curves {Ev}v∈V and set r := |V |.
Then the lattice L := H2(eX,Z) is isomorphic to Zr with a fixed basis consisting of the homological
fundamental classes of the exceptional curves {Ev}v (also denoted by {Ev}v). It carries a negative
definite intersection form (, ), which naturally extends to L ⊗Q. Consider the anticanonical cycle
ZK := −c1(Ω2
eX) ∈H2(eX,Z) ≃HomZ(L,Z) ≃{ℓ∈L ⊗Q : (ℓ,L) ⊂Z}. Then, the Riemann–Roch
expression χ : ℓ7→−(ℓ,ℓ−ZK)/2 defines a weight function wtop,0(ℓ) = χ(ℓ) on Zr, hence a weight for
any cube via identity (2.1.5).
The Z[U]-modules H∗(Rr,wtop) and Hred,∗(Rr,wtop) obtained in this way are called the topological
lattice homologies. One proves that they depend only on the (diffeomorphism type of the) link LX and,
thus, they are independent of the choice of the good resolution φ (see [85, Proposition 3.4.2] or [87,
Proposition 11.1.24]). As Hred,∗(Rr,wtop) has finite Z-rank, its Euler characteristic is well-defined.
By [86, Theorem B], it satisfies eu(H∗(Rr,wtop)) = swσcan(LX)−(Z2
K +|V |)/8, where sw denotes the
Seiberg–Witten invariant Spinc(LX) →Q, which associates a rational number swσ(LX) to each spinc-
structure σ of the link. In other words, H∗(Rr,wtop) is the categorification of swσcan(LX) (normalized
by (Z2
K +|V |)/8), where σcan is the canonical spinc structure of LX.
It turns out that in this construction the set of cubes of Rr can be replaced by those from the first
quadrant (R≥0)r providing the very same homology. In the sequel we will write Htop,∗(X,o) for
H∗(Rr,wtop) = H∗((R≥0)r,wtop).
We must also mention that, in fact, there are more variants of the topological lattice homology ac-
cording to different spinc-structures. These correspond to different choices of the characteristic element
k ∈Char := {ℓ′ ∈L⊗Q|(ℓ′+ℓ,ℓ) ∈2Z for all l ∈L} used in the weight function χk(ℓ) := −(ℓ,ℓ+k)/2
of these theories. They provide bigraded Z[U]-modules with similar properties, cf. [83, 85, 87].


## Page 18

2.3. The homological graded root.
Consider the setting of subsection 2.1. The graded Z[U]-module H0 has an improvement, which
can also be used as a pictorial presentation of H0. It is the homological graded root R, a Z-graded
connected graph, which describes the containment relations of the different connected components in
the tower of spaces {Sn}n∈Z. We will use it mainly for presenting examples in a concise yet geometric
way.
Definition 2.3.1. The vertex set V of R is Z-graded, V = ⊔nVn. The vertices {v−n,i}i ∈V−n corre-
spond bijectively to the connected components {Sn,i}i of Sn. The edges are defined as follows: if two
components Sn,i and Sn+1,j of Sn and Sn+1 satisfy Sn,i ⊂Sn+1,j then we connect v−n,i and v−(n+1),j by
an edge.
From R one can read H0 as follows: each vertex of degree (−n) of R generates a free Z-summand
in H0,−2n = (H0)−2n = H0(Sn,Z) ∼= ⊕iH0(Sn,i,Z), and the U-action is marked by the edges. On the
other hand, H0 in general does not characterize R, see e.g. [83, 3.6 Examples (b)]. For more see, e.g.,
[84, 85, 87] for the cohomological version, and [89, 88] for the homological version.
3. COMBINATORIAL PROPERTIES OF LATTICE HOMOLOGY WITH SPLIT WEIGHT FUNCTION
The weight function w0 plays a crucial role in the definition of lattice homology. Indeed, a priori we
are working on the contractible space Rr, yet, the weight function determines the nontrivial level sets
{Sn}n. In the case of the topological lattice homology (cf. Example 2.2) w0 is given by a Riemann-
Roch expression, however in several analytic and combinatorial situations it has a different, specific
structure: it is a sum of two (geometrically defined) functions, one of them increasing, the other one
decreasing and they satisfy some key additional properties, too, see below. This decomposition usually
reflects (or, is the consequence of) some kind of (e.g., Serre–, Gorenstein–) duality. In this section
we present the general combinatorial setup of such weight functions. In the first subsection we follow
[2, 87] (where the cohomological version is treated), while the second subsection contains some new
technical results whose proofs the reader might skip at first reading to get to the description of the main
constructions faster.
3.1. The combinatorial setup.
3.1.1. Split weight function. Consider the lattice Zr with a fixed basis {ev}v∈V , |V | = r. Fix also an
element d ∈(Z≥0)r ∪{∞} and consider the rectangle R := R(0,d). Furthermore, assume that to each
ℓ∈R∩Zr we assign
(i) an integer h(ℓ) such that h(0) = 0 and h(ℓ+ev) ≥h(ℓ) for any v ∈V , ℓ,ℓ+ev ∈R∩Zr
(i.e., h is increasing);
(ii) and an integer h◦(ℓ) such that h◦(ℓ+ev) ≤h◦(ℓ) for any v ∈V and ℓ,ℓ+ev ∈R∩Zr
(i.e., h◦is decreasing).
E.g., once h and d are fixed with (i) and d ∈(Z≥0)r finite, a possible choice for h◦is hsym
d
, the sym-
metrization of h with respect to d, i.e., hsym
d
(ℓ) := h(d −ℓ) for every ℓ∈R ∩Zr. We sometimes call h
and h◦the ‘height functions’.
We consider the set of cubes of R as in 2.1.2 and the weight function
(3.1.2)
w0 : R∩Zr →Z defined by w0(ℓ) := h(ℓ)+h◦(ℓ)−h◦(0).


## Page 19


19
Clearly w0(0) = 0. Moreover, we define the other functions {wq}q≥1 by (2.1.5). They define the lattice
homology module H∗(R,w). In particular, if Hred,∗(R,w) has finite rank (which in the d < ∞case is
automatic), we have a well-defined Euler characteristic eu(H∗(R,w)) as well (see paragraph 2.1.8).
3.1.3. Key properties. We will focus on pairs (h,h◦) which satisfy certain additional combinatorial
properties.
Definition 3.1.4. We say that h satisfies the ‘matroid rank inequality’ if
(3.1.5)
h(ℓ1)+h(ℓ2) ≥h(min{ℓ1,ℓ2})+h(max{ℓ1,ℓ2}), ℓ1,ℓ2 ∈R∩Zr.
Note that the matroid rank inequality and the increasing property of h implies the following so-called
‘stability property’
(3.1.6)
h(ℓ) = h(ℓ+ev) ⇒h(ℓ+ ¯ℓ) = h(ℓ+ ¯ℓ+ev);
valid for any ℓ, ¯ℓ∈R∩Zr such that ¯ℓ≥0, ¯ℓ≱ev (i.e., ev /∈supp( ¯ℓ)) and ℓ+ℓ+ev ∈R∩Zr.
Example 3.1.7. Assume that V is a k-vector space (with k some underlying field) endowed with a
rank r decreasing multifiltration F on it, i.e., for any lattice point ℓ∈Zr there is assigned a linear
subspace F(ℓ) ⊂V, such that F(ℓ) ⊂F(ℓ′) if ℓ≥ℓ′. We assume that F(0) = V. If we require that
h(ℓ) := dimk(V/F(ℓ)) < ∞for any ℓ∈Zr, then we can define the Hilbert function of F as ℓ7→h(ℓ).
It satisfies h(0) = 0 and it is increasing: h(ℓ) ≥h(ℓ′) if ℓ≥ℓ′. However, in this generality the matroid
rank inequality is usually not satisfied.
On the other hand, we have the following simple criterion:
Lemma 3.1.8. Suppose that V and {F(ℓ)}ℓ∈Zr are as in Example 3.1.7. If the multifiltration satisfies
the identity
(3.1.9)
F(max{ℓ1,ℓ2}) = F(ℓ1)∩F(ℓ2), for all ℓ1,ℓ2 ∈Zr,
then the Hilbert function ℓ7→h(ℓ) = dimk(V/F(ℓ)) satisfies the matroid rank inequality.
Proof. Left to the reader.
□
Example 3.1.10. (V, F as in Example 3.1.7.) If the multifiltration F corresponds to a Zr-grading
V ∼= ⊕a∈ZrVa of the vector space , i.e., F(ℓ) := ⊕a≥ℓVa for all ℓ∈(Z≥0)r, then it naturally satisfies
condition (3.1.9).
Definition 3.1.11. We say that the pair (h,h◦) satisfies the ‘Combinatorial Duality Property’ in R(0,d)
(often abbreviated as CDP) if h(ℓ+ ev) −h(ℓ) and h◦(ℓ+ ev) −h◦(ℓ) are not simultaneously nonzero
for any ℓ, ℓ+ev ∈R∩Zr. Furthermore, whenever d < ∞, we say that the height function h satisfies the
CDP in R(0,d) if the pair (h,hsym
d
) satisfies it.
Examples of pairs (h,h◦) satisfying the CDP can be found in [2, 5] or in several parts of this note
(see e.g., Example 3.1.13 or Lemma 9.2.9).
Theorem 3.1.12 (Categorification Theorem). [2, Theorem 5.2.1] Assume that d < ∞, the height func-
tion h satisfies the stability property, and the pair (h,h◦) satisfies the Combinatorial Duality Property.
Then
eu(H∗(R,w)) = h◦(0)−h◦(d),


## Page 20


20
i.e., H∗categorifies the difference h◦(0)−h◦(d).
This theorem provides us a simple recipe to obtain lattice homological categorifications of certain
numerical invariants. Our results in this manuscript specialize this recipe even further.
Example 3.1.13. T. Ágoston and the first author already defined numerous lattice (co)homology the-
ories in the realm of complex singularity theory using the decomposition of the weight function into
pairs (h,h◦) satisfying the above properties. These constructions have the common key property that
the resulting lattice homology modules categorify important geometric invariants. For example, the
analytic lattice homology of reduced complex curve singularities [5] categorifies the delta invariant,
whereas the analytic lattice homology of normal surface singularities (with QHS3 link) [2] categorifies
the geometric genus. We will review (and highly generalize in an algebraic context) these constructions
from a unified viewpoint in sections 7 and 8.
We note that the weight function of the topological lattice homology of normal surface singularities
(cf. Example 2.2) does not have a splitting into a pair (h,h◦) with the required properties, at least
known by the authors.
3.2. Weight functions satisfying the matroid rank inequality.
In this subsection we will provide some new technical results (combinatorial in nature) regarding
weight functions satisfying the matroid rank inequality. On first reading the reader might skip the
proofs of these statements in order to reach faster the main construction of this manuscript — the lattice
homology of integrally closed submodules — , however, these results will be needed to prove its main
properties. Our setting is the following: we work over the lattice Zr with standard basis {ev}v∈V and
consider a weight function w0 : Zr →Z defined on some rectangle R(d−,d+) (here we allow d−= −∞
and / or d+ = ∞as well) satisfying the matroid rank inequality as described in (3.1.5), or, equivalently,
satisfying:
w0(ℓ+ev)−w0(ℓ) ≥w0(ℓ+ ¯ℓ+ev)−w0(ℓ+ ¯ℓ)
∀ℓ, ¯ℓ∈Zr, such that ¯ℓ≥0, ¯ℓ≱ev,
and R(ℓ,ℓ+ ¯ℓ+ev) ⊂R(d−,d+).
Examples 3.2.1. (i) If h and h◦of paragraph 3.1.1 are defined on the rectangle R(0,d) and both satisfy
the matroid rank inequality, then so does the weight function w0 defined by formula (3.1.2). Moreover,
this is also true if h satisfies the matroid rank inequality and h◦:= hsym
d

complex normal surface singularities (see Example 2.2) satisfies the matroid rank inequality.

4. LATTICE HOMOLOGY OF REALIZABLE SUBMODULES
In this section we present the main conceptual result of the manuscript. First we set some notations
and describe the general constructions involved. In fact, we will develop further the construction of
Example 3.1.7, although, contrary to that case, this time we will consider two filtrations. More specif-
ically, we will replace the vector space V of Example 3.1.7 by a Noetherian ring O, with a k-algebra
structure, and the first Zr-filtration will be associated with r discrete valuations on O. On the other
hand, the second multifiltration will be supported on an O-module M.
Throughout this section k will always denote a field (we do not impose any restriction on it, though,
in all of our applications this will be C), O will be a k-algebra, usually assumed to be Noetherian
(only for convenience and stronger relations with commutative algebra), and M a finitely generated
O-module (once again, finite generacy is assumed only for convenience — for the more general setup
see subsection 12.5). In several of our applications the corresponding k-algebra O will be the local
analytic C-algebra OX,o of a complex analytic spacegerm (X,o), whereas M an O-module of some
kind of differential forms.
4.1. Extended discrete valuations and the associated filtrations.
We will use the notion of discrete valuations in the following (generalized) sense (cf. e.g., [39, 117]).
(Such valuations have different names in the literature, as semivaluations, or pseudo-valuations.)
Definition 4.1.1. A function v : O →N = N ∪{∞} is a discrete valuation if the following properties
hold:
(a) v(fg) = v(f)+v(g) for all f,g ∈O (where ∞+n = n+∞= ∞+∞= ∞for every n ∈N);
(b) v(f +g) ≥min{v(f),v(g)} for all f,g ∈O ;
(c) v is nonconstant, i.e., there exists some f ∈O with v(f) ∈N>0 (hence, the value of its powers
can get arbitrarily high).
From the definition one can deduce (see e.g., the argument in [39, 1.2]) that v(0) = ∞, v|k∗= 0 and
the set pv := { f ∈O : v(f) = ∞} (which we will sometimes call the ‘core’ of the valuation) is a prime
ideal of O. Moreover, in property (b) we necessarily have equality if v(f) ̸= v(g).
Remark 4.1.2. Note that Definition 4.1.1 is weaker than the ‘classical’ definition of the discrete valu-
ation: we do not require neither that O is a domain, nor that pv = (0). Also, we see immediately that
for any nilpotent element f ∈O, we must have v(f) = ∞. Also, any such discrete valuation descends
to a ‘classical’ discrete valuation vO/pv on O/pv with vO/pv(f +pv) := v(f) being independent of the
representative chosen.
Definition 4.1.3. For a fixed O-module M we will call a pair (v,vM) of maps an ‘extended discrete
valuation’ if
• v : O →N is a discrete valuation in the sense of Definition 4.1.1;
• vM : M →Z = Z∪{∞} satisfies the following properties:
(a) vM(fm) = v(f)+vM(m) for all f ∈O,m ∈M
(where, similarly as above, ∞+n = n+∞= ∞+∞= ∞for every n ∈Z);
(b) vM(m+m′) ≥min{vM(m),vM(m′)} for all m,m′ ∈M;
(c) and vM is nonconstant, meaning that there exists some m ∈M with vM(m) ̸= ∞.

‘extended’ and vM its ‘extension’. Moreover, for the pair (v,vM) we might just write v.
Similar (although real valued) objects, called ‘v-valuative functions’, were recently studied in [48],
see especially [48, Definition 3.1].
Example 4.1.4. If v : O →N is a discrete valuation and M = O p, then we can construct an exten-
sion e.g., as vM : (f1,..., fp) 7→min{v(f1),...,v(fp)}. Moreover, any extension can be restricted to a

for any n ∈N\{0} and n′ ∈Z.

As we will see in the next section this general construction (applied for different modules and collec-
tions D) recovers several previously defined lattice homology constructions as particular cases, more-
over, we will list several new applications as well.
The construction, which relies on the collection of extended valuations D, suggests that the output
H∗(Rr,wD) depends essentially on the choice of D. However, our main theorem, stated in the following
subsection, says that (under certain additional assumption regarding D, which is ‘almost automatically’
satisfied) the lattice homology module H∗(Rr,wD) depends only on the pair F M
D (0) ≤M.
4.3. The main theorem.
The precise statement is the following:
Theorem 4.3.1 (Independence Theorem). Let k be any field, O a (Noetherian) k-algebra and M a
finitely generated module over it. Let D = {v1,...,vr} and D′ = {v′
1,...,v′
r′} be two collections of
extended discrete valuations (satisfying Assumption 4.1.9). Suppose that the following conditions hold:
• F M

D′) satisfy the Combinatorial Duality Property (cf. Definition
3.1.11).
Then the spaces Sn,D and Sn,D′ associated with the corresponding lattices and weight functions are
homotopy equivalent for every n ∈Z. Even more, the homotopy equivalences respect the inclusions
Sn,D ,→Sn+1,D and Sn,D′ ,→Sn+1,D′, hence
H∗(Rr,wD) ∼= H∗(Rr′,wD′) as bigraded Z[U]-modules.
That is, the lattice homology and the homotopy types of the cubic complexes Sn,D are independent
of the collection D of extended discrete valuations, they are invariants merely of the module M and the
finite codimensional submodule F M
D (0) ≤M.
The proof of the Independence Theorem will be given in section 12, here we will discuss its main
consequences. For a more general formulation see subsection 12.5.
The Independence Theorem implies that, once we fixed the module M, we can associate a well-
defined lattice homology module to any submodule N of M, whenever N can be ‘realized’ as F M
D (0)

submodules and give the first properties. We proceed in two steps, first we test the ‘realizability’
condition N = F M
D (0), then the additional CDP property.
In the sequel we will omit D from the notation of the Hilbert functions, weight functions and Sn
spaces whenever the finite collection of extended discrete valuations we use is clear from the context.

4.4. Lattice homology of realizable submodules and its main properties.
Set again some field k, a Noetherian k-algebra O and a finitely generated O-module M.
Definition 4.4.1. Let N be a finite codimensional submodule of M. N is called ‘realizable’ if some
finite collection D of extended discrete valuations satisfies N = F M
D (0). In this case D is called a
‘realization of N’. We say that the realization is a ‘CDP realization’ if the associated pair of functions
(hD,h◦
D) satisfies the Combinatorial Duality Property.
These realizability conditions on N ≤M are highly nontrivial, such a realization by some D might
not exist. For example if we choose M = O, then the condition for the existence of such a realization
is equivalent to the ideal N being integrally closed, see Corollary 5.1.6 below. In the general finitely
generated module setting we have the following result:
Proposition 4.4.2 (=Theorem 4.5.4). If the finitely generated, finite k-codimensional submodule N is
integrally closed in M in the sense of Rees [107] (see also [118, Section 16] or Definition 4.5.3 below),
then it is realizable.
The next natural question is the following: is the CDP assumption in the realizability of N (and hence
in the Independence Theorem) an ‘essential’ additional restriction? We have the following answer:
Observation 4.4.3 (=Lemma 4.6.1). If D is a realization of N, then we can easily construct from it a
CDP realization D♮just by postcomposing every extended discrete valuation with the ‘doubling’ map
Z ·2−→Z (the new valuations are denoted simply by {2vv}v).
Thus, using the Independence Theorem 4.3.1, Proposition 4.4.2 and Observation 4.4.3, for any real-
izable / finite codimensional integrally closed submodule N we can define its lattice homology.
Definition 4.4.4. Fix a Noetherian k-algebra O and a finitely generated O-module M. Consider a
(finitely generated) finite k-codimensional realizable submodule N ≤M. From all the possible ones
choose an arbitrary realization D = {vv}v∈V in the sense of Definition 4.4.1. Fix the lattice Zr, with
rank r = |V | (with its standard basis). Take the finite collection D♮= {2vv}v∈V of extended discrete
valuations and consider the corresponding multifiltrations Zr ∋ℓ7→FD♮(ℓ),F M
D♮(ℓ) (introduced in
(4.1.8)). Then define the functions

h(ℓ) = dimk O/FD♮(ℓ)
and
h◦(ℓ) = dimk M/F M
D♮(−ℓ).
Set the weight function w0(ℓ) = h(ℓ) + h◦(ℓ) −h◦(0) on the lattice points ℓ∈Zr (see Lemma 4.2.1)
and extend it to every cube in the cubical decomposition of R⊗Zr as in (2.1.5). Finally, consider the

Remark 4.4.8. By the Independence Theorem 4.3.1, if the chosen collection D is already a CDP
realization of N, then we do not need to convert it to the ‘doubled’ version D♮, the construction with D
will yield the same result.
We state here the first properties of H∗(N ,→M).
They will be discussed and proved in later
(sub)sections. The first algebraic examples will be given in subsection 5.4.
Theorem 4.4.9. Let O be a Noetherian k-algebra, N ≤M finitely generated modules with N realizable
(hence, by definition, dimk(M/N) < ∞).
(a) H∗(N ,→O M) can be computed on a finite rectangle: for any fixed CDP realization D = {v1,...,vr}
of N and lattice point 0 ≪d ≤∞large enough (the precise meaning of ‘large enough’ depends on
the chosen realization), the inclusion R(0,d) ⊂Rr induces a bigraded Z[U]-module isomorphism
H∗(R(0,d),w) →H∗(Rr,w) ∼= H∗(N ,→M) (where w is as defined in (4.4.7)). In fact, the inclu-
sions Sn ∩R(0,d) ,→Sn are all homotopy equivalences for every n ≥mw (they are homotopy inverses
of strong deformation retractions Sn ↘Sn ∩R(0,d)).
(b) The Euler characteristic is well-defined and satisfies eu(H∗(N ,→M)) = dimk(M/N), i.e., the lat-
tice homology H∗(N ,→M) categorifies the codimension codimk(N ,→M).
(c) The Z[U]-module (and the homotopy type of the Sn spaces) only depends on the quotient module
M/N over the algebra O/AnnO(M/N), where AnnO(M/N) denotes the annihilator of the quotient
O-module M/N.
This theorem shows that we can construct a well-defined categorification of any numerical invariant,
whenever this number can be defined as the codimension of some realizable submodule. It turns out that
in the theory of complex analytic singularities many invariants are of this nature: the delta invariant of
reduced curve singularities (see section 7), the geometric genus and irregularity of isolated singularities
of dimension ≥2 (see section 8) and the plurigenera of normal surface singularities (see subsections
8.6–8.7) are some highlighted examples.

Let us now recall Rees’s definition of integral dependence over a submodule in the setting of an
inclusion N ≤M of finitely generated O-modules (with O Noetherian).
Notation 4.5.2. For any prime ideal p◁O let us denote the fraction field of O/p by κ(p) and the κ(p)-
vector space M ⊗O κ(p) ∼= Mp/pMp by W(p). We denote by ιp the composition M →M/pM →W(p)
of the canonical maps.
Definition 4.5.3. [107], see also [118, Section 16]. An element m ∈M is said to be ‘integral over N’
if for every minimal prime ideal p in O and every discrete valuation ring Ov (in the classical sense)
between O/p ≤Ov ≤κ(p) (i.e., the corresponding valuation v satisfies v ≥0 on O/p and v(a) = ∞if
and only if a = 0 in O/p) we have ιp(m) ∈ιp(N)Ov.
The submodule N is called ‘integrally closed in M’, if every element m ∈M, which is integral over
N, already lies in N.
Theorem 4.5.4. Let k be a field, O a Noetherian k-algebra, N ≤M an inclusion of finitely generated O-
modules with codimk(N ,→M) < ∞. Then N is integrally closed in M if and only if there exists a finite
collection D = {vv}v∈V of extended discrete valuations, with their cores pv = {f ∈O : vv(f) = ∞}
being minimal prime ideals, such that N = F M
D (0). Hence, finite codimension and integral closedness
implies realizability.
The proof of Theorem 4.5.4 and further algebraic discussions are separated in the Appendix (see es-
pecially Corollary 15.1.9). We do not have a proof for the opposite statement however (that is, whether
the realizability by a certain D implies that N is integrally closed in M). Nevertheless, by Theorem
4.5.4 we can apply our results from subsection 4.4 safely for any finite codimensional integrally closed
submodule (in the sense of Rees).

4.7. Reduction to a finite rectangle and Euler characteristic.
In this subsection we will prove that, given any CDP realization D of N, the lattice homology module
H∗(N ,→M) can be computed on a finite rectangle R(0,d) (where d depends on the CDP realization
chosen) and it categorifies the codimension codimk(N ,→M).
First, notice that, by Definition 4.1.3 of extended discrete valuations and Assumption 4.1.9, for any
lattice point d ≫0 large enough F M
D (−d) = M.
Notation 4.7.1. Associated with a (CDP) realization D of N we will denote the unique smallest lattice
point d ≥0 satisfying F M
D (−d) = M by dD ∈Zr (for well-definedness use the matroid rank inequality
(3.1.5) for h◦, cf. Observation 4.1.12). Then for all d ≥dD we have h◦(d) ≡0. Notice that, although
this lattice point dD is unique, in most geometric situations it is not straightforward to compute, see
e.g., subsection 8.3.
We now prove that the function w0 satisfies the matroid rank inequality, which will allow us to use
Corollary 3.2.4 to reduce the full lattice to the finite rectangle R(0,dD).
Proof of Lemma 4.2.1 and Theorem 4.4.9 (a). From Observation 4.1.12 and part (i) of Example 3.2.1,
it is clear, that w0 satisfies the matroid rank inequality.
On the other hand, for any standard basis element ev of Zr, by the nontriviality conditions in
the Definitions 4.1.1 (c) and 4.1.3 (c) of the extended discrete valuations (applied for vv), we have
limk→∞h(kev) = ∞and limk→−∞h◦(kev) = ∞. Moreover, for any lattice points ℓ+ ≥dD and ℓ−≤0 we
have h◦(ℓ+) = h◦(dD) = 0 and h(ℓ−) = h(0) = 0. Therefore, we can use Corollary 3.2.4 to prove that
w0 is a weight function and that the lattice homology module H∗(N ,→M) can be computed on any
rectangle R(0,d) with dD ≤d ≤∞.
□
Proof of Theorem 4.4.9 (b). Fix any CDP realization D of N and a lattice point d with dD ≤d ≤∞.
Then by part (a) of this Theorem 4.4.9 and Theorem 3.1.12 applied for the rectangle R(0,d) we get
eu(H∗(N ,→M)) = eu(H∗(R(0,d),w)) = h◦(0)−h◦(d). Hence eu(H∗(N ,→M)) = dimk M/N.
□
Corollary 4.7.2. In the setting of Theorem 4.4.9, a realization D of N is a CDP realization if and only
if the corresponding height functions satisfy the CDP condition in the rectangle R(0,dD).
Proof. The nontrivial direction follows from the definition of the lattice point dD and Proposition 3.2.6,
since the height functions satisfy the matroid rank inequality (cf. Observation 4.1.12).
□
4.8. Reduction to M/N. The lattice homology of M/N.
In this subsection we will prove part (c) of Theorem 4.4.9, namely that the lattice homology of a
realizable O-submodule N ≤M only depends on the quotient module M/N over the quotient algebra
O/AnnO(M/N).
First, we fix the setting. Let O be a Noetherian k-algebra and assume that Q is a finite k-dimensional
O-module such that it can be realized as M/N, where M is a finitely generated O-module and N is
realizable submodule (e.g., an integrally closed submodule). Since M is finitely generated, there exists
a surjective homomorphism α : O p →M from a free module of finite rank p ∈N. By Lemma 4.5.1
(b) N := α−1(N) is realizable in O p, hence the quotient module Q can also be represented as O p/N ,
with N realizable.

H∗(N ,→M) only depends on the O/AnnO(Q)-module Q (see the previously proved Theorem 4.4.9
(c)). We refer to it as the lattice homology of Q, and it will be denoted by H∗(Q).
By Theorem 4.4.9 (b), eu(H∗(Q)) = dim(Q) holds. In other words, the matching Q ⇝H∗(Q) is the
categorification of the numerical invariant Q ⇝dimk Q.

Proposition 4.8.2 (2) and Corollary 5.1.6). Therefore, the Artin k-algebra O/AnnO(Q) is of form
Om/I with I = I . For more on these types of algebras see subsection 6.

4.9. Bounds on the gradings.
In this subsection we will present some natural bounds on the gradings of the nonzero elements of
the lattice homology of realizable submodules. Fix a finite k-codimensional realizable submodule N in
the finitely generated O-module M, where O is a Noetherian k-algebra. Recall that its lattice homology
has the following structure: H∗(N ,→M) = ⊕q≥0 ⊕n∈Z Hq,2n, where q denotes the homological grading
and 2n denotes the weight grading.
4.9.1. Homological grading. It is convenient and natural to introduce the following terminology: the
(lattice) homological dimension of N ≤M (or of M/N) is
(4.9.2)
homdim(N ,→M) :=



max{q : Hq(N ,→M) ̸= 0}
if N ̸= M;
−1
if N = M.
Remark 4.9.3. Equivalently, homdim(N ,→M) = max
 {q : Hred,q(N ,→M) ̸= 0}∪{−1}). Indeed, if
N ̸= M, then Hred,∗̸= 0, since eu(H∗) ̸= 0 and, using any realization, the lattice point 0, with w(0) = 0,
gives a distinct connected component of S0 (by a similar argument to Remark 13.1.6).
If D is a realization of N with cardinality r ≥1, then by Lemma 4.6.1 (2) we have a CDP realiza-
tion D♮with |D♮| = r. Then each Sn space associated with D♮is a compact subspace of Rr, hence
H≥r(Sn,Z) = 0 for all n ∈Z, that is, ⊕q≥rHq(N ,→M) = 0. [The case of D = /0 corresponds to N = M
and homdim(N ,→M) = −1.] We therefore have the following bound:
Proposition 4.9.4. homdim(N ,→M) ≤min{|D| : D a realization of N}−1.
In particular, if we can find a realization D consisting of only one element, then H∗is concentrated
merely in H0 (this is the case, e.g., for the analytic lattice homology of irreducible curve singularities
or almost rational surface singularities, see sections 7 and 8).
From the other point of view, this inequality can be also seen as a lower bound for the numeri-
cal invariant min{|D| : D a realization of N} associated with a pair N ≤M. This invariant is hardly
nontrivial. In the case of realizable (or, equivalently, integrally closed, cf. Corollary 5.1.6) ideals
I = N ≤M = O, it is usually strictly smaller than the number of Rees valuations (see Example 9.6.2),
which are uniquely associated to the ideal I (cf. [106]).
Regarding the inequality in Proposition 4.9.4, it is not clear how sharp it actually is. In the case
of finite codimensional integrally closed ideals, in all our computations with homdim(I ,→O) = 0
we succeeded to find a single valuation which realizes I . This encourages us to make the following
conjecture:
Conjecture 4.9.5. Let us suppose that I ◁O2 = k[x1,x2] is a finite codimensional, integrally closed
ideal. We conjecture that if homdim(I ,→O) = 0, then I can be realized by a single valuation, i.e.,
I = { f ∈O2 : v(f) ≥dI } for some discrete valuation v and integer dI .
In fact, in the case of monomial ideals M ◁O2 = k[x1,x2] the conjecture holds true, even more, the
following much stronger converse of Proposition 4.9.4 is also true:
Theorem 4.9.6. For a finite codimensional integrally closed monomial ideal M ◁O2:
homdim(M ,→O2) = min{|D| : D a realization of M }−1.
