35
The proof and some more detailed discussions will be given in section 10. For more supporting
examples for Conjecture 4.9.5 see also subsections 9.6 and 15.2.1.
4.9.7. Weight grading. By the definition of the weight function, for any given CDP realization D
of N, the set of weight values of wD,0 is bounded from below and the minimum is denoted by mwD.
Moreover, this value can be read off from the bigraded lattice homology Z[U]-module H∗(R|D|,wD)
as mwD = −max{n ∈Z : H0,2n ̸= 0} (see the grading convention in paragraph 2.1.6). Therefore, as a
consequence of the Independence Theorem 4.3.1 we have the following:
Corollary 4.9.8. For any CDP realization D of N, the minimum value mwD = minwD of the cor-
responding weight function is independent of the realization chosen, it is merely an invariant of the
realizable submodule N. It can be read from the lattice homology module as well:
mwD = minwD = −max{n ∈Z : H0,2n(N ,→M) ̸= 0}.
We would like to highlight, however, that the computation of mw and its ‘geometric’ interpretation
seems nontrivial, even in the case of the previously defined lattice (co)homology theories (see e.g., [53]
for some results in the case of analytic lattice homology of reduced curve singularities). In comparison
with the Heegaard Floer theory, it is the analogue of the d-invariant (cf. [100]).
On the other end, Theorem 4.4.9 (a) implies that, for n ≫0 large enough, the Sn spaces are strong
deformation retractable to the rectangle R(0,d), i.e., they are all contractible, hence Hred,−2n = 0 for
n ≫0. In the case of an algebraically closed field and of a local base ring (which is the case for most
applications below) we also have the following strengthening:
Theorem 4.9.9 (Nonpositivity Theorem). Let k be an algebraically closed field, (O,m) a local Noe-
therian k-algebra, M a finitely generated O-module and N ≤M a finite k-codimensional realizable
submodule. Then for every positive integer n > 0, the Sn space is contractible (this is highlighted in the
name of the theorem), hence the weight-grading of the reduced lattice homology module is nonnegative,
i.e., Hred,q,−2n(N ,→M) = 0 for all q ≥0 and n > 0. In other words,
Hred,q(N ,→M) =
M
0≥n≥mw
Hred,q,−2n(N ,→M)
for all q ≥0.
Moreover, if N ̸= M, the upper bound is sharp: S0 is not connected.
The proof will be given in section 13. The name ‘Nonpositivity Theorem’ comes from the origi-
nal version [67, Theorem 6.1.1] presented in the case of the analytic lattice cohomology of reduced
complex analytic curve singularities. In fact, our proof is just a direct generalization of the original
argument presented in [loc. cit.].
5. SYMMETRIC LATTICE HOMOLOGY OF INTEGRALLY CLOSED IDEALS
In this section we will discuss the case when M = O and N = I ◁O is a finite k-codimensional ideal
in it. In this case, the realizability of I is equivalent with integral closedness, moreover, the weight
function corresponding to any realization is symmetric.
5.1. Extending valuations to a rank one free module.
Let us fix a field k and a Noetherian k-algebra O. If we consider the rank one free module M = O,
then every discrete valuation v : O →N extends to M (see Remark 4.1.5 (i)). In fact, these extensions
can be easily classified.


## Page 36


36
Lemma 5.1.1. If we consider O, as a finitely generated module M over itself, and a discrete valuation
v : O →N, then its extensions (v,vM) are precisely pairs of form (v,v+n′) for some n′ ∈Z.
Proof. Clearly vM(1) = n′ for some n′ ∈Z and part (a) of Definition 4.1.3 describes vM uniquely.
□
The corresponding filtrations (cf. (4.1.7)) then naturally satisfy F M
v (ℓ) = Fv(ℓ−n′)◁O.
Corollary 5.1.2. An ideal I ◁O is realizable in the sense of Definition 4.4.1 (with M = O and N = I ),
if and only if there exists some finite collection D := {v1,...,vr} of discrete valuations on O and a
lattice point d ∈(Z≥0)r such that I = FD(d). (Here the name ‘collection’ refers to the fact that all
these valuations respect Assumption 4.1.9)
Remark 5.1.3. Notice that the nonnegativity conditions dv ≥0 (for all 1 ≤v ≤r) on the components
of the lattice point d = ∑r
v=1 dvev are, in fact, unnecessary; Corollary 5.1.2 remains true without them.
Indeed, the negative components dw ≤0 contribute only trivial Fvw(dw) = O terms to the intersection
I = ∩r
v=1Fvv(dv).
This reformulation allows us to compare the realizability property with integral closedness. This
latter one has a much more intrinsic definition for ideals than for modules. Indeed, an element r ∈O
is called integral over I ◁O, if there exists an integer n ∈Z>0 and elements ai ∈I i, i ∈{1,...,n},
such that
(5.1.4)
rn +a1rn−1 +a2rn−2 +...+an−1r +an = 0.
The integral closure of I is the ideal I containing all such elements, and I is called integrally closed
if I = I . The equation (5.1.4) of integral dependence implies that for any discrete valuation v : O →N
(in the sense of Definition 4.1.1) and integer ℓ∈Z, the ideal Fv(ℓ) is integrally closed. Moreover, the
intersections of integrally closed ideals or their preimages under ring maps are also integrally closed.
On the other hand, we do not have a similar property for the images of integrally closed ideals:
Remark 5.1.5. The image of an integrally closed ideal under a ring map is not (necessarily) integrally
closed, not even under surjective maps. For example, the ideal (tm) is integrally closed in the one-
variable polynomial ring k[t], but its image 0 ◁k[t]/(tm) under the natural quotient homomorphism
q : k[t] →k[t]/(tm) is not.
For more details on integral closure of ideals consult [118].
Corollary 5.1.6. Let O be a Noetherian ring with a k-algebra structure and I a finite codimensional
ideal in it. Then I is realizable (i.e., by Corollary 5.1.2, there exists a finite collection D = {v1,...,vr}
of discrete valuations and a lattice point d ∈Zr such that I = FD(d)) if and only if it is integrally
closed.
Proof. Clearly, if I = FD(d) for some collection D and lattice point d as in the statement, then
I = ∩v∈V Fv(dv) is integrally closed in O.
For the other direction, if I is finite codimensional and integrally closed, it is enough to find any
(not necessarily finite) set D′ of valuations which together realize the ideal (finite codimensionality
already implies finiteness of any minimal collection D′, if there exists such). However, [118, Theorem
6.8.3] (translated to our language) states exactly that for an integrally closed ideal I the set of all
discrete valuations v : O →N ∪{∞}, with pv = {f ∈O : v(f) = ∞} being a minimal prime ideal of

37
O, satisfies the above requirement. Hence any finite codimensional integrally closed ideal is realizable.
[Another, in principle more involved, proof would be to simply point to the existence of Rees valuations
in Noetherian rings, see [106] or [118, Theorem 10.2.2].]
□

Proposition 5.1.8. Let us consider the setting of the previous Proposition 5.1.7 and introduce the

5.2. Review of the construction in the symmetric case.
Assume that O is a Noetherian ring with a k-algebra structure. The construction that we wish to
present might have two different starting points. In the first case, we start from a finite collection
of discrete valuations on O and a lattice point and assign to them an ideal and a symmetric lattice
homology module (this will be the case, for example, in the construction of the combinatorial lattice
homology assigned to a Newton boundary in paragraph 9.2.5). In the second case, we start from the

will be the case in all analytic lattice homology constructions from sections 7 and 8).

It supports an additional and natural Z2-action induced by the automorphisms of the Sn,D spaces

The Independence Theorem 4.3.1 has the following counterpart in this setting:
Theorem 5.2.3 (Symmetric version of the Independence Theorem). Let k be any field and O a (Noe-

Then the spaces Sn,D and Sn,D′ associated with the corresponding lattices and weight functions are

That is, the lattice homology and the homotopy types of the cubical complexes Sn,D are independent

5.3. The symmetric lattice homology of integrally closed ideals.
Motivated by the Independence Theorem 5.2.3, as a second starting point, we might fix the Noether-
ian ring O with a k-algebra structure and an integrally closed ideal I ◁O with finite k-codimension.


## Page 39


39
Definition 5.3.1. If the ideal I ◁O can be written as FD(d) for some collection of discrete valuations
D = {v1,...,vr} and lattice point d ∈(Z≥0)r, then we say that the pair (D,d) is a ‘realization of I ’.
By Corollary 5.1.2, this is equivalent to the collection
Dext := {(v1,v1 −d1),...,(vr,vr −dr)}
of extended discrete valuations being a realization of I as an O-submodule of the rank one, free
module O in the sense of Definition 4.4.1. If the pair (hD,hsym
D,d) of height functions satisfies the
Combinatorial Duality Property in the rectangle R(0,d), then we call (D,d) a ‘CDP realization’.
Here several comments are in order. We stress that the realization of some ideal by a pair (D,d)
is highly non-unique. For example, assume that I is realized by some (D,d). Then even with the
very same D it can happen that there are several distinct lattice points d′ such that for all of them
one has I = FD(d′) (e.g., in the case of the analytic lattice homology of an irreducible Gorenstein
curve singularity we have F(c) = F(c−1) = C ◁O — for notations see section 7; or, see the case of
the analytic lattice homology of weighted homogeneous surface singularities with rational homology
sphere link in Remark 8.5.5), hence, a priori, all the symmetrization procedure might depend on the
different choices. Moreover, it is also possible that I can be realized by completely different sets
of valuations (and lattice points), see, e.g., the situation discussed in subsection 9.4. The previous
symmetric version of the Independence Theorem 5.2.3 claims exactly the fact that, using the above
construction, all the different CDP realizations yield the very same lattice homology module.
On the other hand, once a realization (D,d) of I is found, we can naturally construct CDP realiza-
tions from it, similarly to the case of modules. Indeed, the following specialization of Lemma 4.6.1 is
true:
Lemma 5.3.2. Let O and I be as above. Assume that the pair (D,d) is a realization of I , where
D = {(vv,vM
v )}r
v=1 is a collection of discrete valuations on O and d ∈(Z≥0)r is a lattice point. Then
the following facts hold:
(1) the multiset D♯:= {v1,v2,...,vr,vr+1 = v1,,...,v2r = vr} of 2r discrete valuations and the
lattice point d♯:= (d,d) ∈(Z≥0)2r give a CDP realization of N;
(2) The collection D♮:= {2vv}r
v=1 of ‘doubled’ discrete valuations and the ‘doubled’ lattice point
d♮= 2d ∈(Z≥0)r give a CDP realization of N.
As a consequence of this discussion, we can introduce the well-defined symmetric lattice homology
of any finite codimensional integrally closed ideal.
Definition 5.3.3. Let k be a field, O a Noetherian k-algebra and I ◁O a finite k-codimensional inte-
grally closed ideal. By Corollary 5.1.6, it admits a realization, even more, by Lemma 5.3.2 it admits
a CDP realization (D,d). Then we denote the lattice homology module SH∗(O,D,d) associated with
this setup by SH∗(I ◁O), and call it ‘the symmetric lattice homology of the (integrally closed) ideal
I ’. According to the Independence Theorem 5.2.3, it is independent of the CDP realization chosen.
Clearly, by Remark 5.2.4, the symmetric lattice homology SH∗(I ◁O) agrees with H∗(I ,→O O).
Even more, as an analogue of Theorems 4.4.9 and 4.9.9 and Proposition 4.9.4, the symmetric lattice
homology of integrally closed ideals satisfy the following:

5.4.6. Monomial valuations. Let us assign some positive integer weights {w1,...,wm} to the vari-
ables {x1,...,xm} and consider the corresponding direct sum decomposition of Om (i.e., the homo-
geneous monomials xp1
1 · ... · xpm
m of degree d are those for which p1w1 + ... pmwm = d). Then the
associated valuation is called a monomial valuation, and it can, clearly, only realize monomial ideals.
On the other hand, these valuations are enough to characterize the integrally closed monomial ideals
(using their Newton diagrams). We will discuss these valuations and ideals in section 9.
5.4.7. Divisorial valuations. Let (X,o) be a normal complex analytic singularity of dimension ≥2.
Fix a resolution φ : eX →X and let E be an irreducible exceptional divisor in eX. Then E defines a
valuation on the local ring OX,o by the assignment f 7→vE(f) := {vanishing order of f ◦φ along E}.
It is called the divisorial valuation associated with E. If φ(E) = o then vE is called mX,o-centered.
In particular, if D = {Ev}v is a set of irreducible exceptional divisors, and ℓ:= ∑v ℓvEv is a divisor
supported on them (ℓv ∈Z for all v), then FD(ℓ) = { f ∈OX,o : vEv(f) ≥ℓv for all v} = φ∗OeX(−ℓ)o
is an integrally closed ideal in OX,o and codimC(FD(ℓ) ,→OX,o) < ∞. For more details see section 8,
especially Remark 8.1.12.
If dim(X,o) = 2 then, by a theorem of Lipman [74], any integrally closed ideal of OX,o can be
realized in this way for a certain choice of the resolution φ and the divisor ℓ.
5.4.8. Valuations associated with reduced curves and embedded Weil divisors.
If (C,o) is an
irreducible complex analytic curve singularity, then its normalization nC : Spec(C{t}) = (C,o) →(C,o)
induces a valuation of OC,o by f 7→vnC(f) := ordt(f ◦nC). This is the main ingredient for the definition
of the analytic lattice homology of curve singularities. For more see section 7.
Now assume that (C,o) ⊂(X,o) is an irreducible Weil divisor on the normal surface singularity
(X,o), and let φ : eX →X be an embedded resolution of the pair (C,o) ⊂(X,o). Let (eC, eo) denote
the strict transform of (C,o) and let Eφ,C be the irreducible exceptional curve which intersects eC. To
such a pair we can associate two natural discrete valuations of OX,o. One of them is the composition
vnC,C : OX,o

the divisorial valuation associated with Eφ,C. One has vnC,C ≥vEφ,C, but in general they are not equal

[The valuation vnC,C of OX,o is also called the arc-valuation associated with (C,o). For some related

5.4.9. Valuations associated with decorated irreducible Weil divisors. Consider a pair of set-germs
(C,o) ⊂(X,o) as in the previous paragraph 5.4.8 and let φ : eX →X be the minimal good embedded
resolution of the pair. Let P denote the intersection point of the strict transform eC with its supporting


## Page 43


43
exceptional curve. Then for any integer n ∈Z≥0 — the decoration of (C,o) — we blow up the intersec-
tion point of the strict transform of C with its supporting exceptional curve repeatedly n times (that is,
we blow up n times the corresponding infinitely near point P). Let E(n,C) be the exceptional curve in
the final resolution supporting the last strict transform. Then the divisorial valuation vE(n,C) : OX,o →Z
is called the valuation associated with the decorated curve {(C,o),n}. [Ideals of type FvE(n,C)(ℓ) as-
sociated with such a valuation were considered, e.g., in the deformation theory of sandwiched surface
singularities, cf. [33]. Note, however, that we use a different convention for the decorations.] One
sees that vE(n+1,C) ≥vE(n,C) for every n ∈Z≥0. Moreover, for any fixed function f ∈OX,o the limit
limn→∞vE(n,C)(f) = vnC,C(f). For the limiting properties of the corresponding filtration see [92].
For example, if (C,o) = ({x2 +y3 = 0},0) ⊂(X,o) = (C2,0), and n = 2, then FvE(2,C)(8) is the ideal
(y4,y3x,y3 +x2), while for n = 4 we have FvE(4,C)(10) = (y5,y4x,y3x2,y3 +x2).
By Corollary 5.1.6, ideals of type FvE(n,C)(ℓ) are integrally closed, however, if we just look at their
generators, e.g., for (y4,y3x,y3 +x2) (without knowing its realization as above), this property is not im-
mediate at all (compare with [118, Example 1.3.3]). In this way one can construct interesting nontrivial
(e.g., non-monomial) integrally closed ideals.
In fact, we can also present another realization of some of these ideals. Consider again the valuation
vE(n,c) as above and let m = min{vE(n,C)(f ◦φ) : f ∈IC,o}, where IC,o denotes the local ideal of the
curve germ (C,o) ⊂(X,o). Now clearly, if ℓ≤m, then the ideal IC,o is contained in FvE(n,C)(ℓ). Hence
such ideals {FvE(n,C)(ℓ)}ℓ≤m are pullbacks of ideals of OC,o via the quotient map q : OX,o →OC,o. For
examples above, with (C,o) = {x2 + y3 = 0} ⊂(X,o) = (C2,0), we have FvE(2,C)(8) = q−1(Fvν(8))

equations of (C,o) have even higher values.) In fact, any ideal FvF(ℓ) ⊂OX,o with ℓ∈Z≥0 is exactly
the monomial ideal generated by {(a,b) ∈σ ∩N2 |4a+b ≥ℓ} (where (4,1) is the normal vector of the
Newton diagram of (C,o)).
Let us now blow up the intersection point of F with the strict transform once, in this way we get the
resolution associated with the decorated curve {(C,o),1}. Let the new irreducible exceptional curve be
denoted by E. Then the divisorial valuation associated with E in this new resolution gives vE(x) = 4,
vE(y) = 5, vE(z) = 11, vE(w) = 28, hence vE(x4) = vE(zy) = 16, however vE(zy−x4) = 17. Thus, the
ideal I := FvE(17) ,→OX,o is a non-monomial integrally closed ideal. It is generated by monomials
associated with lattice points {(a,b) ∈σ ∩N2 |4a+b ≥17} and the additional equation zy−x4. (This
ideal depends on (C,o) because of the choice of the last blow up: the strict transform of (C,o) is
supported on E). The ideal FvE(17) contains IC,o.

6. SYMMETRIC LATTICE HOMOLOGY OF ‘INTEGRALLY REDUCED’ ARTIN k-ALGEBRAS
In this section we will present some properties of those Artin algebras, which arise as quotients of
Noetherian algebras by finite codimensional integrally closed ideals. The reason behind this inquiry
is part (b) of Theorem 5.3.4, which states that the symmetric lattice homology SH∗(I ◁O) of an
integrally closed ideal I is in fact an invariant of the Artin algebra O/I . We will discuss some char-
acterizations in different settings and provide a Künneth-type formula for computing their symmetric
lattice homology with respect to their classical product decomposition.
6.1. ‘Integrally reduced’ Noetherian k-algebras.
In this subsection we will examine the properties of k-algebras A which can be realized as O/I ,
where O is a Noetherian ring with a k-algebra structure and I = I is an integrally closed ideal
in O of finite codimension. Recall also that, by part (2) of Proposition 4.8.2, the Artin k-algebra
O/AnnO(M/N) of Theorem 4.4.9 (c) is also of this type, so, in fact, all the lattice homology theory of
realizable submodules concerns the representation theory of such algebras.
Definition 6.1.1. We call a Noetherian k-algebra A ‘integrally reduced’ if there exists a presentation
A ∼= O/I with O a Noetherian k-algebra and I ◁O an integrally closed ideal.


## Page 46


46
In such a case, since O is finitely generated, there exists a surjective morphism α : Om →O for
some m ∈N, where Om denotes the polynomial ring with k-coefficients k[x1,...,xm] (in the com-
plete / complex analytic case this could be k[[x1,...,xm]] or C{x1,...,xm}). Since J := α−1(I )
is integrally closed in Om as well, the Artin algebra A can also be represented as Om/J , where
J = J ◁Om.
On the other hand, clearly not any k-algebra is integrally reduced. E.g., in the complex analytic
setting we have the following (valuative) characterization.
Proposition 6.1.2. (see e.g., [73, 2.1 Théorème] or [120])
Assume A is a complex analytic algebra. Then
(i) A is of integrally reduced if and only if the following fact holds:
(6.1.3)
if a ∈A and γ(a) = 0 for any homomorphism γ : A →C{t}/(tm), then a = 0.
(ii) Moreover, if A satisfies this valuative criterion (6.1.3), and it is written as Om/I for some I ,
then I is automatically integrally closed.
Note that part (ii) does not imply that for any realization of A as O/I (with O not necessarily
of type Om) the ideal I is integrally closed. E.g., even if A satisfies (6.1.3), its zero ideal I = (0)
is not necessarily integrally closed (e.g., consider A = C{t}/(t2)). Hence property (ii) fails for the
realization A = A/(0). [With the slightly more general and somewhat combinatorial definition of
‘quasi-valuations’ and ‘quasi-realizability’, this problem can be remedied, see subsection 14, espe-
cially Remark 14.3.14.]
Nevertheless, this property (ii) is true also in the general algebraic case. With the aim of proving this
statement we introduce the notion of the ‘integral reduction’ of an algebra.
6.1.4. Integral reduction algebra. Given any finitely generated k-algebra A, by choosing a generating
set of m elements, we can present it as A = Om/I . By taking the integral closure I of I in O, we
can naturally consider the ‘integral reduction algebra’ \
Om/I = Om/I . This construction, a priori,
depends on the presentation of A as Om/I , however, we show that, in fact, it is independent of that.
Lemma 6.1.5. Let φ : R →O be a split epimorphism of rings and let I ◁O be an ideal. Then the
integral closedness of φ −1(I ) implies the same property for I .
Proof. Let us denote the splitting morphism by ι : O →R (i.e., φ ◦ι = idO).
We have to prove the containment I ⊂I . Consider some f ∈I , then, by definition,
f n +a1 · f n−1 +a2 · f n−2 +...+an = 0 for some ai ∈I i.
Using the splitting, we have ι(f)n + ι(a1) · ι(f)n−1 + ... + ι(an) = 0 with ι(ai) ∈φ −1(I )i, i.e., by
definition ι(f) ∈φ −1(I ) = φ −1(I ). Hence, f = φ(ι(f)) ∈I , and thus I = I .
□

In the case of a complex analytic algebra A, following Proposition 6.1.2 (i), we can give an intrinsic
definition of the integral reduction algebra bA as follows:
Proposition 6.1.9. Let A be a complex analytic algebra. Then its integral reduction is
bA = A/{a ∈A : for all γ : A →C{t}/(tm) we have γ(a) = 0}.
The possibility of a similar intrinsic formulation in the general case in the realm of quasi-valuations
and quasi-realizability will be discussed around Question 14.3.10.
We can now prove the following generalization of part (ii) of Proposition 6.1.2 to the general alge-
braic or completed setting:
Corollary 6.1.10. The property of a Noetherian k-algebra A that it is of form Om/I is well-defined,
i.e., if A ∼= Om/I with I = I , then for any presentation A ∼= Om′/I ′ we have I ′ = I ′. Thus, given
an integrally reduced Noetherian k-algebra, in any presentation of it with generators and relations, the
ideal of relations is inherently integrally closed.
[Therefore, if A ∼= Om/I with I ̸= I , then A is not integrally reduced.]
We will need the following folklore statement (the proof is taken from the answer of user325968 to
the question https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/1905186):


## Page 48


48
Lemma 6.1.11. Let R be a Noetherian ring and I ◁R an ideal in it. Then R/I ∼= R as rings if and
only if I = (0).
Proof. The sufficiency is clear. For the necessity part let us assume that we have a concrete ring
isomorphism φ : R/I →R and let us denote the natural quotient map by q : R →R/I . Now,
if we assume indirectly that I ̸= 0, then we necessarily have I1 := (q ◦φ)−1(I ) ⊋I . Similarly,
I2 := (q◦φ)−1(I1) ⊋I1 and by repeating this procedure we obtain a strictly increasing sequence of
ideals in R. This contradicts the Noetherian property.
□
Proof of Corollary 6.1.10. In this setting Proposition 6.1.6 states that
A ∼= Om/I ∼= ˆA ∼= Om′/I ′ ∼= (Om′/I ′)/(I ′/I ′) ∼= A/(I ′/I ′)
which, by Lemma 6.1.11, implies the integral closedness of I ′.
□
6.2. Direct product decomposition in the Artinian case.
By the structure theorem for Artinian rings (see, e.g., [12, Theorem 8.7]) any Artinian ring A can be
written in a unique way (up to isomorphism) as a direct product of Artinian local rings ∏i Ai. We want
to verify this statement in the category of integrally reduced Artin k-algebras. Clearly, in the complex
analytic case A satisfies (6.1.3) if and only if each factor Ai satisfies it. In general we have the following:

6.3. Lattice homology of integrally reduced Artin k-algebras.
By unpacking part (b) of Theorem 5.3.4 in this setting, we get the following:
Theorem 6.3.1. Assume that A is an integrally reduced Artin k-algebra, i.e., it can be realized as
Om/I , where I = I . Then the lattice homology SH∗(I ◁Om) is independent of the choice of the
realization Om/I of A, it depends merely on the isomorphism type of the k-algebra A.
It will be denoted by SH∗(A). By part (a) of Theorem 5.3.4, eu(SH∗(A)) = dimk(A). In other words,
the matching A ⇝SH∗(A) is the categorification of the dimension (of such type of Artin algebras).
If k is an algebraically closed field and A is local, then the weight-grading of the reduced symmetric
lattice homology is nonnegative, i.e., SHred,q,−2n(A) = 0 for all q ≥0 and n > 0. Moreover, if A is
nonzero, then so is SHred,0,0.
Question 6.3.2. What information does the lattice homology of integrally reduced Artin k-algebras
contain? Which invariants of the Artin k-algebra can we read off from its symmetric lattice homology?

Let us recall, that any integrally reduced Artin k-algebra is the direct product of finitely many in-

symmetric lattice homology modules are related by a Künneth-type formula. Nevertheless, before
presenting this result we have to introduce the chain complex description of lattice homology.
6.4. The chain complex description of lattice homology.
In this subsection we present an equivalent definition of the lattice homology modules H∗(Rr,w)

in the next subsection, in the proof of the Künneth formula.

6.5. Künneth formula.
Consider two integrally reduced Artin k-algebras of form A′ = O′/I ′ and A′′ = O′′/I ′′, where the
corresponding ideals are finite codimensional and integrally closed. Then the direct product algebra
A := A′ × A′′ is also integrally reduced, hence it admits a symmetric lattice homology module. Even
more, we can relate it to the lattice homology modules of its summands via Künneth-type formulas.
We will use the chain complex description of lattice homology presented in subsection 6.4 above.
For notational clarity, we adopt the convention to denote the objects associated with the algebras A′, A′′
and A′ ×A′′ by the superscripts ′, ′′ and ×. Then we have the following statement:
Proposition 6.5.1. Let A′ ∼= O′/I ′ and A′′ ∼= O′′/I ′′ be integrally reduced Artin k-algebras. If

Now, starting from some presentations A′ ∼= O′/I ′ and A′′ ∼= O′′/I ′′ we can choose the product
presentation for A′ × A′′ ∼= (O′ × O′′)/(I ′ × I ′′) (see also Proposition 6.2.1). Also, by Proposition
6.5.1 for any given CDP realizations D′ of I ′ in O′ and D′′ of I ′′ in O′′ we can choose the CDP
realization D× of I ′ ×I ′′ in O′ ×O′′ to get the isomorphism (6.5.2). Then we can use the algebraic
Künneth formula for the (a) PID case and the previous exact sequence for the (b) case.
□

7. ANALYTIC LATTICE HOMOLOGY OF REDUCED CURVE SINGULARITIES
In this section we will present the first example of categorification via lattice homology of a nu-
merical invariant defined as the codimension of a realizable submodule: the delta invariant of reduced
complex curve singularities. Our construction is equivalent with the analytic lattice (co)homology de-
fined by T. Ágoston and the first author in [5], however, the presentation from the point of view of
lattice homology of realizable modules is new.
In the first part of this section we review some needed facts about the theory of complex analytic
reduced curve singularities, then we present the categorification of the delta invariant along the lines of
the general construction and theory from section 4. For this we need to consider Rosenlicht’s regular
differential forms and a filtered version of the classical Rosenlicht–Serre duality. Finally, we show that
this new construction agrees with the analytic lattice (co)homology of [5] and present some examples.
7.1. Categorification of the δ invariant (as an application of the main construction).
Let (C,o) be a reduced complex curve singularity with local C-algebra O = OC,o. Let ∪v∈V (Cv,o)
be the irreducible decomposition of (C,o) (with V = {1,...,r}) and denote the local C-algebra of
(Cv,o) by Ov. We denote the integral closure of Ov by Ov = C{tv}, and we consider Ov as a subring
of Ov. Similarly, we denote the integral closure of O by O = ⊕vC{tv}. The delta invariant of (C,o) is
defined as δ = δ(C,o) = dimC O/O.
Our objective is to establish a categorification of δ(C,o) using the construction of lattice homology
of realizable submodules from subsection 4.4. Therefore we need a finitely generated module and a
realizable submodule of it with codimension δ. A result of Serre will provide these in the realm of
differential forms. In order to present it, we first have to introduce some notations.
The inclusion O ,→O corresponds to the normalization map nC : (C,o) →(C,o), where (C,o) is a
multigerm comprised of r smooth germs. Let Ω1(∗) denote the module of germs of meromorphic dif-
ferential 1-forms on the normalization (C,0) with possible poles (of any order) at most in the preimage

7.2.5. In order to present further properties of this construction, we need the notion of the conductor
element. Let C = (O : O) denote the conductor ideal of O, i.e., the largest ideal of O which is an ideal
of O as well. It has the form (tc1

Remark 7.2.6. For a plane curve singularity germ (C,0) ⊂(C2,0) the components of the conductor
are of form cv = c(Cv)+∑w̸=v i0(Cw,Cv) for all v ∈V , where c(Cv) is the conductor of the component
(Cv,0) and i0( , ) denotes the intersection multiplicity at 0 ∈C2. Furthermore, c(Cv) equals the Milnor
number of the branch (Cv,0). For a formula of cv in the general case see [27].
From the definitions, it is clear that h•(ℓ) = 0 for ℓ≥c (since F(ℓ) ⊂C ⊂O for such ℓ— in fact,
for ℓ= c we have F(c) = F(c) = C ).
Theorem 7.2.7. [5] (a) For any d ≥c the natural inclusion Sn ∩R(0,d) ,→Sn is a homotopy equi-
valence.
(b) One has a bigraded Z[U]-module isomorphism Han,∗(C,o) ∼= H∗(R(0,d),wan) for any d ≥c
induced by the above inclusion map. Therefore, Han,∗(C,o) is determined by the weighted cubes of the
finite rectangle R(0,c).
(c) eu(Han,∗(C,o)) = δ(C,o), that is, Han,∗(C,o) is a ‘categorification’ of δ(C,o).
Remark 7.2.8. (a) Ágoston and the first author proved in [5] that a flat deformation {(Ct,o)}t∈(C,0) of
curve singularities induces a graded Z[U]-module morphism Han,0(Ct̸=0,o) →Han,0(Ct=0,o). More-
over, the Functoriality Conjecture states that this map can be extended to the full lattice homology
Z[U]-module.
(b) The minimal value mwan,0 of the analytic weight function is a ‘new and mysterious’ invariant of
the reduced curve singularity (C,o). By work of Hof and the first author this invariant is the main
ingredient of the characterization of the Cohen-Macauley type of the germ [53].
(c) By previous work of the authors together with Kubasch [67, Theorem 4.2.1], the analytic lattice
homology of reduced curve singularities detects the Gorenstein property. In fact, the Gorenstein prop-
erty corresponds to the weight function being symmteric with respect the conductor element c which
property can also be read off from the ranks of the graded pieces of Han,0(C,o). For more on the
Gorenstein case see subsection 7.4.
(d) In the case of a plane curve singularity, h is computable from the partial multivariable Alexander
polynomials [44, 3.3–3.4], while these Alexander polynomials from the embedded resolution graph of
(C,0) ⊂(C2,0) [38]. In particular, the Hilbert function h, and, hence, the weight function wan,0 is an
embedded topological invariant of (C,0) ⊂(C2,0). In fact, one can read off the multiplicity m(C,0)
from the graded Z[U]-module structure of Han,0(C,0) [67, Theorem 5.2.5]. Moreover, by work of
Kubasch and the second author, for irreducible plane curve singularities the analytic lattice homology
Han(C,0) = Han,0(C,0) is a complete embedded topological invariant [68, Theorem 3.1.1].


## Page 56

(e) The first author introduced in [89] an increasing subspace filtration on the Sn spaces, which
further refines the analytic lattice homology Han(C,o). In fact, the corresponding spectral sequence
encodes rich geometric information about the singularity: it is a complete topological invariant for any
(not necessarily irreducible) plane curve singularity, it recovers the motivic Poincaré series, while the
elements of the first page can be associated with the Heegaard Floer Link homology of the link.

7.4. Gorenstein case.
The original definition of Grothendieck says that a quotient of a regular local ring is Gorenstein if
it is Cohen–Macaulay and its (canonical) dualizing module is free of rank one (see the first written
reference in [113, Subsection II.6]). In the case of a reduced complex analytic curve singularity (C,o)
this means that the O-module ωR
C of Rosenlicht’s regular differential forms is free of rank one, that is,
there exists a ‘Gorenstein form’ αGor ∈ωR
C such that ωR
C = αGorO. Moreover, by work of Rosenlicht
[110] and Serre [114], (C,o) is Gorenstein if and only if dim(O/O) = dim(O/C ) (equivalently, using
our previous notations from subsection 7.2, δ = h(c) or |c| = 2δ). Thus αGorC = Ω1
C and, using the
perfect pairing from (7.1.1), we get a perfect pairing

However, we warn the reader that if the reduced curve singularity (C,o) is not Gorenstein then
usually Han,∗(C,o) ̸= SH∗(C ◁OC,o). For example, take the numerical semigroup S = ⟨4,5,≥8⟩⊂N
and let (C,o) be the singularity of Spec(C[S ]). Then the conductor of (C,o) is 8, the delta invariant is
δ = 5 and codim(C ,→O) = 3. We invite the reader to determine both Han,0(C,o) and SH0(C ,→O):
they are not isomorphic (the first one has Euler characteristic δ = 5, the other one 3). For another
example see Example 7.5.12.
In fact, A. A. Kubasch and the authors proved that the Gorenstein property can be read from the rank
of the kernel of the U-action in bigrading (0,0) [67, Theorem 4.2.1] (see also Remark 7.2.8 (c)).


## Page 58

Remark 7.4.3. Grothendieck’s definition of the Gorenstein property was generalized to all local rings,
and thereby all rings, by Bass [13] (see also [54]), which gives today’s standard commutative algebra
formalism.
7.5. Computational tools and examples.
7.5.1. Semigroup of values. In order to compute the analytic lattice homology of reduced curve singu-
larities one is required to obtain the values of the Hilbert function. As this is a very important classical
invariant, it has well-understood connections to other invariants which can offer paths towards its com-
putation. For example, the Hilbert function h contains equivalent information with the semigroup of
values of the reduced curve singularity (C,o), defined as
SC,o =
 v1(g),...,vr(g)

∈(Z≥0)r : g ∈O is not a zero-divisor
	
.
In general it is a hard problem to classify those numerical semigroups S ⊂(Z≥0)r which can be
realized as semigroup of values of curve singularities. However, there is a family of semigroups, defined
by certain combinatorial properties (listed, e.g, in subsection 2.8 of [67]), called good semigroups of
(Z≥0)r, which contains all the semigroups of values of curve germs. For more see [26, 31, 32, 43]. The
connection of the Hilbert function with the semigroup of values is manifested as follows:

7.5.4. The Alexander polynomial and the Poincaré series. Another path to finding the Hilbert func-
tion could be using the following general formula, which will be our typical approach for plane curve
singularities. This is really useful in the case r ≥2, when no other algorithms are known (in the r = 1
case the semigroup SC,0 is usually known).
The embedded topological type of a plane curve germ can be completely characterized by several
invariants, e.g., by its embedded resolution graph, or its splice diagram, or by the classical multivariable
Alexander polynomial ∆(t1,...,tr). For their description and properties see e.g., [15]. For concrete
formula for ∆(t) in terms of the splice diagram see [38].
In the next formula, which determines the Hilbert series
HC,0(t) = HC,0(t1,...,tr) = ∑
ℓ∈Nr
h(ℓ)t1ℓ1 ···trℓr
of an r-component plane curve (C,0) ⊂(C2,0), it is convenient to replace the Alexander polynomial
by the so-called Poincaré series. Namely, we set P(t) := ∆(t)/(1 −t) if r = 1 and P(t) := ∆(t) for
r > 1. (For more see, e.g., [19, 20].) Moreover, we can consider these invariants for any subgerms
(CJ,0) = ∪j∈J(Cj,0), where J ⊆{1,...,r}.
Theorem 7.5.5 ([44, Theorem 3.4.3]; see also [81, Corollary 4.3]). With the above notations,

[In fact, this formula is also true for not necessarily plane curves as well, however, in the general case
the Poincaré series does not have such a geometric description.]
In particular, the Hilbert function of a reduced plane curve germ is an invariant of the embedded
topological type, and hence the weight function wan,0 is as well. For completeness, we note for plane
germs the multivariable Alexander polynomial is already a complete invariant for the embedded topo-
logical type (cf. [126]); in particular, HC,0(t) can be determined merely by ∆C,0(t), although this
correspondence does not provide an explicit formula.

8. CATEGORIFICATIONS OF ANALYTIC INVARIANTS OF ISOLATED SINGULARITIES OF dim ≥2
In this section we will present further examples of categorification via lattice homology of an in-
variant defined as the codimension of a realizable submodule: the geometric genus pg and irregularity
q of normal isolated complex analytic singularities of dim ≥2 and the plurigenera of normal sur-
face singularities. Our construction in the case of pg is slightly more general than the analytic lattice
(co)homology defined by T. Ágoston and the first author in [2, 4] (where certain restrictions were im-
posed), the present case is well-defined for any normal isolated singularity. On the other hand, the
categorification of the irregularity is a new construction, whereas the case of plurigenera here is treated
differently (with different output) than the authors’ first attempt in [90].
8.1. Geometric genus and irregularity of normal isolated singularities.
Let (X,o) be a complex analytic normal isolated singularity of dimension n ≥2. By a resolution of
(X,o) we mean the following: fix a sufficiently small Stein representative X of (X,o) and let φ : eX →X
be a holomorphic map, such that
• eX is smooth,
• φ is an analytic isomorphism over the regular part U := X \{o} and
• E := φ −1(o)red is a divisor, with each irreducible component of dimension n −1 (when con-
sidered with reduced structure we call it the exceptional divisor).


## Page 63


63
Similarly to the surface case, we call a resolution good, if E is a simple normal crossing divisor,
and minimal (good) if any (good) resolution dominates it. Good resolutions always exist (at least, in
characteristic 0), indeed they correspond to the ‘strong resolutions’ of Kollár (cf. [63], where he also
provides a proof of this fact).

It counts the adjunction conditions imposed by the singularity, cf. [79]. Moreover, the holomorphic

Remark 8.1.4. A normal surface singularity is called rational if pg = 0. This analytic property can be
characterized topologically as well, it only depends on the resolution graph Γφ, which in this case is
called rational, see [10, 11, 70] or Remark 8.4.8 (b).
If (X,o) is a normal surface singularity, then q ≤pg −h1(OE), with equality if (X,o) is weighted
homogeneous (cf. [124, 116], see also subsection 8.5 below). In particular, q = 0 for all rational
singularities.

8.1.15. Nonpositivity Theorem. From the local sheaf theoretical description of the algebra and mod-
ules used in the definition of the analytic lattice homology of p-forms we see that the assumptions of
the Nonpositivity Theorem 4.9.9 are satisfied with field k = C and local k-algebra OX,o. Hence we have
the following statement:
Corollary 8.1.16. Let (X,o) be a complex analytic normal isolated singularity of dimension ≥2. Then
for both p ∈{dimX −1,dimX} and for every positive integer n > 0 the corresponding Sn space is con-
tractible, hence the weight-grading of the reduced analytic lattice homology of p-forms is nonnegative,
i.e., Hred,q,−2n((X,o);Ωp) = 0 for all q ≥0 and n > 0.
Moreover, if s(p) ̸= 0 (with p ∈{dimX −1,dimX}), then the upper bound is sharp: S0 is not connected.
8.2. Comparison with the analytic lattice homology after [2, 4, 87].
In the case of p = n, Ágoston and the first author defined a categorification of the geometric genus
via the so-called analytic lattice homology Han,∗(X,o) of the isolated singularity (X,o) of dimension
n ≥2 [4] (see also [2, 87] for the normal surface singularity case). In this subsection we introduce this
invariant and compare it with the construction of the previous subsection 8.1. It turns out, that whenever
the analytic lattice homology in the sense of [2, 4, 87] exists (i.e., it is well-defined and categorifies pg),
then the two theories agree. Therefore, H∗

8.3. The cohomological cycle and the Gorenstein property.
Recall that in subsection 4.7 (in the general setup of a CDP realization D of some finite codimen-
sional submodule N ≤M) we introduced the notation dD for the unique smallest lattice point, such that
the lattice homology was already realized in the finite rectangle R(0,dD) (i.e., the smallest one satis-
fying F M
D (−dD) = M). [In the case of reduced curve singularities this lattice point was the conductor
element c, see Corollary 7.3.4.]
We wish to identify a concrete cycle which has this property in the case of the analytic lattice ho-
mology of n-forms associated with the normal isolated singularity (X,o) of dimension n ≥2 computed
via the realization given in the proof of Corollary 8.1.5. More precisely, we fix a resolution φ : eX →X
and use the (CDP) realization D = {(vv,vM
v )}v∈V of H0(eX,Ωn

eX) via the extended di-

exceptional divisor E. It turns out that in this setting dD is the cohomological cycle Zcoh ∈(Z≥0)r of the
resolution φ. We recall its definition briefly (for more see, e.g., [109, 57, 4, 87]). If pg = 0 then we set
Zcoh = 0. If pg > 0 then there exists a unique minimal cycle Zcoh > 0 such that hn−1(OeX) = hn−1(OZcoh).
It has the property that for any ℓ̸≥Zcoh one has hn−1(Oℓ) < hn−1(OeX). Then clearly, by the identifica-
tions (8.2.5) and (8.2.4), Zcoh corresponds to dD (it satisfies the conditions imposed in subsection 4.7).

Proposition 8.3.6. For any resolution φ : eX →X set C = φ∗(OeX(−ZK))o ⊂OX,o. Then C is indepen-

Proof. Consider a resolution φ : eX →X and another modification of it ψ : eX′ →eX. Then ψ∗(Ωn

Since any two resolutions can be dominated by a third one [51, 63], this shows that φ∗OeX(−ZK(eX)) is
independent of the resolution.
In the case of surface singularities we can argue in the following elementary way as well. Let
φ : eX →X be a resolution as above, and let ψ : eX′ →eX be a blow up of eX at a point of the exceptional
curve of φ. Let Enew be the exceptional curve of ψ. Then by an elementary computation (see, e.g., [87,
Example 6.3.3 (4)]) ZK(eX′) = ψ∗(ZK(eX))−Enew. Hence, for any function germ g ∈OX,o the inequality
φ ∗(g) ≥ZK(eX) holds exactly when the counterpart ψ∗φ ∗(g) ≥ZK(eX′) holds.
□

Corollary 8.3.7. Assume that (X,o) is a Gorenstein normal isolated singularity of dimension n ≥2.
Let C ⊂OX,o be its the conductor ideal (cf. Definition 8.3.5). Then
SH∗(OX,o/C ) ∼= H∗((X,o);Ωn) (∼= Han,∗(X,o) whenever this latter is well-defined).
8.4. Reduction theorems.
Let us recall that the lattice homology is determined by certain cubical subspaces {Sn}n embedded
in some rectangles of Zr ⊗R. In our present context r, the rank of the lattice Zr, is the number of
valuations we use to ‘realize’ the submodule N in M.
There is a great interest in reducing the rank r of the lattice, if possible. Here are some reasons.
(1) Even theoretically, but definitely in concrete computations, it is hard to identify the filtrations
F(ℓ) and F M(ℓ), whenever ℓruns over a higher-rank lattice. E.g., in the present case (of analytic
lattice cohomology of isolated singularities of dimension n ≥2) already for the first filtration we have
to understand the vanishing orders of all the functions along all the irreducible exceptional divisors. It
would be highly desirable to reduce this set to some geometrically defined ‘essential’ set of divisors
only.
(2) By Proposition 4.9.4 we have Hq(N ,→M) = 0 for q ≥r, hence reduction of r implies additional
vanishing. See also section 10 about the (lattice) homological dimension and its properties.
In the case of analytic lattice homology of p-forms associated with an isolated singularity (X,o) of
dimension n ≥2 a possible reduction can occur as follows.
Theorem 8.4.1 (Reduction Theorem). Let φ : eX →X be a good resolution with irreducible exceptional
divisors {Ev}v∈V . Consider also the C-algebra O, modules Mp and N p (cf. Notation 8.1.6) and the
set of extended discrete valuations Dφ = {(vv,vM
v )}v∈V as in (8.1.9) and (8.1.10). Recall that we have
N p = H0(eX,Ωp
eX) = F M

(b) Assume that n = 2. Though the equivalent conditions (8.4.5) and (8.4.7) look analytical, in this
case they can be reformulated in a topological language (hence they can be tested on the resolution
graph).
First, recall that a singularity is rational if pg = 0. By the Formal Function Theorem this happens if
and only if in any resolution with exceptional divisor E one has h1(OZ) = 0 for any Z > 0 supported
on E. By Artin [10, 11] this can be reformulated in terms of the resolution graph as well (namely:
−(Z,Z −ZK) > 0 for any effective cycle Z > 0). If a graph satisfies the corresponding combinatorial
condition we say that it is a rational graph (for a different combinatorial criterion see [70]).


## Page 72


72
Then condition (8.4.7) for n = 2 is equivalent with the fact that all the connected components of the
full subgraph supported by V ∗are rational. This topological criterion was already used in the literature:
by Definition 7.3.21 of [87], a subset V is a WR-set (‘weak reduction set’) if it satisfies exactly this
condition (i.e., by deleting all the vertices V and the adjacent edges we remain with a rational graph).
Hence, if p = n = 2 then the Reduction Theorem 8.4.1 holds for any WR-set V .
(c) Recall, that in the case of n = 2 any normal surface singularity satisfies q ≤pg. This means that
if V is a WR-set, i.e., V ∗supports only rational singularities, then condition (8.4.5) is valid for p = 1,
too. Hence the Reduction Theorem 8.4.1 holds for any WR-set V for n = 2 and p = 1 as well.
(d) In the n = 2 case a typical example for a WR-set is when we choose V as the set of nodes
(vertices with valency ≥3 or genus > 0). For example, if we consider the star shaped graph of the
minimal good resolution of a weighted homogeneous singularity, then the unique central vertex forms
a WR-set, hence ¯r = 1 and H≥1((X,o);Ωp) = 0. For a generalization of higher dimensional weighted
homogeneous singularities see [4, section 6].
Remark 8.4.10. Clearly, in the setting of the Reduction Theorem 8.4.1 for every subset V ⊂V satis-
fying condition (8.4.2) (for p ∈{n−1,n}), by Proposition 4.9.4, the cardinality

Remark 8.4.11. In the case of the analytic lattice homology of reduced curve singularities (presented
in section 7), there is no natural stimulus for reducing the rank of the underlying lattice. Indeed, its
generators correspond bijectively to irreducible components of the singularity. Nevertheless, in some
specific cases one can produce lower dimensional lattices with weight functions yielding the same
lattice homology module, e.g., in the Newton nondegenerate plane curve case. In fact, in this case
one can use monomial valuations coming from the convex geometry of the Newton polygon to obtain
‘minimal’ realizations of the conductor ideal (cf. Theorem 4.9.6). In a sense, one could consider this
result the corresponding Reduction Theorem in the n = 1 dimensional case.
8.5. Example: the weighted homogeneous case.
In this subsection we will follow through the construction of the analytic lattice homology of p-
forms in the more restricted case of Gorenstein weighted homogeneous normal surface singularities
(n = 2, p ∈{1,2}). We emphasize, however, that we impose neither any additional analytic– (being
hypersurface or complete intersection) nor topological restriction (regarding, e.g., the link of the sin-
gularity). We will compute the Hilbert functions h and h◦in terms of the coefficients of the Poincaré
series and reason that, similarly to Han,∗(X,o), the analytic lattice homology H∗((X,o);Ω1) of 1-forms
can often be nontrivial. Finally, we will provide a concrete example.
8.5.1. Weighted homogeneous singularities [95, 96]. An isolated singularity (X,0) ⊂(CN,0) is
called weighted homogeneous with a good C∗-action if it satisfies the following conditions. First, we
fix an action of C∗on the affine space CN with positive integer weights (w1,...,wN) by
t ·(x1,...,xN) := (tw1x1,...,twNxN).
As usual, we also assume that gcd{wi}i = 1. The singularity (X,0) is weighted homogeneous with
these weights, if the ideal of functions vanishing on X ⊂CN is generated by weighted homogeneous
polynomials. We assume that the origin is the only singular point of the affine variety X.


## Page 73


73
For such a singularity, the affine coordinate ring, and also the local algebra O = OX,0 is Z≥0
graded, i.e., O = ⊕ℓ≥0Rl, with R0 = C. We will denote the Poincaré series of this graded ring by
PX(t) := ∑ℓ≥0 dimC Rℓtℓ=: ∑ℓ≥0 dℓtℓ. In several cases (e.g., if X is a complete intersection or a surface
singularity with QHS3 link) PX(t) can be combinatorially determined, see, e.g., [87, paragraph 5.1.26
and Example 9.7.24].
8.5.2. Analytic lattice homology in the Gorenstein surface case. Let us now assume for simplicity
that the isolated weighted homogeneous normal surface singularity (X,0) is additionally Gorenstein
(however, several steps of the following argument can be extended to the non-Gorenstein and higher
dimensional cases as well). For some basic results about Gorenstein weighted homogeneous singulari-
ties see, e.g., [87, 124, 130]. We also assume that (X,o) is not rational (otherwise pg = q = 0).
Our goal is to determine the analytic lattice homologies of p-forms H∗((X,0);Ωp) in this setting.
We use the previous notations O = H0(eX,OeX), Mp = H0(eX \E,Ωp

the quotient singularities of the exceptional curve of the weighted blow up. In the case p = 2 several
particular examples of the analytic lattice homology after [2] are known (consult, e.g., [87]), in par-
ticular we know that the analytic lattice (co)homology of 2-forms is usually nontrivial. Our goal is to
show the same fact for p = 1 by connecting the modules M2 and M1 via the Euler vector field and by
producing concrete computations for both.
For the computation of the analytic lattice homology modules we use the Reduction Theorem 8.4.1:
by construction, the dual resolution graph is star-shaped (with all the legs rational), hence we need
only one valuation, namely the one associated with the ‘central’ irreducible exceptional divisor E0, cf.
Remark 8.4.8 (d). In fact, E0 is the base of the associated orbifold Seifert bundle, or the exceptional
curve of the weighted blow up. The point is that the direct sum decompositions of the local algebra
O and of the modules M1 and M2 provided by the C∗-action coincide with the graded vector spaces
associated with the E0-divisorial filtration (see, e.g., [87, Example 8.6.13]). More precisely, we have

8.6. Categorification of the L2-plurigenera of isolated singularities.
In the following subsections we will present the various versions of plurigenera of normal surface
singularities introduced by Watanabe [123], Knöller [61] and Morales [80], and show that they can be
computed as the codimension of some realizable submodules. As a corollary, we can construct lattice
homological categorifications of these invariants via the construction of subsection 4.4. The authors
already introduced a similar concept for Gorenstein normal surface singularities in [90], however, the
present version is more general and seems better suited to give interesting new invariants (see also part
(c) of Example 12.5.15).

Corollary 8.6.5. Let (X,o) be a complex analytic normal isolated singularity of dimension ≥2. Then

8.7. Categorification of the ∆m,n plurigenera of normal surface singularities.
In the normal surface singularity case (i.e., dim = n = 2) Knöller and Morales introduced two more
plurigenera, which can be generalized even further. They can all be computed as the codimension
of some realizable submodule, hence our general construction from subsection 4.4 can be applied to
obtain categorifications of these invariants as well. We will use the previous notations and the good
resolution φ : eX →X.
The plurigenera of a given normal surface singularity (X,o) are defined for each pair of integers
m ∈Z>0,n ∈Z∩[−m,0] as the (finite) dimension ∆m,n(X,o) of the quotient vector space:

• the m-th plurigenus (Knöller [61]): γm(X,o) = ∆m,−m;

if x is a singular point of E.
Thus, if we denote by L ′ := OeX′(m(KeX′ + E′) + nE′), then Φ∗(L ) = L ′(−kEnew) for some k ≥0.
The sheaf exact sequence 0 →Φ∗L →L ′ →OkEnew ⊗L ′ →0 induces the cohomological long exact
sequence

the other hand, H0(eX′,Φ∗(L )) ∼= H0(eX,L ) by the properties of the direct image functor, thus

as H0(eX′,OeX′) ∼= H0(eX,OeX)-modules and, clearly, this identification is compatible with that of the
numerators above.
Corollary 8.7.2. Let (X,o) be a normal surface singularity, and φ : eX →X a good resolution with
reduced exceptional divisor E. Then

is a well-defined invariant of the singularity and has Euler characteristic the plurigenus ∆m,n(X,o) for
every m ∈Z>0,n ∈Z ∩[−m,0]. It can be computed in some finite rectangle (depending on the CDP
realization chosen). We will denote it by H∗((X,o),∆m,n).
Proof. We already saw the independence of the quotient module of the resolution, whereas identity
(8.7.1) also shows independence of the Stein representative chosen (it can be lifted to a module iso-
morphism similarly to the case of identities (8.1.1) and (8.1.7)). The fact that the underlying algebra
actions are equivalent can be checked analogously to the case of identity (8.1.7). The realizability of the
submodule can be proved similarly as in the case of Corollary 8.6.2: by using the divisorial valuations
and their natural extensions (cf. (8.6.4)) translated corresponding to the value of n ∈Z≤0. Finally, the
Independence Theorem 4.3.1 and Theorem 4.4.9 yield the desired well-definedness and properties.
□
Relying on the local sheaf theoretic formulation we obtain the following version of the Nonpositivity
Theorem 4.9.9:

Corollary 8.7.3. Consider the normal surface singularity (X,o) and set m ∈Z>0 and n ∈Z∩[−m,0].
Then the weight-grading of Hred,∗((X,o),∆m,n) is nonnegative, i.e., Hred,q,−2n′((X,o),∆m,n) = 0 for all
q ≥0 and n′ > 0. In fact, even more is true: the corresponding Sn′-spaces are contractible for every
n′ > 0. Moreover, if ∆m,n(X,o) ̸= 0, the upper bound of non-contractibility is sharp: S0 is not even
connected.

obstructed by the fact that even rational singularities might have nontrivial higher plurigenera: see,

challenging in general.

era then the one given by the authors in [90]: we can already see in Example 8.7.6 that it deviates far

previous construction (cf. [90, Corollary 6.3.3 and Theorems 8.2.1 and 8.5.3], see also the discussion

surface singularity, independent of the resolution used for its computation and altogether seems to be a
finer invariant. Its connections with singularity deformations and geometric reinterpretations of δm,γm
and λm (cf., e.g., [97]) are yet to be explored.

9. MONOMIAL IDEALS AND NEWTON DIAGRAMS
In this section we will produce a combinatorial lattice homology construction for integral closures
and adjoints of monomial ideals in polynomial rings. We compare it to the analytic lattice homo-
logy of Newton nondegenerate hypersurface singularities with the same Newton diagram and read off
consequences regarding deformations and Rees valuations.

9.3. Hypersurface singularities with Newton nondegenerate principal part.
Given a convenient Newton diagram, we can associate to it a hypersurface singularity, nondegen-
erate in the sense of Kouchnirenko and Varchenko [65, 122], with well-defined embedded topological
type. We seek to compare its analytic lattice homology (of m −1 forms) with the combinatorial one
associated to the corresponding adjoint ideal introduced in the previous subsection. However, we begin
the presentation from the viewpoint of a given complex analytic hypersurface singularity.
Let f : (Cm,0) →(C,0), f(x) = ∑p apxp, ap ∈C (∀p ∈(Z>0)m), be a complex analytic hypersurface
germ. We define its support as S(f) := {p ∈(Z≥0)m : ap ̸= 0}. Then, similarly to subsection 9.1, we
can construct the Newton boundary NΓ and NΓ± associated to S(f); we denote them by NΓ(f) and
NΓ±(f). In the sequel, we will assume that NΓ(f) is convenient. We also write f0 := ∑p∈NΓ(f) apxp,
and we call it the Newton principal part of f.


## Page 85


85
In order to succesfully connect the combinatorics of NΓ(f) and the discrete invariants of the hyper-
surface singularity f, we need to impose a genericity condition on f: for any face F of NΓ(f) (of any
dimension 0 ≤q ≤m −1) we write fF(x) = ∑p∈F apxp and we assume that the equation ∂fF(x) = 0
has no solution in (C∗)m. If f satisfies this condition we say, after Kouchnirenko and Varchenko, that
it has a Newton nondegenerate principal part. Such a germ always defines an isolated singularity. We
also remark that this nondegeneracy condition is satisfied generically (i.e., for a generic choice of the
coefficients of f0). For details see [65, 122]. Moreover, Oka proved the following:
Theorem 9.3.1. [94, Theorem 2.1] Suppose that f : (Cm,0) →(C,0) is an isolated singularity and it
has Newton nondegenerate principal part. Then the Milnor fibration, and hence the embedded topo-
logical type of (X,0) := ({ f = 0},0) ⊂(Cm,0) is determined by the Newton boundary NΓ(f).
Similarly, the same is true for the delta invariant in the case m = 2 and for the geometric genus in the
case m > 2. The precise statement is provided by the following result of Merle and Teissier. In order
to formulate it, we recall that a hypersurface singularity is automatically Gorenstein (that is, it admits
a nowhere vanishing form ωG ∈H0(X \ {0},Ωm−1
X\{0})), in fact, its Gorenstein form (up to a nonzero
constant) is
ωG = dx2 ∧dx3 ∧...∧dxm

resolution of (X,0). If m > 2 then the divisor of the pullback of ωG by the resolution φ is −ZK
(compare with subsection 8.3).

9.4. Equivalence of the analytic and the combinatorial lattice homology.
Having a convenient Newton boundary NΓ, we face the following interesting situation: associated
with NΓ we have two naturally defined symmetric lattice homologies.


## Page 86


86
The first one, the ‘analytic one’, is constructed as follows. Take an isolated complex analytic hy-
persurface singularity f with nondegenerate principal part supported on NΓ and then consider its
analytic lattice homology Han,∗({ f = 0},0) (in the m −1 ≥2 dimensional case we denoted this by
H∗(({ f = 0},0),Ωm−1) in section 8, nevertheless, here we will use the Han,∗notation to stay compati-
ble with the m = 2 case from section 7). In the curve case it depends only on the embedded topological
type, hence only on the Newton boundary NΓ(f). In higher dimensions m > 2 a priori it is not clear
that a similar statement holds, in principle it might be possible that Han,∗({f = 0},0) depends on the
choice of the coefficients of the monomials of f (i.e., of the ‘analytic type’ of ({f = 0},0)).
On the other hand, we have a second module, constructed as in subsection 9.2. Namely, we consider
the monomial ideal M generated by monomials of type {xp : p ∈NΓ+}, and then associate to it the
symmetric lattice homology SH∗(adj(M )◁Om). By Corollary 9.2.11 it agrees with the combinatorial
lattice homology assigned to NΓ, and hence it can be computed combinatorially by counting specific
lattice points under the Newton boundary.
It turns out, however, that the two lattice homology modules are isomorphic, in particular showing
also that the output of the analytic construction depends only on Newton boundary NΓ. In fact this
isomorphism question was our original starting point and main motivation for one of the main results,
the Independence Theorem 5.2.3, of the article.
Theorem 9.4.1. Let f : (Cm,0) →(C,0) be an isolated hypersurface singularity with Newton nonde-
generate principal part. Then, its analytic lattice homology module (in the m > 2 case we can think of
the analytic lattice homology of (m−1)-forms as well) agrees with the combinatorial lattice homology
module assigned to its Newton boundary NΓ(f):
Han,∗({ f = 0},0) ∼= H∗(R(0,cΓ),wΓ).
Proof. Let us set the notation X := { f = 0} ⊂Cm. By Corollaries 7.4.2 (m = 2) and 8.3.7 (m > 2),
we have that Han,∗(X,0) ∼= SH∗(C ◁OX,0), which, by Theorem 6.3.1 (1), agrees with SH∗(Cf ◁OCm,0),
where Cf = q−1(C ) is the pullback of the conductor ideal via the quotient map q : OCm,0 →OX,0.
On the other hand, by Corollary 9.2.11 we have H∗(R(0,cΓ),wΓ) ∼= SH∗(adj(M ) ◁Om), where M is
the monomial ideal in OCm,0 generated by monomials of type {xp : p ∈NΓ+(f)}. Now, [79, 2.1.1
Théorème] of Merle and Teissier (presented here as Theorem 9.3.2) identifies C f and adj(M ), and,
hence, provides the proof.
□
As a corollary, we can answer the question [87, Problem 11.9.52], proposed by the first author:
Corollary 9.4.2. The analytic lattice homology (of (m −1)-forms) is constant along an equisingular
family of hypersurface singularities (of dimension m −1) with Newton nondegenerate principal part
associated with a fixed Newton boundary NΓ. The above Theorem 9.4.1 also provides a combinatorial
construction of this module.
9.5. Another approach for the plane curve singularity case.
For the identification of the ideals part of the proof of Theorem 9.4.1 we also present another, more
computation based, but elementary argument. The motivation to provide this chain of identifications at
the level of more classical invariants is that this alternative proof can be generalized to some Newton
degenerate cases as well, as we will see in Examples 9.6.3 and 9.6.5. Along these steps we also see
that the height functions han and hΓ associated with the analytic and combinatorial situations do not

agree (see Example 9.5.8). In particular, we face the situation that two weight functions (both naturally
assigned to a geometric picture) differ but their lattice homologies agree.

9.6. Some more examples.
The Independence Theorem 5.2.3 implies, that the only really important part for the proof of the
previous Corollary is the fact that the two set of discrete valuations realize the same ideal, that is, in
the terminology of Theorem 9.4.1: q−1(C ) = adj(M ). This can also happen in other, more general
situations, e.g., when the germ is not Newton nondegenerate, or when we compare two germs with
different Newton boundaries. We will give some examples below.
9.6.1. Example: different Newton boundary shapes but the same conductor ideal.
We can
have different monomial ideals M ̸= M ′ such that their adjoints agree. Equivalently, in the plane
curve singularity language, we can have two different Newton nondegenerate curve singularities (with
different Newton boundaries and different embedded topological types) but with agreeing conductor
ideals (more precisely, their pullbacks in OC2,0 along the natural projection maps OC2,0 →OC,0 agree).
Indeed, this is just the case if the set (Z>0)2\P of positive coordinate lattice points lying strictly above
the −1-translate of the Newton boundary agrees for the two singularities, i.e., if the corresponding P
sets, defined in paragraph 9.2.5, agree.

10. LATTICE HOMOLOGICAL DIMENSION AND MONOMIAL IDEALS
Given a Noetherian algebra O over a field k and a finitely generated O-module M, for any realizable
submodule N ≤M we defined its (lattice) homological dimension in (4.9.2) as
homdim(N ,→M) :=



max{q : Hq(N ,→M) ̸= 0}
if N ̸= M;
−1
if N = M.
By construction, the cardinality of any realization of N gives a strict upper bound, more precisely
homdim(N ,→M) ≤min{|D| : D a realization of N}−1
(cf. Proposition 4.9.4).
In this subsection we present some conditions under which the above inequality becomes an identity.
More concretely, we assign to certain combinations of ring and module elements non-vanishing maxi-
mal dimensional homological cycles. In the case when M = O = k[x1,x2] and N = M is an integrally
closed monomial ideal we can produce such ring elements — in fact, monomials — using the geometry
of the corresponding Newton polygon. Thus, in this case we can understand the homological dimension
by means of convex geometry. By the results of the previous section (see especially Corollary 9.5.11)
this also yields a Newton diagrammatic characterization of the analytic lattice homological dimension
of plane curve singularities with Newton nondegenerate principal part.
10.1. A condition for the non-vanishing of the top homology.
In this subsection we present how the existence of a certain combination of ring and module elements
can bound both the cardinality of any realization and the homological dimension. We will use the
following general setting: let k be any field, O a Noetherian k-algebra, M a finitely generated O-module
and N ≤M a realizable submodule in the sense of Definition 4.4.1.
Proposition 10.1.1. Fix a nonnegative integer r ∈Z≥0. Suppose that there exist certain ring elements
f1, f2,..., fr ∈O \AnnO(M/N) and module elements m1,m2,...,mr ∈M \N satisfying the following:
• fimi /∈N, for all i ∈{1,...,r}, while
• fim j ∈N, for all i, j ∈{1,...,r}, i ̸= j.
Then N cannot be realized by less than r extended valuations. Moreover, if there exists a CDP realiza-
tion of cardinality r, then, by Proposition 4.9.4, we get that
homdim(N ,→M) = r −1 = min{|D| : D a realization of N}−1.
More precisely, we prove that Hred,r−1(N ,→M) ̸= 0 in the case when r ≥1.
For the sake of completeness and simpler referencing we also present the symmetric version of the
previous statement (for notations see subsection 5.3):
Corollary 10.1.2. Let I ◁O be a finite codimensional integrally closed ideal and suppose that there
exist f1, f2,..., fr,g1,g2,...,gr ∈O \I ring elements (for some r ∈Z≥0) satisfying the following:
• figi /∈I , for all i ∈{1,...,r}, while
• figj ∈I , for all i, j ∈{1,...,r}, i ̸= j.


## Page 93


93
Then I cannot be realized by less than r discrete valuations. Moreover, if there exists a CDP realiza-
tion of cardinality r, then, by Theorem 5.3.4 (c), we get that
homdim(I ◁O) = r −1 = min{|D| : (D,d) a realization of I }−1.
Example 10.1.3. In the setting of Example 6.5.8 (i.e. O = k[x] × k[y] and I = (x3) × (y2)) such
combination of ring elements can be chosen, e.g., as f1 = g1 := (1,0) and f2 = g2 := (0,1). Also, I
can be realized by 2 valuations and homdim(I ◁O) = 1.

f1 := x, f2 := y, g1 := x2, g2 := y2,

In order to use Corollary 10.1.2 (or Proposition 10.1.1) to its full strength, we have to understand
what the minimal cardinality r of a realizing collection of (extended) discrete valuations is and then find
ring (and module) elements satisfying the required conditions. In the case of realizable monomial ideals
in k[x1,x2] we can fulfill this plan using the tool of Newton polytopes and their geometric realizations.
10.2. Geometric realizations of monomial ideals in k[x1,x2].
Let us consider a finite codimensional integrally closed monomial ideal M = M in the two variable
polynomial ring O2 = k[x1,x2]. By our previous results, its symmetric lattice homology SH∗(M ◁O2) is
well-defined and can be computed using any CDP realization of M . [In fact, since the lattice homology
only depends on the quotient Artin algebra O2/M , this whole argument remains true in k[[x1,x2]] or
k[x1,x2](x1,x2) — or C{x1,x2} in the complex analytic case — as well.] A particularly nice case to
handle is when we choose a CDP realization consisting of only monomial valuations, since then all
the computations become combinatorial in terms of the lattice points under the Newton polytope (see

subsection 9.2 for the case of the monomial ideal adj(M )).

10.3. Lattice homological dimension of monomial ideals in k[x1,x2].
Recall that in subsection 4.9 we defined the homological dimension of realizable submodules, which
in the setting of a proper finite codimensional integrally closed monomial ideal M ◁O2 = k[x1,x2], with
M ̸= O2, reads as homdim(M ◁O2) = max{q : SHq(M ◁O2) ̸= 0}. Moreover, by Proposition 4.9.4
and Observation 10.2.4 we already have the inequality
homdim(M ◁O2) ≤min{|D| : (D,dD) a realization of M }−1
≤min{|L| : L a geometric realization of R}−1.
We claim that, in our current setting, this are, in fact, all equalities. In particular, in this case Conjecture
4.9.5 also holds.
Theorem 10.3.1 (= Theorem 4.9.6). Let M be a finite codimensional integrally closed monomial ideal
in O2 (which could mean k[x1,x2], k[x1,x2](x1,x2) or k[[x1,x2]] depending on the context). Using the
notation R for its support in the exponent lattice (Z≥0)2, we have the following characterization of the
(lattice) homological dimension of M :
homdim(M ◁O2) = min{|D| : (D,dD) a realization of M }−1
= min{|D| : (D,dD) a monomial realization of M }−1
= min{|L| : L a geometric realization of R}−1.
Translated to the language of plane curve singularities with Newton nondegenerate principal part
(via Theorem 9.4.1) this reads as:
Corollary 10.3.2. Let (C,0) = ({ f = 0},0) ⊂(C2,0) be a plane curve singularity with Newton non-
degenerate principal part and convenient Newton boundary NΓ(f). Then we have the following:
homdiman(C,0) :=

if (C,0) is singular.

= min{|D| : (D,d) is a monomial realization of q−1(C )}−1
= min{|L| : L is a geometric realization of
 (NΓ+(f)\NΓ(f))−(1,1)


where q−1(C ) is the pullback of the conductor ideal C of (C,o) (which is monomial — see Theorem
9.3.2) along the natural projection map q : OC2,0 →OC,0.
The proof of Theorem 10.3.1 consists of two parts: a coordinate geometric study of the minimal car-
dinality of a geometric realization of R, and a careful choice of ring elements allowing the application
of Corollary 10.1.2. These two parts are connected by the so-called ‘kerb configurations’.

Proof of Proposition 10.3.6.
Clearly, since every strong kerb configuration is also a weak kerb con-
figuration, we automatically have that wkerbdim(R) ≥skerbdim(R). This implies that it is enough
to prove that linedim(R)−1 ≥wkerbdim(R) and skerbdim(R) ≥linedim(R)−1. In the case when
M = O2 these are satisfied, since all these values are (−1). Therefore, in the sequel we suppose that
M is a proper ideal with linedim(R)−1, wkerbdim(R), and skerbdim(R) being non-negative.
First, let us show that linedim(R)−1 ≥wkerbdim(R).
This is equivalent to the fact, that if there exists a weak kerb configuration {pl}2t
l=0 consisting of (2t +1)
lattice points, then R cannot be geometrically realized by t or less lines (in the sense of Definition
10.2.6), i.e., every geometric realization L = {Lv}r

however, is a consequence of the following fact: in order for L to satisfy the identity (10.2.5), it must
contain some lines {Lvi}t

i=0 satisfying the following series of inequalities:

11. DEFORMATIONS AND SUPERISOLATED SURFACE SINGULARITIES
In this section we analyze the behaviour of the different analytic lattice homology theories with
respect to certain complex analytic singularity deformations. Here we will focus mostly on the curve
and surface cases, and in the surface case we will include in the picture the topological lattice homology
as well.
We will analyze several types of deformations. The introduction of those deformations which are
defined by the property that they preserve the conductor ideal is a novelty in the literature (as far as we
know), and it is imposed by the results of the present manuscript. Our guiding question is: for which
deformations is the lattice homology stable?
A particularly interesting class of singularities with deformations worth analyzing is the family of
superisolated surface singularities and their (canonical) deformations. We start this section with a short
review of their main properties.
11.1. Superisolated singularities. [8, 9, 77, 87]
A hypersurface singularity (X,0) ⊂(C3,0) is called superisolated if the strict transform eX0 of (X,0)
under the blow-up at the origin 0 ∈C3 (or, in other words, the blow-up of the maximal ideal) is smooth.
This condition already implies that (X,0) must have an isolated singularity. Moreover, it turns out, that
if (X,0) is not already smooth then eX0 →X is the minimal resolution eXmin →X of (X,0).
A different characterization is the following. Assume that (X,0) is given as ({f = 0},0), where
f : (C3,0) →(C,0) is a holomorphic map germ. Let us write f as fd + fd+1 + ···, where {fe}e are
its homogeneous components of degree e, with fd ̸≡0. Then (X,0) is superisolated if and only if the
projective curve C := { fd = 0} in P2 is reduced, and its isolated singular points {pi}i are not situated on


## Page 105


105
{ fd+1 = 0}. It turns out that the embedded topological type (hence, in particular, the link LX of (X,0))
is independent of the choice of the higher degree homogeneous parts {fe}e≥d+1 (under the assumption
that { fd+1 = 0}∩Sing(C) = /0). Hence, it basically depends only on C. Via this identification we can
‘embed’ the theory of reduced projective plane curves into the theory of isolated hypersuface surface
singularities. In fact, this connection turns out to bear fruit in the classification of projective plane
curves. For additional connections and relations with lattice (co)homology theory see, e.g., [29, 30].
The family of superisolated germs plays a key role in the theory of normal surface singularities as
well: besides that it creates a bridge between singular projective plane curves and surface singularities,
it is one of the best ‘testing families’ for different conjectures, properties, see e.g., [8, 9, 77, 78, 87] and
references therein.
11.1.1. Some numerical invariants. In the minimal resolution eXmin →X the exceptional curve can
be identified with C. Even more, for any singular point pi of C the embedded topological types of
(C, pi) ⊂(P2, pi) and of (C, pi) ⊂(eXmin, pi) agree. Hence the minimal good resolution eX →X of (X,0)
can be obtained from eXmin by resolving these embedded local plane curve singularities (following the
resolution steps of plane curve singularities).
Let C1 ∪···∪Cr be the irreducible decomposition of the projective curve C, and let di be the degree
of Ci in P2. Clearly ∑i di = d. Then the irreducible curves {Ci}r
i=1, considered in eXmin as exceptional
divisors of the minimal resolution, have the following intersection properties: (Ci,Cj)eXmin = didj for
i ̸= j and (Ci,Ci)eXmin = −di(d −di +1). In particular, (C,C)eXmin = −d. Moreover, in eXmin one also has
ZK = (d −2)C, denoted by ZK,min. Therefore, Z2
K,min = −(d −2)2d. This shows that the numerical
link invariant, computed in any resolution as Z2
K + |V | (where V is the set of irreducible exceptional
divisors of that resolution), equals Z2
K,min +r = −(d −2)2d +r. (For more about this invariant consult
[87, Section 6.3.A] and the references therein.)
It is also known that the geometric genus pg of a superisolated germ (X,0) is d(d −1)(d −2)/6, i.e.,
it is independent of the concrete form of the equation, it depends only on d.
If we denote the dual resolution graph of a good resolution by Γ, and {vC1,...,vCr} are the vertices of
Γ which corresponds to the strict transforms of the irreducible exceptional curves {Ci}r
i=1 of eXmin, then
Γ \ {vC1,...,vCr} is a collection of connected rational graphs (they are exactly the minimal embedded
resolution graphs of the plane curve singularities {(C, pi) ⊂(eXmin, pi)}i). Hence, {vCi}r
i=1 is a WR-set,
it satisfies (8.4.7) and (8.4.2). In particular, the corresponding Reduction Theorem 8.4.1 for the analytic
lattice homology holds.
Example 11.1.2. (a) IfC is smooth then (X,0) belongs to a special family of superisolated singularities.
In this case the minimal resolution eXmin is already good, and the embedded topological type is inde-
pendent of the choices of { fe}e≥d+1, and even of the choice of fd (under the assumption that C remains
smooth). Hence this embedded topological type coincides with the type of ({xd
1 + xd
2 + xd
3 = 0},0).
In the minimal resolution the exceptional curve is irreducible of genus (d −1)(d −2)/2 and it has
self-intersection −d. Also, Z2
K +|V | = −(d −2)2d +1 (and pg = d(d −1)(d −2)/6).
(b) An important family of superisolated singularities consists of those germs for which C is irre-
ducible and all of its singular points are cusps (i.e., locally irreducible). If C is additionally rational,
then the link LX of (X,0) is a rational homology sphere (its resolution graph is a tree with all genus dec-
orations being 0). In fact, it has the following surgery description: LX = S3
−d(#iKi), where {Ki ⊂S3}i
are the links of the local embedded topological types at pi ∈C.


## Page 106
