11.2. Deformations of superisolated singularities.
Superisolated surface singularities come with two natural deformations, in both cases the deforma-
tion happens at the homogeneous level of fd. We recall these constructions below.
11.2.1. The ‘naive’ deformation. Assume that f defines a superisolated singularity withC not smooth.
Let { fd,t}t∈(C,0) be a deformation of fd such that fd,t is homogeneous of degree d for every t ∈(C,0),
fd,t=0 = fd, and the projective curve { fd,t̸=0 = 0} is smooth. (E.g., fd,t is a small generic homogeneous
deformation of fd.) Then set the deformation ft = fd,t + fd+1+··· of f with t ∈(C,0). By the previous
arguments, for t ̸= 0 the map germ ft : (C3,0) →(C,0) defines an isolated hypersurface singularity
whose embedded topological type agrees with that of ({xd
1 +xd
2 +xd
3 = 0},0).
Along this deformation the embedded topological types, the links and the Milnor numbers are not
stable, however, pg = d(d −1)(d −2)/6 and Z2
K,min = −d(d −2)2 remain constant. Note also, that
unless d ≤2, the link of (Xt̸=0,0) is not a rational homology sphere. (As we already saw in Example
11.1.2 (a), in the minimal resolution the exceptional curve is irreducible of genus (d −1)(d −2)/2 and
self-intersection −d.)
11.2.2. The ‘non-naive’ deformation. Assume that f(x1,x2,x3) = 0 defines a superisolated surface
singularity with the coordinates (x1,x2,x3) chosen in such a way that x3 = 0 intersects the projective
curve C transversely along smooth points of C and fd+1(0,0,1) ̸= 0. Then consider the deformation
ft(x1,x2,x3) := fd(x1,x2,tx3)+∑e≥d+1 fe(x1,x2,x3).
Then for each t with |t| sufficiently small ft defines a superisolated singularity. The deformation
does not preserve the embedded topological types, the links or the Milnor numbers, however, it is
pg-constant and ZK,min-constant. Moreover, the embedded topological type (hence the link, too) of
(Xt=0,0) coincides with that of ({xd
1 + xd
2 + xd+1
3
= 0},0), whose link is a rational homology sphere.
In particular, if C = { fd = 0} is a rational cuspidal curve then for any t the links are QHS3’s. This
has the advantage that along such deformation we can also analyze the stability of those invariants
which are defined only for singularities with rational homology sphere links (e.g., the topological lattice
homology).
Note that if we start with a superisolated singularity (X,o), then in the ‘naive’ deformation this germ
appears as the central fiber of the deformation, while in the ‘non-naive’ deformation the nearby fiber
has this topological type.
11.3. Certain types of deformations.
In this subsection we introduce several constraints on general singularity deformations, which make
them somehow accessible or interesting, especially from the point of view of lattice homology. A
novelty of this manuscript is the definition of ‘conductor ideal constant deformations’, which come
directly from the previous results (in particular, under these types of deformations the analytic lattice
homologies remain constant). We also compare them with more classical notions, such as pg-constant
or δ-constant deformations. In order to discuss these properly we have to fix the setting and some
notations.
Assume that F : (X ,0) →(C,0) is a deformation of F−1(0) = (X,0), which factors through an
inclusion (X ,0) ,→(Cm ×C,(0,0)) and the projection to the second factor (Cm ×C,(0,0)) →(C,0),
such that each fiber (F−1(t),0) = (Xt,0) ⊂(Cm,0) is reduced with an isolated singularity. Let us denote
by qt : OCm,0 →OXt,0 the natural quotient morphism.


## Page 107


107
In the next discussions we will assume for simplicity that each singularity (Xt,0) is Gorenstein.
Moreover, conforming to sections 7 and 8, we also require the following:
Assumption 11.3.1. We assume that all of our studied complex analytic curve singularities are reduced,
whereas all higher dimensional singularities are isolated and normal.
Definition 11.3.2. We say that
(1) F is a ‘conductor ideal constant deformation’ if the pullback q−1
t (CXt,0) ⊂OCm,0 is independent
of the parameter t, where CXt,0 is the conductor ideal of (Xt,0) (for singularities of dimension ≥2 this
was defined in Definition 8.3.5, for curve singularities in paragraph 7.2.5— see also the adjunction
conductor ideal of Merle and Teissier presented in Theorem 9.3.2 );
(2) (a) (dimC(Xt,0) = 1 case) F is a δ-constant deformation if the delta invariant δ(Xt,0) is inde-
pendent of the parameter t;
(2) (b) (dimC(Xt,0) ≥2 case) F is a pg-constant deformation if the geometric genus pg(Xt,0) is
independent of the parameter t;
(3) (dimC(Xt,0) = 2 case) F is a Z2
K,min-constant deformation if the numerical invariant Z2
K,min of
(Xt,0) (i.e., Z2
K = (ZK,ZK)eXt,min computed in its minimal resolution eXt,min →Xt) is independent of the
parameter t.
The conductor ideal constant deformations are motivated by the previous theorems of this manu-
script, see, e.g., Corollaries 7.4.2 and 8.3.7. In particular, along such deformations the analytic lattice
homology remains constant, moreover, as its Euler characteristic (or the codimension of the conductor
ideal), so does the δ invariant, or, in the higher dimensional case, the geometric genus pg.
On the other hand, the interest in Z2
K,min-constant deformations in our context is motivated by the
following fact. Let F : (X ,0) →(C,0) be the germ of a flat deformation of a normal Gorenstein two-
dimensional singularity (X,0). Then, by a result of Laufer [72, Theorem 5.7], F admits a very weak
simultaneous resolution (possibly after a finite base change) if and only if Z2
K,min is constant along F.
(This can be compared with a theorem of Teissier, which says that a deformation of reduced curve
singularities is equinormalizable if and only if it is δ-constant [119].) Having such a simultaneous
resolution implies that one can find a natural linear map between the lattices associated with the res-
olutions of (Xt̸=0,0) and (Xt=0,0) [119], which might help to define a connecting functor (a natural
bigraded Z[U]-module homomorphism) between the analytic (or topological) lattice homologies of the
central and the generic fiber.
11.4. Conductor ideal constant deformations.
In this subsection we will study, through numerous examples, the main properties of conductor ideal
constant deformations. E.g., we will prove that both the naive and non-naive deformations of superiso-
lated singularities are of this kind and compare this new notion with the other types of deformations
introduced in the previous subsection.
First, though, we formalize the following consequence of our previous theorems (especially Corol-
laries 7.4.2 and 8.3.7):
Theorem 11.4.1. Let F : (X ,0) →(C,0) be a conductor ideal constant deformation of the n-dimen-
sional Gorenstein germs (Xt,0) satisfying Assumption 11.3.1 as above. Then
• if n = 1, then the analytic lattice homology Han,∗(Xt,0) stays constant;
• if n ≥2, then the analytic lattice homology of n-forms H∗((Xt,0);Ωn) stays constant.

Before showing some concrete examples, let us recall also the following corollary, which will be our
main technical tool in this section:
Corollary 11.4.2 (= Corollary 9.4.2). The analytic lattice homology (of n-forms) is constant along an
equisingular deformation of isolated hypersurface singularities with Newton nondegenerate principal
part associated with a fixed Newton boundary NΓ. Moreover, it agrees with the symmetric lattice
homology of the adjoint of the monomial ideal supported on NΓ+ (for notations and terminology see
section 9).

11.5. Further connections between deformations and lattice homology theories.
In this subsection we wish to review some further facts and conjectures regarding the behaviour of
both the analytic and topological lattice (co)homology modules along some surface singularity defor-
mations. We start this discussion by recalling the construction of the natural morphism which connects
the above two lattice homology theories.
Let us fix a normal surface singularity (X,o) whose link LX is a rational homology sphere. In
particular, the topological lattice homology is well defined (cf. Example 2.2), whereas the analytic
lattice homology can also be computed through the original definition of [2] (see subsection 8.2).
Then for any good resolution φ : eX →X and any effective cycle ℓ∈L = H2(eX,Z), from the exact
sequence 0 →OeX(−ℓ) →OeX →Oℓ→0, we obtain that h(ℓ) ≤h0(Oℓ), where h is the analytic Hilbert
function associated with the divisorial valuations of the resolution (cf. (8.1.14) or (8.2.2)). In particular,
wan(ℓ) = h(ℓ)−h1(Oℓ) ≤h0(Oℓ)−h1(Oℓ) = χ(ℓ) = wtop(ℓ) ∀ℓ∈L≥0.
This shows that we automatically have an inclusion of the filtered spaces: Sn,top ⊂Sn,an for any n ∈Z.
In particular, we have a bigraded Z[U]-module morphism
H∗: Htop,∗(X,o) = H∗((R≥0)r,wtop) →H∗((R≥0)r,wan) = Han,∗(X,o).


## Page 112


112
By [87, Example 11.9.29] H∗is an isomorphism if (X,o) is contained in one of the following families
of singularities: rational, Gorenstein elliptic, weighted homogeneous, superisolated associated with a
unicuspidal irreducible rational curve, or splice quotient singularities with almost rational graphs.
If for a certain normal surface singularity (X,o) the morphism H∗is an isomorphism, then the Euler
characterisitics of the two lattice homologies must agree, too. In particular, the geometric genus pg
(which is, a priori, an analytic invariant of (X,o)) must agree with the normalized Seiberg–Witten
invariant of the link LX (associated with the canonical spinc-structure). This provides a topological
description of pg (compare with the Seiberg-Witten Invariant Conjecture from [87]).
On the other hand, H∗is not always an isomorphism, e.g., for a superisolated singularity associated
with a rational multicuspidal curve usually it is not. For example, if (X,o) is the superisolated singu-
larity associated with a degree 5 irreducible rational curve C which has two cusps, each with a single
Puiseux pair of type (3,4) and (2,7), then Htop,1 ̸= 0 while Han,1 = 0, cf. [87, Example 11.9.49].
Corollary 11.5.1. Consider a deformation of Gorenstein normal surface singularities, such that the
link of each (Xt,o) is a rational homology sphere and for each (Xt,o) the morphism H∗is an isomor-
phism. If this deformation is conductor ideal constant, then the topological lattice homology stays
constant as well (even if the link is not constant).
Parallel with the early results in the theory of topological lattice (co)homology, there appeared an
expectation that along a Gorenstein Z2
K,min-constant deformations this invariant remains stable, too.
However, it turned out that this is not the case, counterexamples might appear already for the non-naive
deformation of superisolated germs associated with curves having more cusps. We see this fact also via
Proposition 11.4.6: whenever H∗is not an isomorphism for a certain superisolated germ (X,0), Htop,∗
cannot be stable along its non-naive deformation (indeed, for the central fiber H∗is an isomorphism as
Htop,∗(Xt=0,0) ∼= Htop,∗({xd
1 + xd

= 0}, and, since this is weighted homogeneous,
Htop,∗({xd
1 +xd

3
= 0},0) ∼= Han,∗({xd

(see [87, Conjecture 11.3.39]): along a Gorenstein Z2

(with QHS3 link) Htop,0 is constant, too.
In [87, Conjecture 11.9.48] there is formulated another conjecture: Han,∗is constant along a pg-
constant deformation of normal surface singularities with QHS3 links.
Recall that in this work we extended the analytic lattice homology for any normal surface singularity,
without any restriction about their links. Hence we might wonder about the extended version of this last
conjecture: is Han,∗constant along any pg-constant deformation? The answer is negative, as Example
11.4.7 (e) shows, there are Gorenstein deformations with both Z2

12. THE PROOF OF THE INDEPENDENCE THEOREM AND A MORE GENERAL VERSION
In this section we present the proof of the main technical result of this paper: the Independence
Theorem. The argument will involve the split weight function techniques pioneered by T. Ágoston and
the first author, relying heavily on Combinatorial Duality Property and matroid rank inequality of the
height functions. Our key combinatorial result is the Path Ordering Lemma, which replaces the case-
by-case analysis used in the original proofs of well-definedness of analytic lattice homology theories
and reduction-type theorems. To deal with the final homotopy theoretical question in our cubical setting
we use Quillen’s Fiber Theorem, or alternatively the technique of quasifibrations by Dold and Thom.
We will also present a more general formulation of the Independence Theorem and give an application
for the equivariant analytic lattice homology of normal surface singularities.

12.3. The Path Ordering Lemma.
We will state and prove this lemma slightly more generally than needed for the Adjoining Theorem.
First we need to introduce some convenient notations.
Fix some lattice Zr with basis {ev}v∈V and weight function w : Zr →Z on it. Let ℓand ℓ′ be two
lattice points ℓ,ℓ′ ∈Zr, with ℓ′ ≥ℓ. Following part (iii) of paragraph 2.1.7 we call a finite sequence
γ = {xi}t
i=0 of lattice points an ‘increasing path’ from ℓto ℓ′, if x0 = ℓ, xt = ℓ′ and xi+1 = xi + ev(i)
for some index v(i) ∈V for all 0 ≤i < t. Denote by s+(γ) := ∑t−1
i=0 max{0,w(xi+1) −w(xi)} and
s−(γ) := ∑t−1
i=0 min{0,w(xi+1)−w(xi)}. Obviously w(ℓ′)−w(ℓ) = s+(γ)+s−(γ).
For ease of reference, let us call an edge (x,{ev}) (also denoted, for simplicity, as (x,x + ev)) from
x to x+ev ‘positive, ‘neutral’ or ‘negative’ (and mark them with +,0 or −accordingly) depending on
the sign of the difference (w(x+ev)−w(x)).
Lemma 12.3.1 (Path Ordering Lemma). Suppose that the weight function w satisfies the matroid rank
inequality, i.e.,
w(x+ ¯x+ev)−w(x+ ¯x) ≤w(x+ev)−w(x)
∀x ∈Zr, ¯x ∈(Z≥0)r,v ∈V ,ev /∈supp(¯x).
Then, for any two lattice points ℓ,ℓ′ ∈Zr, with ℓ′ ≥ℓbeing a vertex of the r-cube (ℓ,V ), there exists
an increasing path eγ from ℓto ℓ′, such that in eγ all the positive edges precede the neutral and negative
ones, and all the negative ones follow the positive and neutral ones (i.e., the order of the edges is:
positive, neutral, negative), with s+(eγ) maximal and s−(eγ) minimal (i.e., for any other increasing path
γ from ℓto ℓ′ we have s+(eγ) ≥s+(γ) and s−(eγ) ≤s−(γ)).
Proof. It is enough to prove the following: given an increasing path γ from ℓto ℓ′, we can construct
another one, γord, having the prescribed edge order and also s+(γord) ≥s+(γ) and s−(γord) ≤s−(γ).
The main observation following from the matroid rank inequality for w is that
if the edge(x,x+ev) is positive,

The opposite statement is true for the negative edges as well.

We can also see from (12.3.2) that through these three types of changes, the s+ function cannot
decrease, and s−cannot increase.
Due to the finiteness of the path, the following easy algorithm gives us an increasing path that
satisfies the prescribed edge order conditions: first, move all the existing positive edges to the front
(through simple switches of type (1) and (2)), then move every negative edge to the back (through
simple switches of type (1) and (3)), then, move the newly created positive edges in front of the neutrals,
then move the newly created negative edges behind the neutrals etc. repeat this process until you get to
the right order.
□
Remark 12.3.3. The statement of the lemma remains valid if the matroid rank inequality is only sat-
isfied in a (finite) rectangle and we only use lattice points contained in that. On the other hand, the
conclusion is no longer true if ℓ′ is not a vertex of the cube (ℓ,V ), e.g., if ℓ′ = ℓ+2ev there exists only
a single increasing path, hence no room for switches.
Example 12.3.4. Let us consider the combinatorial setup and notations of paragraph 3.1.1. Suppose
that both h and h◦satisfy the matroid rank inequality and the pair (h,h◦) satisfies the Combinatorial
Duality Property. Then the weight function w = h + h◦−h◦(0) satisfies the matroid rank inequality,
and, hence, the Path Ordering Lemma holds true for it. In fact, besides the CDP for the pair (h,h◦),
it is enough to require the matroid rank inequality for h. Indeed, that already prescribes the shape of
switches (1) and (2), while in the case of switch (3), although the first new edge can become negative,
it will not ruin the order and s+(γ

in the wrong direction.

Notice, however, that h+ and h◦+ also have these properties: they are Hilbert functions of certain
valuative multifiltrations, so they satisfy the matroid rank inequality, thus we only have to prove the
CDP. This will follow from the subsequent remark.

We firmly believe that using more advanced techniques (e.g., homotopy colimits such as in [93])
one could construct the homotopy inverses {sn}n∈Z such that they would genuinely commute with
the inclusion maps, i.e., to have set-map equalities in identity (12.4.3) (and also in the last two rows
of Theorem 12.4.1). That would mean, that already the filtered homotopy type of ... ⊂Sn−1 ⊂Sn ⊂
Sn+1 ⊂... ⊂Rn associated with a realizable submodule N could be considered a well-defined invariant.
Similarly, we also believe in the well-definedness of the lattice chain homotopy type (see again [93]).
This would also help in the case of direct products of integrally reduced Artin k-algebras and Künneth-
type relations, cf. subsection 6.5, especially Remark 6.5.4. However, this investigation will take place
in a subsequent work.
12.5. A more general case of the Independence Theorem.
In this section we present a slightly more general setting, where our argument for the Independence
Theorem works.
Let k be a field, M1,M−1 and M0 vector spaces with a k-bilinear ‘multiplication’ operation
• : M1 ×M−1 →M0, (m1,m−1) 7→m1 •m−1.
[In Section 4 these vector spaces are M1 = O, M−1 = M0 = M and the ring action on the module gives
the • multiplication.]
Let N0 ≤M0 be a subspace.
Definition 12.5.1. A ‘compatible filtration’ on the tuple (M1,M−1,N0 ≤M0) is a triple, denoted by
(F M1,F M−1,F M0), of nonconstant decreasing subspace filtrations (i.e., if ℓ≤ℓ′ in Z, then the corres-
ponding subspaces F Mε(ℓ) ≥F Mε(ℓ′) for all ε ∈{±1,0}) such that

Remark 12.5.9. The equivalence relation from the above Definition 12.5.8 is rather cumbersome.
This is due to the fact that in the absence of some stronger algebraic structure, we have to make sure
artificially that the isomorphisms on the quotient level can be lifted to linear maps on the dividend
vector spaces. Nevertheless, if one is satisfied with a weight function and Sn-spaces defined only on a
finite rectangle, then one can revert to a simpler equivalence relation (cf. Remark 14.3.16) using the
quasi-valuations from section 14 instead of the unbounded valuative functions from Remark 12.5.2.

Example 12.5.15. (a) We get back the setting of Section 4 if we choose M1 := O, M−1 = M0 = M
and N−1 = N0 = N, then the ring action on the module will give the • multiplication and the extended

braically closed), M is a finitely generated O-module and G is a finite Abelian group acting on both

Remark 12.5.16. Here a warning is in order: the Nonpositivity Theorem 4.9.9 does not generalize (at

12.6. The equivariant analytic lattice homology of surface singularities.
T. Ágoston and the first author defined the equivariant analytic lattice (co)homology of surface sin-
gularities in [3]. It is an invariant of singularities with rational homology sphere link and categorifies
the equivariant geometric genus. It was a priori associated with a resolution φ : eX →X and later proved
to be independent of it. In this subsection we give an equivalent description of it through our more gen-
eral construction from subsection 12.5. Our definition has the advantage that by Theorem 12.5.14 it is
immediately well-defined (i.e., it is independent of the choice of φ) and comes with a natural Reduction
Theorem.
However, contrary to the case of section 8, here we cannot get rid of the rational homology sphere
assumption on the link, due to the fact that the universal abelian covering (needed for the construction
itself) is well-defined only in this case.
Throughout this section let us fix a normal surface singularity (X,o) with rational homology sphere
link LX. Before giving the construction we have to introduce several notions and notations. We follow
[3, Section 5] and [87, Sections 6.2 and 6.8].
12.6.1. The combinatorics of a resolution [83, 84].


## Page 126


126
Let φ : eX →X be a good resolution of (X,o) with exceptional curve E := φ −1(0), and let ∪v∈V Ev
be the irreducible decomposition of E. Let Γ be the dual resolution graph of φ.
The lattice L := H2(eX,Z) ∼= H2(E,Z) is freely generated by the fundamental homology classes of
{Ev}v∈V (denoted in the very same way) and is endowed with the natural negative definite intersection
form (, ). The dual lattice is L′ = HomZ(L,Z) ≃{l′ ∈L ⊗Q : (l′,L) ∈Z}. It is generated by the
(anti)dual classes {E∗
v }v∈V defined by the identity (E∗
v ,Ew) = −δvw (where δvw stays for the Kronecker
symbol). L′ can also be identified with H2(eX,Z) ∼= H2(eX,∂eX) via the Universal Coefficient Formula
(since H1(eX,Z) ∼= H1(E,Z) is free) and the Lefschetz duality.
Then the intersection form embeds L into L′ with
L′/L ∼= Tors(H1(LX,Z)) = ker



(since LX ≃∂eX),
which will be abridged by H. The class of l′ in H is denoted by [l′].
There is a natural partial ordering of L′ and L: we write l′
1 ≥l′

2 = ∑v rvEv with every rv ≥0.
We set L≥0 = {l ∈L : l ≥0} and L>0 = L≥0 \ {0}. The support of a cycle l = ∑nvEv is defined as
supp(l) = ∪nv̸=0Ev.
Since LX is a rational homology sphere, each Ev is rational and the dual graph of any good resolution
is a tree. In this case H1(LX,Z) = H is finite and we denote by bH the Pontrjagin dual Hom(H,S1) of
the abelian group H. Let θ : H →bH be the isomorphism [l′] 7→e2πi(l′,·) of H with bH.
12.6.2. Natural line bundles.
As previously noted, L′ = HomZ(L,Z) is isomorphic to H2(eX,Z), which is the target of the first
Chern class c1 : Pic(eX) →H2(eX,Z). This morphism appears in the following exact sequence (induced
by the exponential exact sequence of sheaves):
(12.6.3)
0 →H1(eX,OeX) −→Pic(eX)
c1
−→H2(eX,Z) →0.
In this sequence c1 admits a natural group section sL over the integral cycles L ⊂L′. Namely, for
any l ∈L one takes OeX(l) ∈Pic(eX) with c1(O(l)) = l. By [84] (see also [87, Section 6.2]) this sL
can be extended in a unique way to a natural group section s : L′ →Pic(eX). Its existence is basically
guaranteed by the facts that H = L′/L is finite, while Pic0(eX) := H1(eX,OeX) is torsion free. The line
bundles s(l′), indexed by l′ ∈L′, and denoted also by OeX(l′) := s(l′), are called natural line bundles.
In fact, L ∈Pic(eX) is natural if and only if some power of it has the form OeX(l) for an integral cycle
l ∈L.
12.6.4. The universal abelian covering. Let c : (Xa,o) →(X,o) denote the universal abelian covering
of (X,o): (Xa,o) is the unique normal singular germ such that Xa\{o} is the regular covering of X \{o}
associated with the kernel of the Hurewicz map π1(X \{o}) →H (cf. [115]).
Since eX \E is isomorphic to X \{o}, the map π1(eX \E) = π1(X \{o}) →H defines a regular Galois
covering of eX \E as well. This has a unique extension ec : Z →eX with Z normal and ec finite. (In other
words, ec : Z →eX is the normalized pullback of c via φ.) The (reduced) branch locus of ec is included
in E, and the Galois action of H extends to Z as well. Since E is a normal crossing divisor, the only
singularities that Z might have are cyclic quotient singularities (which are, in particular, rational). Let
r : eZ →Z be a resolution of these singular points such that (ec◦r)−1(E)red is a normal crossing divisor.

12.6.10. The geometric genus of the universal abelian covering.
Let (Xa,o) →(X,o) be the universal abelian covering of (X,o), and consider the notations of diagram
(12.6.5). By definition, the geometric genus pg(Xa,o) is h1(eZ,OeZ) (cf. (8.1.3)). Since r : eZ →Z is the

13. THE PROOF OF THE NONPOSITIVITY THEOREM
In this section we will state and prove the Nonpositivity Theorem 4.9.9 in a slightly more general
form. We follow the strategy discussed in [67, Section 6] (with the required modifications), where the
analogue of this theorem was presented in the case of the analytic lattice cohomology of reduced curve
singularities.
13.1. The general statement.
We use the notations and notions defined in sections 3 and 4.
Theorem 13.1.1. Let k be an infinite field, O a k-algebra, M an O-module and N ≤M a realizable
submodule. Suppose that there exists a CDP realization D = {v1,...,vr} of N (in the sense of Definition
4.4.1), such that there exists a prime ideal p◁O with p = {f ∈O : vv(f) ≥1} for all v ∈V = {1,...,r}
(usually p is a maximal ideal). Let us denote d := dimk(O/p) < ∞. Then the Sn,D spaces corresponding
to this setup are contractible for n ≥d. Hence, the weight-grading of the reduced lattice homology is
not lower than 2−2d, i.e., Hred,q,−2n(N ,→O M) = 0 for all q ≥0 and n ≥d.
Moreover, if N ̸= M, then Sd−1,D is in fact non-connected.
Proof of the Nonpositivity Theorem 4.9.9 using Theorem 13.1.1. If k is algebraically closed, then it is
automatically infinite. Moreover, for valuations vv satisfying Assumption 4.1.9 with (O,m) local, the
prime ideal { f ∈O : vv(f) ≥1} must be the unique, necessarily finite k-codimensional, maximal ideal
m. In fact, since k = k, we have dimk(O/m) = d = 1. Hence we can apply Theorem 13.1.1 with d = 1
to obtain the desired result.
□
Example 13.1.2. Both Han,∗(X,o) of a normal isolated singularity of dimC ≥2 and Han,∗(C,o) of
a reduced curve singularity satisfy the above assumptions with d = 1, hence, have the nonpositivity
property (cf. Corollaries 8.1.16 and 7.1.5).
Example 13.1.3. d > 1 can happen, for example, if K/k is a finite field extension and O is in fact a
K-algebra. E.g., H∗((t) ,→R C[t]) ∼= T −
0 ⊕T0(2).

Sn,D-spaces are well-defined: by the Independence Theorem 4.3.1 they are independent of the CDP
realization chosen (see also Theorem 12.4.1). Thus it is enough to prove their contractibility for a
convenient modification D of the original / given realization D. For the sake of simpler arguments we
will make the following operations on the collection of extended valuations:

The proof of Theorem 13.1.1 follows the next strategy: we construct a strong deformation retraction
from the (contractible) space R(0,d) (d ≥dD, see subsection 4.7) to its subspace Sn,D ∩R(0,d) for any
n ≥dimk(O/p) = d. This is done inductively via strong deformation retractions from Sn,D ∩R(0,d)
to Sn−1,D ∩R(0,d) for descending values of n, which will, in turn, be the composition of deformations
eliminating lattice points ℓ∈Sn,D ∩R(0,d) of maximal weight wD(ℓ) = n > d in a prescribed order. In
order to describe the process we need to introduce some relevant notions and their properties.
13.2. Generalized local minima.
The question of contractibility, or, even more, of connectedness of the Sn spaces is strongly related
to the local minimum points of the weight function (i.e., those lattice points ℓ∈Zr for which for any
v ∈V and εv ∈{±1} we have w(ℓ) < w(ℓ+ εvev)). Indeed, any such lattice point ℓcorresponds to a
distinct connected component of Sw(ℓ) consisting only of itself. For more relations between the local
minimum points of the weight function and the corresponding lattice homology / graded root consult
[66, Subsection 2.3] or [67, Subsection 3.1]. It turns out that in order to understand the local minima of
the weight function, it is convenient to use a slightly more general concept, namely that of ‘generalized
local minima’. In this subsection we give a detailed discussion of this notion and its main properties.
These form the technical backbone of the proof of Theorem 13.1.1.
Definition 13.2.1. A lattice point p ∈(Z≥0)r satisfying the condition w(p) < w(p−ev) for any v ∈V
is said to be a ‘generalized local minimum point’ of the weight function.
Let us consider the previous setting: let k be an infinite field, O a k-algebra, M an O-module and
N ≤M a realizable submodule, and let w denote the weight function corresponding to a CDP realization
of N. Then we have the following results:

there exists some mp ∈M such that vM(mp) = −p.

13.3. The M-vertices of cubes.
For cubes we use several notations, namely (ℓ,J), or R(ℓ,ℓ+ eJ), where eJ := ∑v∈J ev. In this way
we basically distinguish the vertices ℓand ℓ+ eJ. However, in certain discussions it is convenient to
distinguish some other vertices, e.g., a vertex ℓ+eK (K ⊂J) identified by having the maximal weight
w(ℓ+ eK) = max{w(ℓ′) : ℓ′ is a vertex of (ℓ,J)}. Then we can rename the cube (ℓ,J), viewed from
ℓ+eK in ‘positive and negative’ coordinate directions, see below.


## Page 135


135
Notation 13.3.1. For a lattice point ℓ∈Zr and index sets J+,J−⊂V with J+ ∩J−= /0, let (ℓ,J+,J−)
denote the cube with vertices ℓ+ eK+ −eK−, where K+ and K−run over all subsets of J+ and J−
respectively (i.e., (ℓ−eJ−,J+ ∪J−) or R(ℓ−eJ−,ℓ+eJ+) using the previous notations). In this case the
dimension of the cube is q =
{J+ ∪J−}
.
Definition 13.3.2. The lattice point ℓis called an ‘M-vertex’ of the cube □, if it is a vertex of it, i.e.,
□= (ℓ,J+,J−) for some J+,J−⊂V , and the weight function satisfies the following:
(i) w(ℓ+ev) ≤w(ℓ) for all v ∈J+, and
(ii) w(ℓ−ev) ≤w(ℓ) for all v ∈J−
(i.e., its weight is no less than any of its neighbours’).
A lattice point having maximal weight in a cube is an M-vertex of that cube (hence a cube can have
multiple M-vertices). The lemma below states the converse in the following combinatorial setting:
Lemma 13.3.3. Consider a weight function with form w = h + h◦−h◦(0) as in (3.1.2) on a cube
(ℓ,J+,J−) ⊂Rr, where h,h◦: Zr ∩(ℓ,J+,J−) →Z are an increasing and, respectively, a decreasing
function as in paragraph 3.1.1, both satisfying the matroid rank inequality and the pair (h,h◦) satisfying
the CDP.
Now, if ℓis an M-vertex of (ℓ,J+,J−) then w(ℓ′) ≤w(ℓ) for any vertex ℓ′ of (ℓ,J+,J−).
Proof. (I) Part (i) of Definition 13.3.2 is equivalent with h(ℓ) = h(ℓ+ev) for any v ∈J+. Then induction
on the cardinality |K+|, for K+ ⊂J+, and inequality (3.1.5) imply h(ℓ) = h(ℓ+eJ+).
(II) Similarly, (ii) reads as h◦(ℓ−ev) = h◦(ℓ)−1 for any v ∈J−. Then, induction on the cardinality
|K−|, for K−⊂J−, and inequality (3.1.5) show that h◦(ℓ−eJ−) = h◦(ℓ).
(III) Now for any vertex ℓ′ of (ℓ,J+,J−), we have ℓ−eJ−≤ℓ′ ≤ℓ+eJ+. Firstly, by the increasing
property of h, we have h(ℓ−eJ−) ≤h(ℓ′) ≤h(ℓ+ eJ+) = h(ℓ). Secondly, by the decreasing property
of h◦, we have h◦(ℓ+ eJ+) ≤h◦(ℓ′) ≤h◦(ℓ−eJ−) = h◦(ℓ). Hence, by the definition of w, we have
w(ℓ′) ≤w(ℓ).
□
Notice, that the assumptions of Lemma 13.3.3 are always satisfied when using the construction of
section 4 (cf. Observation 4.1.12), more specifically, in the setting of Theorem 13.1.1 as well.
Remark 13.3.4. Notice, that Lemma 13.3.3 is no longer true if we only require w to satisfy the matroid
rank inequality.
13.4. The deformation theorem.
In this subsection we present the proof of the Deformation Theorem and show how it implies The-
orem 13.1.1. Throughout this subsection we will use exclusively the modified realization D from
Remark 13.1.4 and, hence, (without the danger of confusion) we will denote the corresponding height
and weight functions and cubical complexes simply by h, h◦, w and Sn (n ∈Z). Recall, that due to the
Independence Theorem 12.4.1, these Sn-spaces are homotopy equivalent to those corresponding to any
other CDP realization of the submodule N ≤M.
Theorem 13.4.1 (Deformation Theorem). In the setting of Theorem 13.1.1 for any integer n ∈Z, with
n ≥d := dimk(O/p), and lattice point d ≥dD (cf. subsection 4.7) there exists a strong deformation
retraction
R(0,d) ↘Sn ∩R(0,d).
In particular, R(0,d) and Sn ∩R(0,d) are homotopy equivalent and, thus, both are contractible.

13.5. Nonpositivity Theorem for direct products of integrally reduced local Artin algebras.
In this subsection we will prove the Nonpositivity Theorem 6.5.9 for direct products of integrally
reduced local Artin algebras. We will use the notions and notations of section 6. Let us recall the
statement:
Theorem 13.5.1 (= Theorem 6.5.9). Let k be an algebraically closed field and A an integrally reduced
Artin k-algebra. Then A can be written in a unique way as a direct product of integrally reduced local
Artin k-algebras ∏l

grading of the reduced symmetric lattice homology is not lower than 2−2l, i.e., SHred,q,−2n(A) = 0 for
all q ≥0 and n > l −1. Moreover, if A is nonzero, then so is SHred,l−1,2−2l.

14. QUASI-VALUATIONS AND QUASI-REALIZABILITY
In this section we will present yet another — in some sense more general — approach to the main
results of this manuscript: the Independence Theorem and the well-definedness of the lattice homology
of realizable submodules. Our starting point is the following deficiency in Definition 4.4.1 of realizabil-
ity: the fact that the submodule N ≤M is realizable does not (necessarily) imply that N/N is realizable
in M/N (see, e.g., the example in Remark 5.1.5), even though the lattice homology H∗(N ,→M) only
depends on this quotient M/N. This weakness follows from the Definition 4.1.1 of discrete valua-
tions, which, by their properties, assign the value ∞to every nilpotent element. Moreover, it is also
unfavorable that, although the output depends only on the quotient M/N, the construction of the lattice
homology module requires the existence of valuations defined on the whole M. In order to remedy these
issues, we introduce the notion of ‘quasi-valuations’, which are, in some sense, quotiented / restricted
discrete valuations, crafted to handle nilpotent and torsion elements as well.
14.1. Quasi-valuations.
Consider the following setting: let k be a field and let O be a Noetherian k-algebra.
Definition 14.1.1. Let us define a commutative monoid structure on the set {0,1,...,d−1,d} ⊂N (with
the induced ordering and d ∈Z>0) as follows:

This operation clearly preserves the ordering. Denote this monoid with Nd.
Definition 14.1.2. We call a function q : O →Nd a ‘discrete quasi-valuation’ if it has the following
properties:


## Page 140


140
• q(0) = d;
• q(fg) = q( f)∗q(g) for every f,g ∈O;
• q(a f) = q( f) for every f ∈O, a ∈k∗= k \{0}.
• q(f +g) ≥min{q(f),q(g)} for every f,g ∈O.
Given a quasi-valuation q on O we can define the ideals

k[x,y,z,t]/(x,y,z,t)3 with d = 13 in the sense of Example 14.1.4 is q. Indeed, such a valuation would have
• v(t(xz−y2)) = v(t)+v(xz−y2) = q(t)+q(xz−y2) = 15;

• v(z(xt −yz)) = v(z)+v(xt −yz) = q(z)+q(xt −yz) = 16;

• 15 = v(t(xz−y2)) = v(z(xt −yz)+y(z2 −yt)) < min{v(z(xt −yz)),v(y(z2 −yt))} = 16,
which contradicts condition (b) of Definition 4.1.1 of discrete valuations.
14.2. Extended quasi-valuations.
We also define the extensions of quasi-valuations to modules analogously to the case of extended
discrete valuations (cf. Definition 4.1.3). Let O be a Noetherian k algebra and M be a finitely generated
O-module.

Remarkably, all the results of section 4 remain true in the setting of (extended) quasi-valuations. For
the sake of completeness we present them in this new language here.

On the other hand, notice that, even though N is realizable in M, N/N is not (necessarily) so in
M/N (see in particular the example in Remark 5.1.5). This means, that especially in torsion modules
over nilpotent algebras, the class of quasi-realizable submodules might be strictly larger than that of
realizable submodules. It is not clear, however, whether this phenomena can occur in the free and
symmetric case as well, i.e., when M = O = Om = k[x1,...,xm] (or k[x1,...,xm](x1,...,xm),k[[x1,...,xm]],
etc., depending on the context — for the complex analytic case see Corollary 14.4.4).
Question 14.3.10. Are there quasi-realizable ideals in Om which are not integrally closed? (Here we
also consider those ideals, which have infinite k-codimension in Om but can be obtained as FD(dD) for
some finite set D of extended quasi-valuations — not necessarily respecting Assumption 14.3.3.) More
generally, under what conditions do quasi-realizable and integrally closed ideals agree in a Noetherian
k-algebra?
This question naturally holds significance in the study of integrally reduced algebras, i.e., quotients
of Om by integrally closed ideals (cf. section 6). Indeed, an affirmative answer to Question 14.3.10
would imply the following characterization:
a Noetherian k-algebra A is integrally reduced ⇔
⇔the ideal 0◁A is quasi-realizable in the wider sense of Question 14.3.10 ⇔
⇔if a ∈A and q(a) = 0 for all quasi-valuations q : A →Nd, then a = 0.
(14.3.11)

(Once again, compare with the complex analytic case in Proposition 6.1.2 and Corollary 14.4.4).
This would also imply the following intrinsic characterization of the integral reduction of a Noether-
ian k-algebra A:
bA = A/{a ∈A : for all q : A →Nd we have q(a) = 0}.
(Compare with the complex analytic case in Proposition 6.1.9).)

(e) If M = O and N = I ◁O a quasi-realizable ideal, then every weight function wD correspoding to

Remark 14.3.14. One advantage of the quasi-valuative viewpoint is that quasi-realizability is more
robust with respect to module operations: if N ≤M is quasi-realizable, then so is
• φ −1(N) for any O-module homomorphism φ : M′ →M, since the precomposition of a quasi-
valuation with a homomorphism is still a quasi-valuation;
• ψ(N) for any quotient map ψ : M →M/N′ with N′ ≤N, since restrictions of quasi-valuations
with these parameters are quasi-valuations.
So, in this setting N ≤M is quasi-realizable if and only if so is N/N in M/N (which is not true
for the integral closedness and realizability properties, see, e.g., the example in Remark 5.1.5) and
H∗(N ,→M) ∼= H∗(N/N ,→M/N).
Moreover, these quasi-valuations offer a more flexible setting in which to investigate the homological
dimension Conjecture 4.9.5. Indeed, this is due to the fact, that by Propositions 14.1.8 and 14.2.9 all

here a concrete example, providing a single quasi-valuation for the quasi-realization of the submodule

14.4. Analogies with arcs and jets.
Given a field k and a (Noetherian) k-algebra O, (formal) arcs of SpecO are defined as morphisms
SpecK[[t]] →SpecO, where K ⊃k is a field extension (for more see, e.g., [58] — compare also with
paragraph 5.4.8). On the ring level, these correspond to maps α : O →K[[t]], which, post-composed
with the ordt : K[[t]] →N ∪{∞} order function, give rise to valuations vα = ordt ◦α. In fact, the
valuations corresponding to the normalization of components of reduced curve singularities (cf. (7.1.2))
are also of this form with k = K = C.
Remark 14.4.1. Clearly, for an arc α : O →k[[t]], the corresponding Hilbert function hvα, defined
as in (14.3.5), satisfies hvα(ℓ+ 1) −hvα(ℓ) ∈{0,1} for any ℓ∈Z. Indeed, for any two functions
f, f ′ ∈Fvα(ℓ)\Fvα(ℓ+1), i.e., satisfying
α(f) = atℓ+ higher order terms, α(f ′) = a′tℓ+ higher order terms for some a,a′ ∈k \{0},
their linear combination a′ f −a f ′ ∈Fvα(ℓ+1). The reverse implication is an interesting question.
Question 14.4.2. Let v : O →N∪{∞} be a discrete valuation with its corresponding Hilbert function
satisfying hv(ℓ+1)−hv(ℓ) ∈{0,1} for any ℓ∈Z. Under what conditions is there an arc αv : O →k[[t]]
satisfying v = vαv?
This question is also related to the theory of maximally valued fields. Indeed, if O is local, then the
quotient domain O/pv has Krull dimension 1 by [16, Theorem 4.4.6], where pv denotes the so-called
‘core’ { f ∈O : v(f) = ∞}. Therefore the natural extension of v to Frac(O/pv) is automatically 0-
dimensional, hence we can apply Kaplansky’s Embedding Theorem [59, Theorem 6]: if the residue
field k of v is algebraically closed of characteristic 0, then there is an embedding Frac(O/pv) ⊂k((t))
in the field of formal Laurent series (see also [23, section 4] and an ‘embedded’ version in [121]).
14.4.3. In the sense in which discrete valuations correspond to (formal) arcs, the analogues of quasi-
valuations are the m-jets. Indeed, these latter objects are defined as follows: for m ∈N, a k-morphism
SpecK[t]/(tm+1) →SpecO (i.e., a homomorphism O →K[t]/(tm+1)) is called an m-jet. These ring
maps post-composed with the suitable order function ordt : K[t]/(tm+1) →Nm+1 (with ordt(0) = m+1)
give quasi-valuations on O in the sense of Definition 14.1.2.
This connection helps us to reformulate Proposition 6.1.2 (or [73, 2.1 Théorème]) and answer Ques-
tion 14.3.10 affirmatively in the complex analytic case as follows:
Corollary 14.4.4. Let I be an ideal in the convergent power series ring Om = C{x1,...,xm}. Then
I is integrally closed ⇔I is realizable ⇔I is quasi-realizable.
Note however, that not all quasi-valuations come from jets:
Example 14.4.5. There exists no jet j : k[x,y,z,t]/(x,y,z,t)3 →K[τ]/(τ13) such that post-composing
with the order function ordτ : K[τ]/(τ13) →N13 gives the quasi-valuation q from Example 14.1.11.

quasi-realizable ideals be quasi-realized using only quasi-valuations coming from jets?
15. APPENDIX
15.1. Realizable and integrally closed submodules.
We recall here Rees’s original definition of integral dependence over a submodule. First we describe
the setting and fix some notations. Let O be a Noetherian ring and N ≤M an inclusion of finitely
generated O-modules. For any prime ideal p ◁O let us denote the fraction field of O/p by κ(p), the
κ(p)-vector space M ⊗O κ(p) ∼= Mp/pMp by W(p) and the image of N ⊗O κ(p) in W(p) by V(p). We
also denote by ιp the canonical composition M →M/pM →W(p).
Definition 15.1.1. ([107], see also [118, Section 16]) An element m ∈M is said to be integral over N
if for every minimal prime ideal p in O and every discrete valuation ring Ov between O/p and κ(p)
(i.e., the corresponding discrete valuation v satisfies v ≥0 on O/p and v(a) = ∞if and only if a = 0 in
O/p) we have
ιp(m) ∈ιp(N)Ov,
i.e., the image of m in Mp/pMp can be written as ιp(m) = ∑i vini, where vi ∈Ov and ni lies in the
canonical image of N inside W(p) for each i.
The submodule N is called integrally closed in M, if every element m ∈M, which is integral over N,
already lies in N.
We want to translate this definition to our language of extended discrete valuations on the finitely
generated O-module M.
Lemma 15.1.2. Consider a finitely generated module M over the Noetherian ring O and a discrete
valuation v : O →N = N∪{∞} (in the sense of Definition 4.1.1) with W(pv) ̸= 0 (where pv denotes the
core { f ∈O : v(f) = ∞} of the valuation). Then there exists some (in fact, there exist infinitely many)
map vM : M →Z = Z∪{∞}, such that the pair (v,vM) is an extended discrete valuation (in the sense
of Definition 4.1.3).
Proof. Any such valuation v : O →N with core pv ◁O descends to a discrete valuation of O/pv which
in turn extends uniquely as vκ to its field of fractions κ(pv). We can now consider the finite dimensional
κ(pv)-vector spaceW(pv) ̸= 0. One can then extend this valuation to this vector space to give it a valued
vector space structure in the sense of [56, Definition 3.3] (this corresponds to our Definition 4.1.3 if we

Proposition 15.1.5 combined with the original Definition 15.1.1 of integral dependence yields the
following:
Corollary 15.1.8. An element m ∈M is integral over N if and only if for every discrete valuation
v : O →N, with its core pv := { f ∈O : v(f) = ∞} being a minimal prime ideal in O, and every
extension vM : M →Z in the sense of Definition 4.1.3, we have vM(m) ≥vM(N).

15.2. Further questions, open problems.
In this subsection we list some (further) open problems, questions and conjectures related with the
different lattice homological constructions presented in the previous sections, which might be guiding
objectives for future research.
15.2.1. Homological dimension.
Let O be a Noetherian k-algebra and let N be a finite codimensional realizable submodule in the
finitely generated O-module M. In this context we can introduce two numerical invariants (see also
subsection 4.9 and section 10): the ‘(lattice) homological dimension’
homdim(N ,→M) :=



max{q : Hq(N ,→M) ̸= 0}
if N ̸= M;
−1
if N = M.
and the ‘realizability cardinality’ realiz(N ,→M) := min{|D| : D is a realization of N} of N. Proposi-
tion 4.9.4 states the inequality homdim(N ,→M) ≤realiz(N ,→M)−1 as a direct consequence of our
construction.
Question 15.2.2. Is this inequality sharp? Are there examples when this is not an equality?
In answering this question we are obstructed by two main difficulties: in general it is not easy to
compute H∗or to establish vanishing bounds for Hq (they are mostly obtained exactly by the previ-
ous inequality, or by direct computations); and also the classification of the corresponding (extended)
valuations is not (always) known.
The simplest case seems to be when homdim(N ,→M) = 0. In Conjecture 4.9.5 we formulated the
expectation that any integrally closed finite codimensional ideal of k[x,y] with homological dimension
zero can be realized by a single valuation. In the body of the manuscript we supported this statement
by several examples, see, e.g., Examples 9.6.1–9.6.2, or the case of the ideal (y4,y3x,y3 +x2) ⊂C[x,y]
from paragraph 5.4.9 (which can be realized as FvE(2,c)(8)).
Moreover, using the language of quasi-valuations (cf. subsection 14), one could also study the
slightly weaker conjecture, whether the vanishing of higher homologies imply quasi-realizability by a
single quasi-valuation. We gave a supporting example for this statement in the setting of the analytic
lattice homology of reduced curve singularities in Example 14.3.15.
Returning to the more general Question 15.2.2, in section 10 we presented sufficient conditions for
the equality homdim(N ,→M) = realiz(N ,→M)−1 and proved that in the case of realizable monomial


## Page 154


154
ideals of k[x,y] these conditions hold. We expect the same to be true in every polynomial ring, for
supporting examples see Remark 10.3.21.
In fact, we formulated the above conjecture only for the algebra k[x,y] since in this case we have a
better picture of the discrete valuations. It can happen that the conjecture holds true in more general
algebras as well.
15.2.3. Constructing minimal realizations.
Let O be a Noetherian k-algebra and let N be a finite codimensional realizable submodule in the
finitely generated O-module M as above. Related to the previous paragraph 15.2.1, we formulate the
following question as well.
Question 15.2.4. Is there a natural construction which provides a minimal set of valuations which
realizes N ,→M ?
In the case when M = O = k[x1,x2] and N = M is a realizable monomial ideal we gave a recipe
to produce such a minimal set of realizing monomial valuations in section 10 (see especially Remark
10.3.20). This depends on the strong connection between the homological dimension and the convex
geometry of the Newton polytope (cf. Theorem 10.3.1). We emphasize, however, that such realizations
are highly non-unique, e.g., our exact constructions in section 10 depend on a choice of a small enough
rational number ε ∈Q>0.
We also note that if I ⊂O is an integrally closed ideal of O, then the very same set of Rees
valuations realizes all the integral closures I n of its powers I n ⊂O. Moreover, the set of Rees
valuations is unique [106]. However, if we wish to realize only I , then it turns out that it might be
realized by fewer valuations than the number of Rees valuations, see, e.g., Example 9.6.2.
15.2.5. Hilbert–Samuel-type regularity.
Again, consider an integrally closed ideal I ◁O of finite codimension, and suppose that all of
its powers I n = I n are also integrally closed (such ideals are called normal in the literature and
are extensively studied.) Then, for every I n we can consider the symmetric lattice homology mod-
ule SH∗(I n ◁O). Since all of them are realized by the same set of Rees valuations [106], we can
run the construction of the homologies in the very same lattice. Also, since the numerical sequence
eu(SH∗(I n ◁O)) = dimk O/I n shows certain regularity (at least for n ≫0) via the Hilbert–Samuel
polynomial of I , we expect some regularity in the sequence of modules SH∗(I n ◁O) as well.
Problem 15.2.6. Understand the behavior of the sequence of modules SH∗(I n◁O) (at least for n ≫0).
15.2.7. Stability along a given multifiltration.
More generally, we can consider the symmetric lattice homologies of all the ideals realized by a
fixed collection of discrete valuations D of a k-algebra O. In Remark 5.3.7 we introduced the notation
I = I (ℓ) := FD(ℓ) for any ℓ∈(Z≥0)r. Then the modules SH∗(I (ℓ) ◁O) = SH∗(O,D♮,2ℓ) give
a categorification of the Hilbert function hD associated with D, where D♮is the doubling of D from
Lemma 5.3.2.
Problem 15.2.8. Understand the behavior of the modules ℓ7→SH∗(I (ℓ)◁O) (at least for ℓ≫0).
[Notice that, using these notations, in the previous paragraph 15.2.5 we were asking a question about
the ideals I (n·dI ), where I (dI ) = I .]


## Page 155


155
For example, we claim that if O = OC2,0 and the valuations of D correspond to normalizations of
embedded irreducible curve singularities {(Cv,0)}r
v=1 (cf. (7.1.2)), then the modules SH∗(I (ℓ) ◁O)
for ℓ≥2c(∪vCv) can be rather explicitly described, where c(∪vCv) is the conductor element. [A similar
situation was discussed in the last part of paragraph 5.4.9 without the assumption on the conductor.]
The precise results in this case will be presented in a forthcoming manuscript.
15.2.9. Connections with the theory of integrally closed ideals and modules over 2-dimensional
regular local algebras
The theory described in the title is rather rich, it was initiated by Zariski [131] (see also [75] for
a modern treatment), who proved the following unique factorization result: the product of any two
integrally closed ideals in a 2-dimensional regular local ring is again integrally closed, moreover any
such non-zero ideal can be expressed uniquely (except for ordering) as a product of simple integrally
closed ideals.
Notice that, using our language, if I1 and I2 are finite codimensional integrally closed ideals in
the 2-dimensional regular local k-algebra O, then they are realizable. Moreover, if we choose such a
collection D of discrete valuations on O which realizes simultaneously I1, I2 and I1I2, then we
also have the fact that FD(dI1 + dI2) = I1I2, where FD(dI1) = I1 and FD(dI2) = I2 (for the
notations see section 7).
Question 15.2.10. Can we connect the symmetric lattice homology of I1 and I2 with that of I1I2?
Does lattice homology see simpleness in this context?
Although in higher dimensions the product of integrally closed ideals might not be so, Zariski’s
results were extended to some higher dimensional cases by Lipman [75] (see also the case of the Goto-
class G ∗of integrally closed ideals in [21]). Kodiyalam and Hayasaka extended the study to integrally
closed modules [62, 49, 50]. We hope that our lattice homology construction can become a useful tool
in further exploration of this area.
15.2.11. Integral reduction and lattice homology.
In section 6 we introduced ‘integrally reduced algebras’, i.e., quotients of polynomial rings with
integrally closed ideals. Although the notion is well defined, an intrinsic characterization (similar to
that of Lejeune-Jalabert and Teissier in [73, 2.1 Théorème] in the analytic case — see our formulation
in Proposition 6.1.2) is missing (see especially Question 14.3.10 heading into this direction and its
possible consequence (14.3.11)).
We also introduced the ‘integral reduction’ operation, which produces from any source algebra an
integrally reduced one. The connections between the input and the outcome, though, need further study.
Question 15.2.12. Which (algebraic) invariants and properties remain stable under such an integral
reduction operation? Does it have any geometric significance?
In the case of integrally reduced Artin algebras, we can consider their well-defined lattice homology
modules, which categorify their k-dimension and depend mysteriously on their multiplicative structure.
Problem 15.2.13. Connect the lattice homology with other algebraic invariants of integrally reduced
Artin algebras.
15.2.14. Quasi-valuations, arcs and jets


## Page 156


156
In section 14 we introduced the notion of ‘quasi-valuations’, which are generalizations of discrete
valuations configured to better handle nilpotent elements and zero divisors of the base k-algebra. Al-
ready their definition brings up a number of interesting questions (cf. Questions 14.1.5 and 14.2.7)
regarding their relations with discrete valuations and quotient operations. We also claimed that the
Independence Theorem remains valid in their context, which raised the question whether this general-
ization really extends our playing field, i.e.,
Question 15.2.15. Are all Artin algebras, arising as quotients with quasi-realizable ideals, integrally
reduced?
In subsection 14.4 we also presented some analogies between discrete valuations and formal arcs,
respectively between quasi-valuations and jets (see Questions 14.4.2 and 14.4.6 arising naturally in this
context). The search for connections between the symmetric lattice homology modules of integrally
closed ideals of a Noetherian k-algebra and the formal arc– and jet-spaces of the same algebra seems
to be a fascinating project.
In the complex analytic case the authors expect some deep relation between the cohomology of the
(restricted) contact loci associated with an isolated hypersurface singularity f : (Cm,0) →(C,0) and
its analytic lattice homology (of m−1-forms) H∗(({f = 0},0),Ωm−1). This would create an additional
connection with the Floer homology of compactly supported Milnor monodromy (via the results and
arc-Floer conjecture of [18]).
15.2.16. Filtered version.
The analytic lattice homology of reduced curve singularities has a filtered enhancement / version
introduced by the first author in [88], which is a much stronger invariant (e.g., it is a complete embedded
topological invariant for plane curves). In its construction an additional filtration by the norm |.| of the
lattice points is taken into account to produce a rich spectral sequence.
A similar approach could be taken in the case of the lattice homology of realizable submodules of
section 4 by considering the following ‘generalized norm’
n : (Z≥0)r →Z≥0, ℓ7→dim(O/F(ℓ))+dim(F M(−ℓ)/N) = h(ℓ)−h◦(ℓ)+codim(N ,→M)
on the lattice points (compare with (7.2.1) in the curve singularity case). One can prove, that the Inde-
pendence Theorem 4.3.1 can be strengthened to also respect the n-value of the local minimum and local
maximum points of the weight function. On the other hand, the spectral sequence corresponding to this
filtration will not necessarily be invariant. The consequences and applications of this enhancement will
be discussed in a forthcoming paper.
15.2.17. Functoriality and exact sequences.
The theory of different lattice homology constructions in its current form lacks the incorporation of
functors / morphisms. In the case of the lattice homology modules associated with complex analytic
singularities a lot of effort is put into assigning nontrivial graded Z[U]-module morphisms to certain
singularity deformations. The best result so far concerns the analytic lattice homology of reduced curve
singularities: [5, Theorem 6.4.1] claims that a flat deformation {(Ct,o)}t∈(C,0) induces a degree zero
graded Z[U]-module morphism Han,0(Ct̸=0,o) →Han,0(Ct=0,o) on the homological degree 0 part. In
fact, Ágoston and the first author constructed compatible maps F0,n : π0(Sn,t̸=0) →π0(Sn,t=0) between
the connected components of the Sn spaces for every level n ∈Z. The ‘Functoriality Conjecture’
claims that these {F0,n}n maps can be extended to the Sn-spaces up to homotopy, such that these would


## Page 157


157
induce a degree zero graded Z[U]-module morphism Han,∗(Ct̸=0,o) →Han,∗(Ct=0,o) on the whole
lattice homology modules.
Problem 15.2.18. Find nontrivial graded Z[U]-module morphisms between the lattice homologies of
certain realizable submodules. What information can they encode? Can we produce exact sequences?
One advantage of the theory of lattice homology of realizable submodules in this regard is that
we can use different realizations inducing different lattices and weight functions to compute the same
lattice homology module. In this way, if we want to compare, for example, the Sn-space filtrations
of two realizable submodules of the same ambient module, then we can use the same collection of
extended valuations realizing both. This might be especially helpful, since we do not really understand
yet the combinatorial requirements on the weight / height functions to obtain nontrivial maps (e.g., not
tautological inclusions) between different Sn-space filtrations, let alone exact sequences on the level of
the lattice homology modules.
15.2.19. Stability under singularity deformations
Already at the introduction of the topological lattice homology associated with complex analytic
singularities there arose the question of which deformations preserve this new invariant. The addition
of new analytic variants just added more layers and complexity to this question. In this manuscript we
introduced the notion of conductor ideal constant deformations of Gorenstein singularities under which
the analytic lattice homology (of top forms) is tautologically stable. We initiated their study in subsec-
tion 11.4 through numerous examples comparing them with δ–, pg– and Z2
K,min-constant deformations,
though, much is yet to be uncovered.
In subsection 11.5 we also gave a short review of the main conjectures and open problems relating
the analytic and topological lattice homologies with singularity deformations. Of particular interest
is [87, Conjecture 11.9.48], which claims that along flat pg-constant deformations of normal surface
singularities (with QHS3 links) the analytic lattice homology is stable. Notice that if we omit the
topological condition on the link (and use the more general analytic lattice homology of 2-forms),
then this statement becomes false: see the pg– (and Z2
K,min–), but not H∗(.,Ω2)-constant deformation
in Example 11.4.7 (e) with the generic fiber having non-QHS3 link. Nevertheless, [87, Conjecture
11.9.48] in its original form is still open.
15.2.20. Characterization of H≥1 and mw = minw0 in the case of complex analytic singularities.
In general, the presence and ranks of higher homological degree summands and the numerical in-
variant mw = minw0 are rather mysterious.
Problem 15.2.21. Give a characterization of the graded Z[U]-modules {Hq(X,o)}q≥1 and of minw0 (in
the case of the analytic lattice homology theories) associated with isolated (hypersurface) singularities
of dimension 1 and 2 in terms of their classical singularity invariants.
Recall that in Proposition 10.1.1, in the general algebraic case, we assign top dimensional homolog-
ical cycles to some combinations of ring and module elements. Even though this result can be further
strengthened for plane curve singularities with Newton non-degenerate principal part and convenient
Newton boundary (cf. Corollary 10.3.2), in general, for complex analytic singularities the geometric
interpretations of {Hq(X,o)}q≥1 with classical invariants are still painfully missing.


## Page 158


158
On the other hand, the invariant mw = minw0 of reduced curve singularities was already successfully
linked with their Cohen Macaulay type by A. Hof and the first author in [53]. The higher dimensional
case, however, is yet to be studied.
